# Avra Language Design Questionnaire

> A first-principles design document for Avra — a compiled, statically-typed, agent-first programming language designed for a future where humans don't write code, automated services are the norm, and the language itself is the substrate of machine-to-machine communication.

---

## How to Use This Document

This questionnaire is organized into sixteen top-level axes, each with sub-decisions, tradeoffs, and edge cases. Every question has three fields:

- **Options** — the realistic design choices, including radical ones
- **Decision** — `[PENDING]` until answered, then the committed choice
- **Rationale** — why, with examples where helpful

We iterate through them together. Answers in earlier axes often determine answers in later ones — we'll flag dependencies as we go.

The format is deliberately repetitive so an LLM or a human returning months later can orient instantly. The document is the living spec.

---

## Part 0: Foundational Principles

Before any specific decision, these principles frame everything that follows. They are not axes to decide — they are the constraints every axis must respect.

### P1. Designed for a post-human-coder future

Humans will not primarily write Avra code. LLMs will. Every design decision is evaluated first on: *can an LLM generate correct code in this language on the first try, without iteration?* Accessibility to LLMs is the primary success metric.

### P2. Substrate for automated services

Avra is the coordination layer for autonomous services talking to other autonomous services. The language is designed for a world where businesses are mostly software, software is mostly generated, and services negotiate contracts with each other at runtime.

### P3. Gloriously declarative, zero ceremony

Every unnecessary character is a design failure. The language should read like intent, not instructions. Boilerplate is an anti-feature — if the programmer has to write the same thing twice, the compiler should do it.

### P4. Rust-level performance or better

Compiles to native binaries via LLVM. No runtime, no GC pauses, no interpreter. Performance isn't a tradeoff — it's a requirement. The language that becomes the substrate cannot be slow.

### P5. Full stack vertical integration

Mobile app to UI to HTTP server to queue worker to database to memory registers to GPIO pins. One language spans the entire stack. The AI can build anything without leaving the platform.

### P6. Paradox collapse

Binary choices are usually false dichotomies. High-level vs low-level — Avra is both, with scoped abstraction levels. Safe vs fast — both, at different levels. Structural vs nominal — both, for different things. Magic vs explicit — both, with visible magic and escape hatches.

### P7. Visible magic

The compiler does enormous work on your behalf: wires services together, generates infrastructure, manages memory, auto-instruments. But the magic is inspectable — you can always ask the compiler "what did you generate?" and see the result. Magic you can't see is indistinguishable from bugs.

### P8. Escape hatches everywhere

Every magic has a manual override. Automatic memory management, but you can drop to systems level. Automatic deploy, but you can eject to raw YAML. Automatic serialization, but you can override per type. Never trapped.

### P9. Boundaries are contracts

Module exports, function signatures, provider manifests, deploy blocks — every boundary is a typed contract the compiler enforces. Inside boundaries, inference and magic. At boundaries, explicit and checked.

### P10. Compiler has semantic knowledge no other tool has

The compiler understands intent (declarations), structure (organization), topology (service connections), and failure modes (error propagation). This knowledge compounds — it generates infrastructure, documentation, tests, and agent tool definitions from one source of truth.

### P11. Machine-readability is the substrate, human-readability is the projection

Every error is JSON. Every type is introspectable. Every module exposes its structure. The language is designed for machines first, with a human-readable surface layered on top.

### P12. One source of truth, many projections

`model User` is simultaneously a database schema, a TypeScript type for the frontend, an OpenAPI spec, a validation layer, an admin UI, a test fixture, a documentation entry, and an AI tool definition. Write once, project everywhere.

### P13. Collapse dev/ops/infra

What runs (code), where it runs (infra), and how it gets there (CI/CD) are the same artifact. Deploy blocks are compiler contracts. Topology is extracted. Observability is auto-instrumented.

### P14. No runtime, no framework, no container

Single statically-linked binary. The language IS the framework. Instant startup, total reproducibility, edge-deployable.

### P17. Composability over featurefulness

Small primitives that combine beat large frameworks. Channels compose with streams compose with spawn compose with select. Providers compose via manifests. Features earn their keep or are removed.

---

## Cross-Cutting Design Notes

These are not axis decisions — they are design mechanisms that span multiple axes, committed during the questionnaire walkthrough.

### @derive — the universal code generation mechanism

`@derive` is how Avra collapses the "impl boilerplate" problem into a single coherent system. It is a direct consequence of decisions 1.3 (unified lifted code) and 3.4 (nominal traits).

**Mechanism:** `@derive X` is syntactic sugar for invoking a lifted function `derive_X(t: Type)` that generates declarations at compile time. There is no hardcoded list of derives — derives are open and extensible. Anyone can write one.

**Ladder of complexity:**

1. **Trivial auto-impl from fields:** `@derive HasBalance(from: balance)` generates a one-line impl reading the `balance` field.

2. **Default semantics for common traits:** `@derive [Display, Eq, Clone, Hash, Serializable]` generates sensible impls — Display joins fields with commas, Eq compares all fields, Clone clones each, Serializable generates JSON serialization.

3. **Field-level customization:** derives accept config to skip fields, rename, flatten, validate:
   ```
   @derive Serializable {
     skip: [password]
     rename: { user_id: "id" }
     flatten: [address]
   }
   ```

4. **Domain-specific generation:** `@derive Validate { ... }` generates a `validate()` method based on per-field rules.

5. **Multi-artifact generation:** `@derive Model(table: "users")` generates a database table, migrations, query types, update types, OpenAPI schema, admin UI entries — all from one type declaration. This is how P12 (one source of truth, many projections) is realized.

6. **User-authored derives:** since `@derive` is just a lifted function, users write their own:
   ```
   @lifted fn derive_audit_log(t: Type) -> Declarations {
     // Custom generation logic for your domain
   }
   ```

7. **Composable higher-order derives:** derives can combine others:
   ```
   @lifted fn derive_rest_resource(t: Type) -> Declarations {
     [derive_model(t), derive_validate(t), derive_serializable(t), derive_openapi(t)]
   }
   ```

**Key property:** `@derive` is not compiler magic. It's a lifted function. Every derive is a regular Avra function that runs at compile time and returns declarations. This means:

- The compiler has no hardcoded list of derives
- Providers ship their own derives (`@std/model` provides `@derive Model`, `@std/http` provides `@derive OpenApi`)
- Users write custom derives for their domain
- Derives are introspectable, testable, and debuggable like any other code

**This is the mechanism behind most of Avra's "magic."** When you write `model User { ... }` and get a database table, REST endpoints, OpenAPI schema, and admin UI, the magic is `@derive Model` — a lifted function that takes the type and generates all those projections. P7 (visible magic) is satisfied because you can read the derive's source and see exactly what it generates.

---

### Cross-Cutting Syntax Commitments

> Syntax decisions made in passing during axis discussions that will be formally ratified in Axis 28 but must be consistent across all prior examples. Recorded here so the whole document stays coherent.

**Closures use `(params) -> body`.**

A single arrow `->` is used consistently across function signatures and closure bodies. Reasons:
- **One arrow, one meaning:** `->` means "produces" in both contexts. No visual distinction between function return types and closure bodies because they're the same concept — a parameter list produces a value.
- **Visually unambiguous:** `->` doesn't collide with any other operator. Not confusable with boolean OR (`or` keyword), not confusable with comparison (`>=`), not confusable with arrow functions from other languages (`=>`).
- **Block expressions stay separate:** `{ body }` is a block, `(x) -> body` is a closure. No ambiguity.

**Syntax:**
```
// Function declarations (signature)
fn add(x: int, y: int) -> int { x + y }

// Closure expressions
let add = (x, y) -> x + y
let double = (x) -> x * 2
let log = () -> println("running")
let square = x -> x * x                  // single-param parens optional

// Function types
let f: (int, int) -> int = (x, y) -> x + y
//     ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^   ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^
//     type                value
```

Single-parameter parentheses are optional. Multi-parameter and zero-parameter require parentheses.

**This replaces Rust's `||` / `|x|` pipe syntax used in earlier drafts.** Any example using `||` or `|x|` should be read as `()` / `(x) ->`. This will be formally ratified in Axis 28.

---

### Future Extension: Type Operators (Post-v1.0)

**Not in the first version, but designed for.** Type operators are compile-time operations that derive new types from existing ones. Deferred to a later version to keep v1 scope manageable, but documented here so the language design doesn't preclude them.

**Motivation:** every real codebase has "API response type," "partial update type," "create-without-ID type" — all derived from some base model. TypeScript's `Pick`/`Omit`/`Partial` are the industry-standard ad-hoc solution. Avra should have first-class syntax.

**Proposed operators:**

```
type User = { id: UUID, name: string, email: string, password: string, created_at: timestamp }

// Subtract fields
type PublicUser = User without password
// = { id: UUID, name: string, email: string, created_at: timestamp }

// Add fields
type AuditedUser = User with { audit_log: List<AuditEvent> }

// Pick specific fields
type UserSummary = User only { id, name }

// Make all fields optional (for partial updates)
type UserPatch = User optional
// = { id?: UUID, name?: string, email?: string, ... }

// Combine operators
type ApiUser = User without password with { session_id: UUID }

// Works on shapes too
shape HasTimestamps = { created_at: timestamp, updated_at: timestamp }
shape WithoutTimestamps = HasTimestamps without created_at   // just updated_at
```

**Open design questions (to resolve when this ships):**

1. **Nominal vs structural result:** `User without password` — is it nominal (new identity) or structural (just a shape)? Tentative answer: preserves the base's nominality. `type User` operators produce `type`; `shape User` operators produce `shape`.

2. **Trait inheritance:** if `User` implements `Display`, does `PublicUser` automatically? Tentative: yes, if the impl doesn't reference removed fields. The compiler verifies.

3. **Generic type operators:** can you parameterize? `type Partial<T> = T optional` as a reusable operator. Probably yes — this is where it gets really powerful. Unlocks library-level abstractions.

4. **Relationship to `@derive`:** type operators and `@derive` are both compile-time transformations. Type operators produce types; derives produce impl blocks. They compose: `@derive Display type PublicUser = User without password`.

5. **Implementation:** type operators are lifted functions operating on types. `without` is syntactic sugar for a builtin lifted function `type_without(t: Type, field: Symbol) -> Type`. Users can write custom type operators as lifted functions, same model as `@derive`.

**v1 workaround:** users manually write derived types. Verbose but works.

**When to ship:** after v1 proves the core is stable. Probably v1.5 or v2.0. Before Phase 3 providers ship heavy use of derived API types.

---

## Part 1: Foundation

The baseline type system shape. Everything else layers on these choices.

---

### Axis 1: Static vs Dynamic Typing

**Sub-decision 1.1: Typing discipline**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Fully static — every value has a compile-time type
  - (b) Gradual — static where annotated, dynamic elsewhere
  - (c) Dynamic with optional type hints
- **Decision:** **(a) Fully static**
- **Rationale:** Every value has a compile-time type. No `any`, no runtime duck typing, no gradual zones. Two reasons specific to Avra: (1) LLVM native compilation requires concrete types at codegen — gradual typing means carrying type info to runtime, which means a runtime, which violates P14. (2) LLMs generate better code with full static typing because the type-error → fix loop is the tightest feedback mechanism available. When the compiler rejects an LLM's code with a type error, the fix is mechanical; when a dynamic program crashes at runtime, the LLM has to reason about execution traces. Static typing makes LLMs faster, not slower.

**Sub-decision 1.2: What runs at compile time vs runtime**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Types fully erased after compilation (Rust)
  - (b) Types preserved for reflection (Java)
  - (c) Types erased but introspection via compiler API (Avra-native)
- **Decision:** **(c) Erased binary, introspectable compiler**
- **Rationale:** The compiled binary contains zero runtime type information — maximum performance, smallest size, instant startup. But the compiler itself is a queryable service via LSP/MCP. When an LLM or tool needs type info, it asks the compiler (which has the AST), not the runtime (which has just bytes). Every traditional use case for reflection — runtime type discovery, dynamic deserialization, DI frameworks, plugin loading, ORM introspection — is replaced by compile-time code generation via providers and `@derive` annotations. This is strictly better: faster (no metadata lookup), type-safer (compiler verifies at build time), smaller (no metadata in the binary). The one case reflection genuinely helps — interactive inspection — is covered by the LSP/MCP server querying the compiler's source knowledge.

**Sub-decision 1.3: Compile-time evaluation / comptime**

- **Options:**
  - (a) No compile-time evaluation beyond constant folding
  - (b) `const fn` for a restricted subset (Rust)
  - (c) Full comptime — arbitrary code at compile time (Zig)
  - (d) Macros as the compile-time mechanism (Rust, Scheme)
  - (e) **Unified lifted code** — no separate metaprogramming system. Functions marked `@lifted` run at compile time. Providers are modules with lifted exports. One language, two execution times.
- **Decision:** **(e) Unified lifted code**
- **Rationale:** Collapses the comptime-vs-macros paradox by refusing the premise that compile-time is a separate language from runtime. There is no separate metaprogramming system — there's just Avra code, and some of it is marked `@lifted` to run at compile time. Providers are modules with lifted exports and keyword registrations. Macros don't exist as a distinct mechanism. LLMs don't need to learn a second language or a restricted subset. Provider authors and user programmers are the same role — writing a provider is writing Avra code with compile-time annotations. The compiler's introspection API (from 1.2) is the same Avra functions operating on Avra data — no separate "compiler API" for metaprogramming.
- **Key properties:**
  - `@lifted fn foo() { ... }` — function runs at compile time
  - `const X = { ... computation ... }` — implicitly lifted
  - Providers register keywords via lifted functions
  - `@derive` annotations are lifted functions that return generated impl blocks
  - Hygiene handled by `generate_declaration` helper by default
  - Pure syntactic transforms (rewriting one AST into another) are NOT supported — this is a genuine limitation. DSLs with custom syntax use provider-registered keywords instead.
- **Cost:** compile times. Mitigations: lifted evaluation is cached, parallelized, and budgeted (error if a lifted call takes > N seconds).
- **Rename consideration:** the spec uses "lifted" as the user-facing term. "Comptime" may appear in compiler-internal docs but doesn't appear in user-facing syntax.

---

### Axis 2: Soundness vs Pragmatism

**Sub-decision 2.1: Soundness target**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Sound type system — if it compiles, type errors are impossible at runtime
  - (b) Mostly sound with known escape hatches (Rust's `unsafe`, TypeScript's `any`)
  - (c) Unsound by design, catches bugs but doesn't prove absence
- **Decision:** **(b) Mostly sound with escape hatches, scoped to abstraction levels**
- **Rationale:** Pure soundness is incompatible with vertical integration (P5) — FFI and hardware access require the compiler to trust foreign code. But unsound-everywhere (TypeScript's `any`) loses the LLM feedback loop that makes static typing valuable (1.1). The synthesis: soundness is a property of the *boundary*, not the code, and it aligns with abstraction levels. App-level Avra is fully sound. Systems-level is sound with compiler-checked ownership. Bare and hardware levels are unsound on purpose — you opted in. An unsafe value cannot leak out of a bare block into app-level code without an explicit conversion. This matches Rust's unsafe-as-abstraction-barrier model, but organized around Avra's scoped levels rather than per-block unsafe annotations.
- **Key properties:**
  - App level: type errors impossible at runtime. Period.
  - Systems level: type errors impossible; lifetime errors caught at compile time.
  - Bare level: unsound by design. Compiler trusts the author within the block.
  - Hardware level: unsound with targeted validation (register addresses, bit field layouts).
  - No `any` type in normal code. Unsound escape hatches are *scoped*, not ambient.
  - Soundness violations contained: a bare block's outputs must be convertible to sound types before returning to app/systems.

**Sub-decision 2.2: Where escape hatches live**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Per-expression (`value as int`)
  - (b) Per-block (`unsafe { ... }`)
  - (c) Per-abstraction-level (bare level drops safety)
  - (d) All of the above, at different granularities
- **Decision:** **(c) Per-abstraction-level only — no `as`, no `unsafe` blocks, no narrow annotations**
- **Rationale:** The abstraction level IS the soundness annotation — we already have this concept, and doubling up with a separate `unsafe` keyword is the kind of overlap Avra explicitly avoids (P6 paradox collapse). If you want unsound operations, drop to `bare { }`. This is more restrictive than Rust but makes auditing dramatically easier: show me all bare blocks = show me all unsoundness in the codebase. No scattered `unsafe` blocks throughout otherwise-safe code.
- **No `as` keyword:** Rust's `as` is a known wart — it conflates type narrowing (refining what the compiler already knows) with type reinterpretation (treating bytes as a different type). Avra separates them:
  - **Type narrowing:** use `is` or pattern matching. `if r is Success(s) { ... }` compiles to a tag check (no reflection needed — erased types still have tag bytes for enums/unions).
  - **Type conversion:** use explicit trait methods. `value.to_string()`, `s.parse::<int>()?`. No shortcut syntax for "trust me, this is a T."
  - **Type reinterpretation:** only inside `bare { }` blocks, via explicit annotations like `@transmute`. Visually jarring, clearly unsafe.
- **Runtime mechanism:** pattern matching and `is` narrowing work on erased types via compile-time-generated tag bytes (enums have `{ tag: u8, payload }` layout; unions with concrete distinct types are distinguished by layout; structural unions require discriminant fields — to be decided in 8.4/8.5).
- **Assertion casts:** `as!` does not exist. If you need "trust me it's a T," you pattern-match with a panic: `let s = match value { string(s) -> s, _ -> panic("expected string") }`. Verbose on purpose — unsafety should feel unsafe.

**Sub-decision 2.3: Runtime type errors — allowed?**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Never — compiler proves absence
  - (b) Only at explicit cast sites (`as!`)
  - (c) At any FFI boundary
  - (d) Allowed but marked
- **Decision:** **(b+) Runtime type errors impossible in app/systems code; panics exist for unrecoverable situations; panics contained at task boundaries**
- **Rationale:** The "runtime type error" category is empty in Avra's sound levels. No `as!` exists (decision 2.2). FFI boundaries return Result, not panics. Pattern matching narrows via tag checks, not reflection. The type system soundness claim holds: if it compiles at app/systems level, you will never get a "this value turned out to be a different type than the compiler thought" at runtime. Panics still exist, but only for genuinely unrecoverable situations: user-written `panic()`/`assert` (bugs), OOM, stack overflow, bare-level UB leakage. None of these are type errors.
- **Containment:** panics kill tasks, not programs. A panic in a spawned task becomes a `Result<T, PanicError>` to the parent. The main thread panicking kills the program, but main should spawn supervisors, not do work. This makes Avra "crash-contained" in the useful sense (Erlang's "let it crash" philosophy) without the unrealistic "no panics ever" sense.
- **Not pursued:** the pure "uncrashable" design — wrapping every arithmetic op, every allocation, every indexing operation in Result — was considered and rejected. The cost (pervasive Result-propagation) outweighs the benefit (you still can't meaningfully recover from OOM or stack overflow anyway). The research-grade options (panic negotiation, self-modifying programs, time-travel replay) were considered and deferred — interesting but not v1 material.
- **Forward compatibility note:** panics should be structured events carrying full context from day 1 (not just strings). This keeps the door open for richer supervision strategies (checkpoint restart, structured replay, LLM-assisted repair) in future versions without breaking changes to the panic contract.

---

### Axis 3: Nominal vs Structural Typing

**Sub-decision 3.1: Primary type mechanism**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Nominal only — types identified by name (Rust, Java, C)
  - (b) Structural only — types identified by shape (TypeScript, Go interfaces)
  - (c) Both, with explicit split — Avra's current direction
- **Decision:** **(c) Both, with explicit split**
- **Rationale:** Data wants to be structural (width subtyping without ceremony), behavior wants to be nominal (trait impls must be explicit), identity wants to be nominal (UserId vs ProductId distinction). No existing language gets this right — TypeScript is everything-structural, Rust is everything-nominal. Avra splits by keyword: `shape` for structural data records, `type` for nominal types and identity wrappers, `trait` for nominal behavior. This is the paradox-collapse (P6) applied to the nominal/structural dichotomy — both serve different purposes and coexist cleanly.

**Sub-decision 3.2: Keywords for the split**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `type` for nominal, `shape` for structural
  - (b) `type` for both, modifier like `@nominal` or `@structural`
  - (c) `record` for structural, `type` for nominal
  - (d) Context-dependent (inline vs declared)
- **Decision:** **(a) `type` for nominal, `shape` for structural**
- **Rationale:** "Shape" is the natural English word for what structural typing does — match the shape of data. `type` retains its conventional meaning (declared type, nominal identity). Two keywords make the split syntactically visible — reading `shape X = ...` vs `type X = ...` tells you immediately which semantics apply. Annotations (`@nominal`) add noise. Context-dependence (like TypeScript's `type`) is confusing because the same syntax means different things in different contexts.
- **Key property:** a nominal `type` whose fields include a `shape`'s fields automatically satisfies that shape. Structural compatibility doesn't require giving up nominal identity — a `User` is still a `User` (nominal), AND it satisfies `HasName` (structural) because its fields match. The split is about how the type system *identifies* types, not about which types can interoperate.

**Sub-decision 3.3: Records — structural or nominal?**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Always structural — shape matching, no declared identity
  - (b) Always nominal — declared types, no accidental matching
  - (c) Both available via different keywords
- **Decision:** **(c) Both available — `shape` for structural, `type` for nominal**
- **Rationale:** Trivially follows from 3.1 + 3.2. The substantive guidance is the default pattern for users and LLMs:
  - **Default to `shape`** for data passing through (requests, responses, rows, config, intermediate results). Most data in real programs is structural in spirit — you care about the fields, not the identity.
  - **Reach for `type`** when identity matters for correctness. IDs (`UserId`, `OrderId`), units (`Dollars`, `Meters`), domain wrappers (`Email`, `PhoneNumber`), state tags. The whole point is that a `UserId` is not an `OrderId` even if both wrap UUIDs.
- **Inline record literals** (`{ name: "alice" }`) are structural values with an inferred shape. They can be passed where a `shape` is expected. They cannot be passed where a nominal `type` is expected without an explicit conversion — the nominal type's identity must be opted into.
- **LLM guidance in CLAUDE.md:** the LLM should write `shape` first and only switch to `type` when identity matters. This nudges most code toward structural, which is the more ergonomic default.

**Sub-decision 3.4: Traits — structural or nominal?**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Nominal only — must explicitly `impl` to participate
  - (b) Structural — any type with the right methods participates
  - (c) Nominal by default, structural via opt-in
- **Decision:** **(a) Nominal only**
- **Rationale:** Traits are behavior contracts with intent. Structural trait matching (Go interfaces) causes accidental-interface bugs — a type satisfies an interface by coincidence because method names collide. It also makes refactoring dangerous (renaming a method might silently un-implement a trait somewhere) and makes "which traits does this type implement?" an open question across the whole codebase. Nominal impls make intent explicit, keep refactoring safe, and make implementation discoverable.
- **The shape/trait distinction:** shapes are about *data you can read* (field access at known offsets, no code runs). Traits are about *behavior you can invoke* (method calls, arbitrary code). They serve different purposes:
  - `shape HasBalance = { balance: int }` — requires a literal `balance` field
  - `trait HasBalance { fn balance(self) -> int }` — allows computed/derived values
- **Shared namespace across `shape`, `type`, `trait`:** you cannot have `shape HasBalance` and `trait HasBalance` in the same scope. A name in Avra points to one thing. If both would apply, pick the trait (strictly more powerful — any shape can be expressed as a trait with a trivial impl, but not vice versa). When you need both semantics, name them differently: `shape AccountData = { balance: int }` and `trait Accountable { fn balance(self) -> int }`.
- **Boilerplate mitigation via `@derive`:** the cost of nominal traits ("I have to write impl blocks") is addressed by `@derive`, which is a lifted function (per 1.3) that generates the impl. `@derive HasBalance(from: balance)` auto-generates the trivial impl when a type has the required field. See section below for the full derive model.

**Sub-decision 3.5: Width subtyping on records**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Allowed — a wider record is a subtype of a narrower one
  - (b) Disallowed — records match exactly
  - (c) Allowed on read, disallowed on write
- **Decision:** **(a) Allowed — wider shape is a subtype of narrower**
- **Rationale:** Width subtyping is the core value proposition of structural typing. Without it, `shape` is barely more useful than nominal types. If `user: { name: string, email: string }` cannot pass where `{ name: string }` is expected, users are forced to manually strip fields or declare narrower types everywhere — recreating the boilerplate that shapes exist to eliminate. With width subtyping, pipelines compose naturally: a function that takes `HasName` accepts anything with a name field, regardless of what else it carries.
- **Safety:** width subtyping on immutable shapes is always safe — you're just ignoring extra fields during reads. The complications only arise with mutation, which is handled in 3.6 (depth subtyping) and the broader mutability axis.

**Sub-decision 3.6: Depth subtyping on records**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Allowed — `{ x: Child }` subtype of `{ x: Parent }`
  - (b) Disallowed — field types must match exactly
  - (c) Allowed only for immutable fields
- **Decision:** **(c) Allowed only for immutable fields**
- **Rationale:** Depth subtyping is safe for reads (narrower type satisfies wider type's access patterns) but unsound for writes (a function expecting `{ owner: Animal }` could write a `Cat` into a field that's actually `{ owner: Dog }`). Because Avra is immutable by default (will commit in axis 11), most fields are immutable and depth subtyping just works. The restriction only bites on `mut` fields, which must match exactly.
- **Mental model:** immutable fields are covariant (narrower types OK). Mutable fields are invariant (exact match required). The compiler infers this from whether fields are declared `mut`, so the user never writes variance annotations. This is how the immutability-by-default decision dissolves most of the variance complexity in structural typing.

**Sub-decision 3.7: Private fields on structural types**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Allowed — private fields break structural matching
  - (b) Disallowed — structural types are always all-public
  - (c) Allowed but structural types ignore private fields
- **Decision:** **(b) Disallowed — `shape` is public fields only**
- **Rationale:** Structural typing and encapsulation have opposing goals. Shapes exist to describe data without ceremony — any matching structure works. Privacy exists to restrict access — "only I can construct/inspect this" — which is nominal identity in disguise. Allowing private fields on shapes would muddle both concepts.
- **The clean rule:** `shape` = public data description. `type` = nominal identity with optional privacy. If you need private fields, you need identity — use `type`. This keeps the split meaningful and forces users to make the right choice explicitly.
- **Error message:** trying to declare `shape User = { name: string, private x: string }` produces a compile error pointing to `type` as the alternative.

**Sub-decision 3.8: Newtype / wrapper types**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `type UserId = string` creates a nominal wrapper
  - (b) Separate `newtype` keyword
  - (c) Struct wrapping: `type UserId = { value: string }`
- **Decision:** **(a) `type UserId = UUID` creates a nominal wrapper**
- **Rationale:** `type` already means nominal (3.2), so `type UserId = UUID` naturally means "UserId is nominally distinct from UUID." Zero ceremony — no wrapper struct, no `.value` field access. A separate `newtype` keyword would duplicate functionality and violate P17 (composability over featurefulness). Mandatory struct wrapping is ugly — users shouldn't write `.value` everywhere to access the underlying type.
- **Construction and access:**
  - Construct: `UserId(some_uuid)` — explicit conversion
  - Access underlying: `uid.raw()` trait method or reverse construction `UUID(uid)`
  - Comparison with raw requires explicit conversion
- **Representation:** at runtime, `UserId` and `UUID` are the same bytes. Nominal distinction is erased after type-checking. Zero runtime cost.
- **Type safety guarantee:** `type UserId = UUID` and `type OrderId = UUID` are different types. A function taking `UserId` will reject `OrderId` even though both are UUIDs at runtime. This is the primary use case — preventing ID mixing at compile time.

---

### Axis 4: Inference Strength

**Sub-decision 4.1: Inference scope**

- **Options:**
  - (a) No inference — annotate everything
  - (b) Local only — inside function bodies
  - (c) Local + bidirectional — propagate expected types down
  - (d) Global (Hindley-Milner) — infer across function boundaries
- **Decision:** **(c) Local + bidirectional**
- **Rationale:** Global Hindley-Milner is tempting (no annotations anywhere) but has three fatal downsides for Avra: (1) error messages become terrible — when inference fails globally the compiler can't point to the "wrong" line, it points somewhere in the middle of a propagation chain; (2) function signatures stop being contracts — a small body change silently alters the public API, violating P9; (3) LLMs struggle with fully-inferred code because annotations are the spec — an LLM has to re-derive types every time it reads the function. Local + bidirectional gets 95% of the benefit without any of these costs.
- **Mechanism:**
  - Function signatures are explicit (commitment to P9 boundaries)
  - Function bodies infer everything
  - Bidirectional means expected types flow *down* into expressions — `let xs: List<User> = [...]` infers element type from context
  - `let x = expr` always infers; `let x: T = expr` provides the expected type for RHS inference

**Sub-decision 4.2: Function signature annotations**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Required — parameters and return types must be annotated
  - (b) Optional — infer from body
  - (c) Required for parameters, optional for return
- **Decision:** **(a) Required — both parameters and return types**
- **Rationale:** Function signatures are P9 boundaries. The signature is the contract — callers shouldn't need to read the body to know what they get back, and refactoring the body shouldn't silently alter the public API. Required annotations also double as a mini-spec that LLMs can read without parsing implementation. Inferred returns (TypeScript-style) feel lightweight but cause refactoring hazards and force hover-to-understand workflows.
- **Syntax:** `fn name(param: Type, ...) -> ReturnType { body }`
- **Exception:** void-returning functions may omit `-> Type`:
  ```
  fn log(msg: string) { println(msg) }     // void, return type implied
  fn get(id: UUID) -> User { ... }          // explicit return type
  ```
- **Closures are separate** — covered in 4.3. Only declared `fn` requires full signatures.

**Sub-decision 4.3: Closure parameter annotations**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Required — closures have explicit types
  - (b) Inferred from context (the expected closure type at call site)
  - (c) Inferred only for single-parameter case with `it`
- **Decision:** **(b) Inferred from context, for both single and multi-parameter closures**
- **Rationale:** Closures have call-site type context that declared `fn`s don't — `list.map(...)` already knows the closure takes the list's element type. Forcing annotations on closures is ceremony without benefit. Unlike declared functions (which are boundaries requiring contracts), closures are inline expressions where the types are derivable from context.
- **The ladder (all three forms work):**
  ```
  list.map((x: int) -> x * 2)    // full annotation (rare, verbose)
  list.map(x -> x * 2)           // parameter names, types inferred
  list.map(it * 2)               // `it` pronoun (single-param shortcut)
  
  // Multi-param also infers
  list.map((x, i) -> (i, x))     // both x and i inferred from context
  pairs.fold(0, (acc, p) -> acc + p.value)  // acc and p inferred
  ```
- **Annotation-only cases:** when the compiler genuinely can't infer (e.g., closure stored in a variable with no immediate call context), the user must annotate. Error message guides them to either add an annotation or provide context.

**Sub-decision 4.4: Empty collection literal inference**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Error without annotation — `[]` must have type context
  - (b) Infer to generic placeholder, constrain later
  - (c) Default to a specific type (e.g., `List<never>`)
- **Decision:** **(a) Error with a specific, actionable message**
- **Rationale:** Deferred inference (b) and type defaults (c) both hide the problem — when a downstream operation triggers the actual mismatch, the error message points to a consequence rather than the root cause. Erroring at the declaration site catches the ambiguity immediately with a clear fix. Bidirectional inference (4.1) handles most cases — `let xs: List<User> = []` works, `fn f(xs: List<User>) { ... }; f([])` works. The error only fires when a literal genuinely has no expected type, which is rare in practice.
- **Error format:**
  ```
  error[E0054]: cannot infer element type for empty list literal
  
    let xs = []
         ───   ──
          │     │
          │     ╰── `[]` here, no elements to infer from
          ╰── variable declared here
  
  help: annotate the type
    let xs: List<User> = []
  
  help: or provide initial elements
    let xs = [first_user]
  ```

**Sub-decision 4.5: Inference on method calls**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Method receivers must have concrete types
  - (b) Method calls participate in inference (trait resolution)
  - (c) Methods work only on concrete types; traits require bounds
- **Decision:** **(b) Method calls participate in inference, with strict resolution requirements**
- **Rationale:** Methods can be called on generic `T` only when the compiler can prove the method exists. The proof comes from three sources: (1) trait bounds (`T: Display`), (2) shape bounds (`T: HasName`), or (3) concrete types. The compiler does NOT do Go-style "look around for a method" — every method call has a definite resolution at compile time. This enables monomorphization, keeps errors local, and eliminates runtime method lookup.
- **Shape bounds give field access; trait bounds give method access:**
  ```
  // OK — shape declares the field
  fn greet<T: HasName>(x: T) -> string { `hello ${x.name}` }
  
  // ERROR — shapes have no methods
  fn call<T: HasName>(x: T) { x.do_something() }
  
  // OK — trait declares the method
  fn show<T: Display>(x: T) { println(x.display()) }
  
  // Combined — field AND method access
  fn both<T: HasName & Display>(x: T) -> string {
    `${x.name}: ${x.display()}`
  }
  ```
- **Error on unbounded generic method access:**
  ```
  fn f<T>(x: T) {
    x.name     // ERROR: T has no known fields
               // help: add a bound `T: HasName` or concrete type
  }
  ```

**Sub-decision 4.6: `let` vs explicit annotation ergonomics**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `let x = expr` — always infer
  - (b) `let x: Type = expr` — annotate when desired
  - (c) Require annotation for ambiguous expressions
- **Decision:** **(a) Both forms always work; style guidance in CLAUDE.md**
- **Rationale:** Mechanics are simple — both forms are valid everywhere. The question is when to use which, which is a style concern not a language rule. The compiler never forces annotation on normal code (exception: 4.4 empty literals).
- **LLM/style guidance (CLAUDE.md):**
  - **Use `let x = expr`** when the type is obvious from the RHS: `let count = users.length`, `let name = user.name`
  - **Use `let x: Type = expr`** when:
    - The expression result is an important boundary: `let users: List<User> = fetch(...)`
    - Disambiguating inference for intent: `let id: UserId = UserId(raw)`
    - Providing context for bidirectional inference: `let xs: List<User> = []`
    - The expression is complex enough that the type documents the result
  - At function signatures: always annotate (4.2).
  - At obvious locals: skip annotations.

---

## Part 2: Shape

How data is expressed, composed, and made generic.

---

### Axis 5: Polymorphism Strategy

**Sub-decision 5.1: Generic mechanism**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Parametric polymorphism (generics over types)
  - (b) Ad-hoc polymorphism (traits / type classes)
  - (c) Both — primary mechanism vs secondary
- **Decision:** **(c) Both as equals, plus shape bounds as a third constraint mechanism**
- **Rationale:** Parametric and ad-hoc polymorphism solve different problems. Generics abstract over *any type* (`List<T>` works for any T because the list doesn't inspect contents). Traits abstract over *types with specific capabilities* (`fn show<T: Display>(x: T)` requires T to know how to display itself). Almost every modern language has both because they're genuinely different — Rust, Swift, Haskell all have both.
- **Three constraint mechanisms (from 4.5):**
  - Unconstrained: `fn first<T>(xs: List<T>) -> T?` — works for any T
  - Trait bounds: `fn show<T: Display>(x: T)` — T must implement Display
  - Shape bounds: `fn greet<T: HasName>(x: T)` — T must have a name field
  - Combined: `fn f<T: HasName & Display>(x: T)` — both bounds
- **Syntax:** type parameters in angle brackets, bounds after colon, intersect with `&`.

**Sub-decision 5.2: Monomorphization vs dynamic dispatch**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Monomorphization only — specialized code per type
  - (b) Dynamic dispatch via trait objects (`dyn Trait`)
  - (c) Both — mono by default, `dyn` when needed
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — monomorphization by default, `dyn` for heterogeneity**
- **Rationale:** Mono delivers P4 Rust-level performance — specialized code per type enables inlining, dead code elimination, zero dispatch overhead. But some patterns genuinely need runtime dispatch: heterogeneous collections, plugin systems, dependency injection where concrete types aren't known. `dyn` is the escape hatch for these.
- **Syntax:**
  - `fn f<T: Display>(x: T)` — monomorphized (fast, specialized per T)
  - `fn f(x: impl Display)` — sugar for the above
  - `fn f(x: dyn Display)` — dynamic dispatch via vtable (slower, heterogeneous)
  - `let items: List<dyn Display> = [user, product, order]` — the main use case
- **Cost model:**
  - Mono: zero runtime overhead, larger binary (one version per specialization)
  - Dyn: fat pointer (data + vtable), ~1-2ns per method call, smaller binary
- **Critical for Phase 3:** the provider system requires `dyn` to store heterogeneous provider instances and register callbacks of different types. Spec `dyn` before Phase 3 ships. This resolves the accumulated open question from earlier deep dives.
- **Default guidance:** reach for mono first (just use a generic). Use `dyn` only when you genuinely need a heterogeneous collection or runtime-chosen implementation.

**Sub-decision 5.3: Constraint expression**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Trait bounds only: `fn f<T: Display>(x: T)`
  - (b) Structural bounds only: `fn f<T: { name: string }>(x: T)`
  - (c) Both — choose per-use
- **Decision:** **(c) Both, plus unconstrained — resolved as part of 5.1**
- **Rationale:** This is a consequence of 5.1's three-mechanism system. Bounds can be trait-based, shape-based, or both combined with `&`. Shape bounds can also reference inline shape literals (`T: { name: string }`) or declared shapes (`T: HasName`). Both forms work.
- **Examples:**
  ```
  fn f<T>(x: T)                              // unconstrained
  fn f<T: Display>(x: T)                     // trait bound (named)
  fn f<T: HasName>(x: T)                     // shape bound (named)
  fn f<T: { name: string }>(x: T)            // shape bound (inline)
  fn f<T: HasName & Display>(x: T)           // combined
  fn f<T: { name: string } & Display>(x: T)  // combined with inline shape
  ```

**Sub-decision 5.4: Where clauses / bound syntax**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Inline bounds only
  - (b) `where` clauses for complex bounds
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — inline for simple, `where` for complex**
- **Rationale:** Inline bounds (1-2 constraints) keep simple signatures compact. `where` clauses become necessary when bounds get complex (3+ constraints) or reference associated types. Forcing one or the other makes either simple code noisy or complex code unreadable.
- **Guidance:**
  - 1-2 bounds → inline: `fn show<T: Display & Eq>(x: T)`
  - 3+ bounds or associated type bounds → `where` clause:
    ```
    fn convert<T, U>(x: T) -> U
      where
        T: Into<U> & Clone,
        U: Display & Eq & Serializable
    { ... }
    ```
  - Bounds on associated types always use `where`:
    ```
    fn process<I: Iterator>(xs: I)
      where I.Item: Display
    { ... }
    ```

**Sub-decision 5.5: Const generics**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None — generics only over types
  - (b) Const generics over integers (array sizes, etc.)
  - (c) Full const generics over arbitrary types
- **Decision:** **(b) Const generics over integers, deferred to v1.x**
- **Rationale:** Const generics over integers unlock fixed-size arrays, matrices, and bit fields — all useful for systems/bare level performance work (SIMD, stack allocation, compile-time bounds). Rarely needed at app level. Full const generics over arbitrary values (strings, tuples) add enormous complexity for rare use cases. Skip entirely.
- **Deferred to v1.x:** not required for v1 shipping. Add when systems-level performance work demands them. The syntax `<T, const N: int>` is reserved so the grammar doesn't need to change when the feature lands.
- **Examples (post-v1):**
  ```
  type Array<T, const N: int>           // fixed-size array
  type Matrix<T, const R: int, const C: int>  // R×C matrix
  type BitSet<const N: int>             // N-bit set
  ```
- **Not allowed:** const generics over strings, tuples, structs, or other arbitrary types. Integers only.

---

### Axis 6: Variance

**Sub-decision 6.1: User-facing variance annotations**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Required — programmer declares variance
  - (b) Inferred — compiler derives from use
  - (c) Not exposed — language has no variance
- **Decision:** **(b) Inferred from use — no user-facing annotations**
- **Rationale:** Variance is already determined by language semantics committed earlier (immutability-by-default, depth subtyping on immutable fields only, mut fields invariant). The compiler can derive variance from a type's structure without user annotation:
  - Immutable field containing T → covariant in T
  - Mutable field containing T → invariant in T
  - T in function parameter position → contravariant
  - T in function return position → covariant
- Scala-style annotations (`+T`, `-T`) are a known cognitive burden and especially hostile to LLMs. Inferred variance eliminates the problem entirely without losing expressiveness.
- **What users see:** variance doesn't appear in their code. Ever. The compiler enforces correctness behind the scenes. Users see the consequences — "you can pass `List<Dog>` where `List<Animal>` is expected because the list is immutable" — through working code or clear error messages, not through annotation syntax.

**Sub-decision 6.2: Default variance for containers**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Invariant by default (safest)
  - (b) Covariant by default for immutable, invariant for mutable
  - (c) Covariant by default, allow opt-out
- **Decision:** **(b) Covariant for immutable containers, invariant for mutable**
- **Rationale:** Direct consequence of 3.6 (depth subtyping on immutable fields only) and 6.1 (inferred variance). Stated explicitly here for clarity: `List<Dog>` IS a subtype of `List<Animal>` when the list is immutable; it is NOT a subtype when the list is `mut`. This is the correct and safe default — it gives you the ergonomic benefit of covariance where it's safe (immutable) and blocks it where it's unsound (mutation).
- **Examples:**
  ```
  let dogs: List<Dog> = [...]
  let animals: List<Animal> = dogs       // OK — immutable List is covariant
  
  mut dogs: List<Dog> = [...]
  mut animals: List<Animal> = dogs       // ERROR — mut List is invariant
  ```

**Sub-decision 6.3: Function type variance**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Contravariant in args, covariant in return (standard)
  - (b) Invariant everywhere
  - (c) Configurable per function
- **Decision:** **(a) Contravariant in args, covariant in return**
- **Rationale:** The Liskov-correct default. A function accepting `Animal` can substitute for one accepting `Dog` (accepts more). A function returning `Dog` can substitute for one returning `Animal` (returns something more specific, still a valid Animal). No modern language deviates from this. Inferred by the compiler; users never write variance annotations on function types.
- **Example:**
  ```
  // Expected: fn(Dog) -> Animal
  
  fn make_pet(dog: Dog) -> Animal { dog }          // exact — OK
  fn pet_any(animal: Animal) -> Animal { animal }  // accepts more — OK
  fn make_dog(animal: Animal) -> Dog { ... }       // returns narrower — OK
  ```

---

### Axis 7: Higher-Kinded Types

**Sub-decision 7.1: HKT support**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None — no abstraction over type constructors
  - (b) Associated types only — traits can have type members
  - (c) Full HKTs — `Functor<F>` where F is a type constructor
  - (d) GATs (generic associated types) as middle ground
- **Decision:** **(b) Associated types only**
- **Rationale:** Full HKTs fail the LLM-first test — roughly 40% first-try accuracy for LLMs writing HKT code vs 95% for associated types. HKTs are also incompatible with structural typing (type constructors have no stable identity in a structural system). Associated types cover 95% of real use cases (operator traits, Iterator's `Item` type). GATs can be added later when a concrete use case emerges; not needed for v1.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  trait Iterator {
    type Item                             // associated type
    fn next(self) -> Option<Self.Item>
  }
  
  trait Add<Rhs = Self> {
    type Output                           // associated type
    fn add(self, rhs: Rhs) -> Self.Output
  }
  
  // Usage
  fn sum<T: Iterator>(xs: T) -> T.Item where T.Item: Add<Output = T.Item> {
    ...
  }
  ```
- **Deferred to v1.x:** GATs when the "map preserves container" pattern shows up in practice.

**Sub-decision 7.2: Associated types on traits**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Only on operator traits (`Add::Output`)
  - (b) On any trait
  - (c) None
- **Decision:** **(b) On any trait**
- **Rationale:** Restricting associated types to operator traits would be arbitrary — Iterator's `Item` is one of the most common non-operator use cases. The general principle: use generic parameters for "caller picks the type" (e.g., `List<T>`), use associated types for "impl picks the type" (e.g., `Iterator.Item` — once you impl Iterator for List<int>, the item type is int, not a caller choice).
- **Key property:** associated types enforce "there is exactly one correct answer" when that's true. A `List<int>` iterates over `int`, not over "whatever the caller asks for." Generic parameters would allow nonsensical multiple impls (`impl Iterator<string> for List<int>`); associated types block them.
- **Syntax access:** `Self.Item` inside the trait definition, `T.Item` from outside with a generic `T`.

---

### Axis 8: Algebraic Data Types

**Sub-decision 8.1: Sum type mechanism**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Tagged enums only (nominal, closed)
  - (b) Union types only (structural, open)
  - (c) Both — enums for declared, unions for ad-hoc
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — enums for domain concepts, unions for ad-hoc flexibility**
- **Rationale:** Enums and unions serve different purposes and both are valuable.
  - **Enums** are for declared domain concepts with nominal identity. Adding a variant is a breaking change (the correctness guarantee that powers exhaustive matching). Use for state machines, AST nodes, command types, domain models.
  - **Unions** are for ad-hoc type flexibility without declarations. `string | int` as a function parameter doesn't need a named type. Use for function parameters, JSON shapes, API boundaries.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  // Enum — nominal, declared, closed
  enum Shape {
    circle(radius: float)
    rect(width: float, height: float)
    point
  }
  
  // Union — structural, ad-hoc
  type StringOrInt = string | int
  type ApiResponse = SuccessResponse | ErrorResponse
  ```

**Sub-decision 8.2: Enum variant syntax**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Always qualified: `Shape.circle(5.0)`
  - (b) Contextually inferred: `.circle(5.0)` when type is known
  - (c) Always unqualified (variants in scope)
- **Decision:** **(b) Contextually inferred — `Shape.circle(5.0)` when ambiguous, `.circle(5.0)` when type is known**
- **Rationale:** The dot prefix visually signals "this is a variant of some enum" without cluttering code with the enum name when context makes it obvious. Match arms and function calls with known types use the shorthand; free-standing construction uses the qualified form. This is Swift's approach and works well for both humans and LLMs.
- **Examples:**
  ```
  // Ambiguous context — qualified
  let s = Shape.circle(5.0)
  
  // Known context — dot shorthand
  fn area(s: Shape) -> float {
    match s {
      .circle(r) -> 3.14 * r * r
      .rect(w, h) -> w * h
      .point -> 0.0
    }
  }
  
  // Known via parameter type — dot shorthand
  process(.active)                 // resolves to Status.active
  ```

**Sub-decision 8.3: Variant field style**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Named only: `circle(radius: float)`
  - (b) Positional only: `circle(float)`
  - (c) Both — named for multi-field, positional for wrappers
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — positional for single-value wrappers, named for multi-field**
- **Rationale:** Single-value wrappers like `Ok(T)`, `Some(T)`, `Err(E)` are natural without names — the type IS the field. Multi-field variants benefit from names to avoid argument-order confusion (`rect(width: 10, height: 20)` vs `rect(10, 20)`). Forcing one style makes either wrappers verbose or multi-field variants error-prone.
- **Style rule:** single-value wrappers use positional; variants with 2+ fields use named.
- **Examples:**
  ```
  enum Shape {
    circle(radius: float)                 // named
    rect(width: float, height: float)     // named
    point                                  // no fields
  }
  
  enum Result<T, E> {
    Ok(T)                                  // positional wrapper
    Err(E)                                 // positional wrapper
  }
  
  enum Option<T> { Some(T), None }         // positional wrapper + unit variant
  ```

**Sub-decision 8.4: Union type exhaustiveness**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Unions are open — no exhaustive matching
  - (b) Unions are closed when all members are concrete types — discriminated
  - (c) Only literal unions are exhaustive (`"a" | "b" | "c"`)
- **Decision:** **(b) Closed when all members are distinguishable — discriminated union matching**
- **Rationale:** If the compiler can prove a union has a finite, distinguishable member set at the match site, exhaustive matching works. This gives Avra TypeScript's best feature (discriminated unions) while remaining sound. The "distinguishable" check is a compile-time property:
  - Literal unions (`"a" | "b" | "c"`) — finite strings, all distinguishable
  - Primitive type unions (`string | int`) — different runtime layouts
  - Nominal type unions (`User | Product`) — different type identities (compiler inserts tag)
  - Structural unions require a discriminant field (see 8.5)
- **Example:**
  ```
  type Status = "pending" | "active" | "done"
  
  fn label(s: Status) -> string {
    match s {
      "pending" -> "waiting"
      "active" -> "running"
      "done" -> "complete"
      // exhaustive — all three covered
    }
  }
  
  fn broken(s: Status) -> string {
    match s {
      "pending" -> "waiting"
      "active" -> "running"
      // COMPILE ERROR: non-exhaustive, missing "done"
    }
  }
  ```
- **Resolves open question from earlier deep dives.**

**Sub-decision 8.5: Structural union discrimination**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Require explicit discriminant field (`{ kind: "a", ... } | { kind: "b", ... }`)
  - (b) Auto-discriminate by shape difference
  - (c) Reject ambiguous structural unions at compile time
- **Decision:** **(a) + (c) combined — auto-distinguish non-overlapping shapes, reject ambiguous ones, style guidance toward explicit discriminants**
- **Rationale:** The compiler's rule:
  - **Non-overlapping shapes** (completely different field names) are auto-discriminated — `{ data: string } | { error: string }` works because `data` and `error` are distinct keys.
  - **Overlapping shapes** require an explicit discriminant field — `{ name: string } | { name: string, age: int }` is ambiguous, the compiler rejects it with a help message.
  - **Style guidance (CLAUDE.md):** always prefer a `kind` or `type` discriminant field. It's forward-compatible (adding a variant doesn't break existing matches) and makes intent explicit.
- **Example:**
  ```
  // Works — non-overlapping shapes
  type Response = { data: string } | { error: string }
  
  // Preferred — explicit discriminant
  type Action =
    | { kind: "create", payload: User }
    | { kind: "delete", id: UUID }
    | { kind: "update", id: UUID, payload: UserPatch }
  
  // ERROR — overlapping shapes
  type Bad = { name: string } | { name: string, age: int }
  // help: add a discriminant field like `kind: "a" | "b"`
  ```
- **Runtime layout:** for unions with explicit discriminants, the discriminant IS the tag. For non-overlapping shapes without a discriminant, the runtime distinguishes by which fields are set (slightly more expensive check but no hidden tag byte).
- **Resolves open question from earlier deep dives.**

**Sub-decision 8.6: Inline enums**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `enum(pending, active, done)` as first-class AST node
  - (b) Desugar to `type X = "pending" | "active" | "done"` (literal union)
  - (c) Remove — use declared enums only
- **Decision:** **(c) Remove inline enums — require declared enums for domain concepts**
- **Rationale:** One mechanism is better than two. The inline `enum(...)` syntax was convenient shorthand but created a "which form do I use?" decision for both humans and LLMs. Requiring declared enums everywhere gives us:
  - **Single mechanism** for nominal sum types
  - **Nominal identity** even for small enums — no accidental string typos
  - **Refactor safety** — rename a variant, compiler finds every usage
  - **Reusability** — a declared enum can be used across models, functions, services
  - **The `.pending` shorthand** keeps working naturally
- **The cost (trivial):** one extra line of declaration. Pays for itself immediately through reuse and safety.
- **Examples:**
  ```
  // REQUIRED — declared enum for domain concepts
  enum TaskStatus { pending, active, done, failed }
  
  model Task { status: TaskStatus }
  
  match task.status {
    .pending -> "waiting"
    .active -> "running"
    .done -> "complete"
    .failed -> "errored"
  }
  ```
- **Literal unions still exist** for genuinely ad-hoc cases (parsing external data shapes you don't control, e.g., `response_type: "ok" | "error"` from a third-party API). The distinction is:
  - **Declared enum** for domain concepts (reusable, nominal, refactor-safe)
  - **Literal union** for pure data-flow at boundaries where nothing domain-specific is happening
- **Serialization:** `@derive Serializable` on an enum naturally produces the variant name as a JSON string. `TaskStatus.pending` → `"pending"` in JSON. No functional loss vs inline enums.

**Sub-decision 8.7: Wildcard arm policy**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Allowed freely, no warnings
  - (b) Lint warning when wildcard hides > N variants
  - (c) Require explicit `else` / `..` to opt into wildcards
- **Decision:** **(b) Allowed with lint warning when wildcard hides meaningful variants (threshold: > 2)**
- **Rationale:** Banning wildcards sounds safer but breaks legitimate uses: external `@non_exhaustive` enums (provider-owned, can add variants between versions), range matching, guard splits. The lint approach gets the safety benefit of exhaustiveness checking without the collateral damage — when a wildcard hides 3+ variants, the compiler warns with the hidden variants listed, making it trivial for the LLM/human to see what was missed and decide.
- **Warning format:**
  ```
  warning[E0092]: wildcard arm hides 3 variants
    |
  4 |   _ -> ignore()
    |   ^ matches: .pending, .active, .failed
    |
    = help: handle each variant explicitly, or annotate with @allow_wildcard
  ```
- **The LLM feedback loop:** warning message includes the hidden variants, so LLMs either add cases or use `@allow_wildcard` to acknowledge. No hidden bugs — every wildcard is either complete or explicitly acknowledged.

**Sub-decision 8.8: Non-exhaustive enums for providers**

- **Options:**
  - (a) No non-exhaustive marker — every enum is exhaustive
  - (b) `@non_exhaustive` attribute — external consumers need wildcard
  - (c) Provider enums default to non-exhaustive, user enums to exhaustive
- **Decision:** **(c) Smart defaults by source — provider-exported enums non-exhaustive by default, user enums exhaustive by default**
- **Rationale:** Provider authors ship enums knowing they'll evolve (HTTP methods, queue states, event types all likely to gain variants). Making non-exhaustive the default for provider exports prevents the footgun of provider updates silently breaking consumer match statements. User enums are local to the user's codebase — breaking your own code on variant additions IS the correctness guarantee (find every match site when you add a variant).
- **Override available in both directions:**
  ```
  // Provider export — non-exhaustive by default
  export enum HttpMethod { GET, POST, PUT, DELETE }
  
  // User match requires wildcard (non-exhaustive)
  match method {
    .GET -> handle_get()
    .POST -> handle_post()
    _ -> method_not_allowed()     // required
  }
  
  // Provider opt-in to exhaustive (for genuinely closed sets)
  @exhaustive export enum Protocol { http, https }
  
  // User enum — exhaustive by default
  enum TaskStatus { pending, active, done, failed }
  
  // User opt-in to non-exhaustive (for forward-compat across versions)
  @non_exhaustive enum FutureProofStatus { ... }
  ```
- **Interaction with 8.7:** the wildcard lint doesn't fire on non-exhaustive enums — the wildcard is required, so warning about it would be noise.
- **Resolves open question from earlier deep dives.**

**Sub-decision 8.9: Recursive enum memory**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Auto-box at app level, explicit at systems level
  - (b) Always require explicit `Box` / indirection
  - (c) Always auto-box everywhere
- **Decision:** **(a) Auto-box at app level, explicit at systems level**
- **Rationale:** Matches the scoped abstraction philosophy (P5/P6). App-level users don't think about memory layout — the compiler auto-boxes recursive enum variants transparently (refcounted per axis 9). Systems-level users own memory layout — recursive structures require explicit indirection (`Ref<Expr>`) so there are no hidden allocations.
- **App level — no ceremony:**
  ```
  enum Expr {
    lit(int)
    add(Expr, Expr)        // auto-boxed invisibly
    mul(Expr, Expr)
  }
  
  fn eval(e: Expr) -> int {
    match e {
      .lit(n) -> n
      .add(l, r) -> eval(l) + eval(r)    // normal field access
      .mul(l, r) -> eval(l) * eval(r)
    }
  }
  ```
- **Systems level — explicit:**
  ```
  systems {
    enum Expr {
      lit(int)
      add(Expr, Expr)         // ERROR: unbounded size at systems level
      // help: use explicit indirection
    }
    
    enum Expr {
      lit(int)
      add(Ref<Expr>, Ref<Expr>)     // correct systems-level form
    }
  }
  ```
- **Bootstrap impact:** the self-hosted compiler (written at app level) has recursive AST types (`enum Expr`, `enum Stmt`, `enum Pattern`). Auto-boxing means the compiler-in-Avra doesn't need Box annotations everywhere — significant ergonomic win for Phase 2+.
- **Resolves open question from earlier deep dives.**

**Sub-decision 8.10: Enum methods via impl**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Yes — `impl` works on enums same as structs
  - (b) No — use standalone functions
- **Decision:** **(a) Yes — `impl` blocks work on enums**
- **Rationale:** Enums are just types. If traits are nominal behavior contracts (3.4), they should apply to enums uniformly with every other type. Both trait impls and inherent impls work on enums.
- **Examples:**
  ```
  enum Shape {
    circle(radius: float)
    rect(width: float, height: float)
    point
  }
  
  // Trait impl
  impl Display for Shape {
    fn display(self) -> string {
      match self {
        .circle(r) -> `circle(${r})`
        .rect(w, h) -> `rect(${w}×${h})`
        .point -> "point"
      }
    }
  }
  
  // Inherent impl (no trait)
  impl Shape {
    fn area(self) -> float {
      match self {
        .circle(r) -> 3.14 * r * r
        .rect(w, h) -> w * h
        .point -> 0.0
      }
    }
  }
  ```

---

## Part 3: Memory and Effects

The runtime model — how memory is managed, what side effects are visible.

---

### Axis 9: Memory Management Model

**Sub-decision 9.1: Primary strategy**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Refcounting (Swift, Python)
  - (b) Ownership/borrowing (Rust)
  - (c) Garbage collection (Go, Java)
  - (d) Manual (C, Zig)
  - (e) Scoped — different strategies per abstraction level
- **Decision:** **(e) Scoped — different strategies per abstraction level**
- **Rationale:** P6 paradox-collapse applied to memory. No other production language makes memory management a scoped property. Each level has the right strategy for its domain:
  - **App level:** refcounting + arenas + targeted cycle detection — automatic, invisible, deterministic deallocation
  - **Systems level:** ownership with inferred lifetimes — zero runtime overhead, compile-time-checked
  - **Bare level:** manual — raw pointers, explicit alloc/free
  - **Hardware level:** memory-mapped — no allocation, direct addressing
- This is Avra's signature feature. It's what enables P5 vertical integration (mobile UI to registers in one language) without compromising P4 performance or P3 ergonomics.

**Sub-decision 9.2: Number of abstraction levels**

- **Options:**
  - (a) One — uniform model
  - (b) Two — application + systems
  - (c) Four — app / systems / bare / hardware
  - (d) Configurable per module
- **Decision:** **(c) Four — app / systems / bare / hardware**
- **Rationale:** Each level has distinct semantics and distinct targets. Collapsing any pair loses useful compiler help:
  - **App** — default, 95% of code, RC + arenas + cycle detection
  - **Systems** — performance-critical, ownership-tracked, zero RC overhead
  - **Bare** — general-purpose unsafe, raw pointers, SIMD, inline assembly
  - **Hardware** — embedded targets, register map validation, volatile I/O defaults
- **Why four specifically:** bare and hardware could theoretically collapse, but hardware level adds compiler validation for register addresses, bit field layouts, and volatile semantics that bare doesn't need. Separating them lets the compiler help with embedded development while keeping bare as general-purpose unsafe.

**Sub-decision 9.3: Level transition mechanics**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Block-scoped: `systems { ... }` transitions inside a function
  - (b) Function-scoped: `systems fn foo() { ... }` marks the whole fn
  - (c) Module-scoped: per-file declaration
  - (d) All three
- **Decision:** **(d) All three — block, function, and module scoping**
- **Rationale:** Each scope granularity solves a different use case:
  - **Block** (most common): a function mostly at app level but drops into systems/bare for a performance-critical section
  - **Function** (systems-level libraries): whole function runs at lower level, callers must be at that level or lower
  - **Module** (whole subsystems): device drivers, firmware, embedded code where every function is bare or hardware level
- **Examples:**
  ```
  // Block-scoped
  fn process_image(img: Image) -> Image {
    let meta = img.metadata                    // app level
    let filtered = systems {
      let buf = Buffer.alloc(img.size)          // systems level
      apply_filter(buf, img)
      Image.from_buffer(buf)
    }
    filtered.with_metadata(meta)                // back at app level
  }
  
  // Function-scoped
  systems fn fast_multiply(a: *f32, b: *f32, n: int) { ... }
  // Callable only from systems or bare level
  
  // Module-scoped (top of file)
  level hardware
  // every function in this file runs at hardware level
  ```
- **Boundary semantics** (details handled in later sub-decisions): values crossing level boundaries are converted or rejected based on the types' memory models.

**Sub-decision 9.4: RC atomicity**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Non-atomic (single-threaded)
  - (b) Atomic (thread-safe)
  - (c) Inferred per-type from use across spawn boundaries
- **Decision:** **(a) Non-atomic RC for v1.0; evolve to (c) inferred atomic when CPU parallelism lands**
- **Rationale:** Honest staging over claiming research-grade inference from day one. The v1.0 runtime is single-threaded green-threading (like early Go or node.js) — tasks cooperate on one OS thread, yield on I/O, scheduler runs another task. This gives us I/O concurrency (fine for most web services and agents, which is the bulk of Avra's target use case) without any RC race possibility because there's literally only one thread.
- **v1.0 model:**
  - RC operations are non-atomic (~2-5ns per op)
  - `spawn { ... }` creates green-thread tasks on the single runtime thread
  - Channels work between tasks on that thread
  - I/O is non-blocking — task yields on read/write, scheduler runs another
  - CPU-bound work needs to drop to systems level and manage threads manually
- **v1.x evolution (when CPU parallelism lands):**
  - Add `spawn cpu { ... }` for tasks on additional OS threads
  - Compiler analyzes which types can cross a `spawn cpu` boundary (captured by cpu-task closures, sent through channels used by cpu tasks, stored in statics)
  - Those types get atomic RC automatically
  - Everything else stays non-atomic
  - The inference rules will be grounded in real concurrency patterns rather than speculative
- **Prior art note:** Swift's `Sendable` is the closest shipping precedent for inferred atomicity (semi-inferred — user declares intent, compiler verifies). Full automatic inference based on spawn-capture analysis is more ambitious than any production language ships today; the staged approach lets us build confidence before committing.

**Sub-decision 9.5: Cycle detection**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None — document the limitation
  - (b) Weak references — programmer breaks cycles
  - (c) Targeted mark-and-sweep — only on types that could form cycles
  - (d) Full cycle collector
- **Decision:** **(c) Targeted mark-and-sweep with drop-site-precise scheduling**
- **Rationale:** (a) is hostile — bidirectional models, trees with parent pointers, and observer patterns all create cycles naturally. (b) requires programmers to anticipate cycles, violating P3 and especially hard for LLMs. (d) has unpredictable pause times, violating P4. Targeted approach: compiler analyzes type graphs statically, marks cycle-capable types, only instruments those. Drop-site-precise scheduling: sweeps run only at drop sites where refcount decrements but doesn't reach zero. Zero cost for non-cycle-capable types and for drops that reach zero normally. See axis 9 architectural summary for the full semantics and prior-art assessment.

**Sub-decision 9.6: Arena allocation**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None
  - (b) Pattern-detected (handle-and-respond scopes)
  - (c) Provider-driven (HTTP provider declares request scope)
  - (d) Explicit annotation (`@arena fn`)
- **Decision:** **(b) + generalized (c) + (d) — compiler-detected by default, provider-declarable scopes, explicit annotation for manual override**
- **Rationale:** Arenas are a major perf win for short-lived allocations (web requests, parser state, render frames, background job iterations, temporary computations). Three complementary mechanisms:
  - **Compiler-detected (primary):** scopes that allocate many short-lived objects and return zero or one composed value are automatically arena-backed. Bump allocation; O(1) cleanup at scope end; returned values copied out to the parent allocator.
  - **Provider-declarable (general, not HTTP-specific):** providers can mark function-like scopes as arena-suitable via a generic mechanism (e.g., `@arena_scope` on a callback type). Any provider pattern where a callback has well-defined input → output with intermediate allocations can participate — web request handlers, background job handlers, UI frame render callbacks, game tick functions, stream message processors, database query handlers, agent step handlers, etc. The language itself does not privilege any domain.
  - **Explicit annotation (escape hatch):** `@arena fn foo()` forces arena behavior when the programmer knows better than the compiler's heuristic.
- **Example (compiler-detected):**
  ```
  fn parse_config(text: string) -> Config {
    // compiler detects: many short-lived allocations, one returned value
    let tokens = tokenize(text)      // arena-allocated
    let ast = parse(tokens)          // arena-allocated  
    let config = validate(ast)       // arena-allocated
    config                            // returned — copied out
    // arena freed in O(1), no individual RC decrements
  }
  ```
- **Example (provider-declarable, generalized):**
  ```
  // A provider defines a callback shape that's arena-suitable
  @arena_scope
  type Handler<In, Out> = fn(In) -> Out
  
  // Any provider using this shape gets arena semantics for free
  // — HTTP, job queues, stream processors, frame renderers, etc.
  ```
- **What makes a scope arena-suitable:**
  - Bounded lifetime (scope end is deterministic)
  - Returned value composed from intermediate allocations (or no return)
  - No spawning of tasks that escape the scope
  - No storage of scope-local values in outer-scope containers
- **Interaction with cycle detection:** arena-scoped cycles are fine. The whole arena frees together; cycles within it are freed together. No cycle sweep needed inside arena scopes.
- **Versioning:** compiler-detected arenas ship in v1.0 (significant perf win, architecturally clean). Provider `@arena_scope` mechanism ships in v1.x when the provider system itself ships.

**Sub-decision 9.7: RC elision optimizations**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None — always retain/release
  - (b) Last-use move optimization
  - (c) Non-aliased locals skip RC entirely
  - (d) All of the above
- **Decision:** **(d) All of the above in v1.0 (basic elision); evolve to aggressive Perceus-style elision in v1.x**
- **Rationale:** RC elision is where most of the perf difference between naive and good RC lives (Swift's ~3-5× improvement over naive ARC is attributable to elision). v1.0 ships basic elision — low-hanging fruit with substantial payoff. v1.x adds aggressive elision as a compiler pass that plugs into the architecture without user-visible changes.
- **v1.0 basic elision (the options listed):**
  - (b) Last-use move: the final use of a value hands off the existing refcount instead of retaining
  - (c) Non-aliased locals skip RC: known-unique values (just-constructed, no alias path visible) have retain elided
  - Additional: retain+release pairs within the same function scope are eliminated
  - Additional: retain/release pairs are re-eliminated after inlining (second elision pass)
  - Expected improvement: 3-5× vs naive RC for typical workloads
- **v1.x aggressive elision (Perceus-inspired):**
  - Interprocedural analysis — elide retain/release across function boundaries without requiring inlining
  - Drop-and-realloc reuse — when a value is dropped and a new value of the same type/size is allocated at the same site, reuse the memory directly (no call to allocator)
  - Borrow inference at call sites — if the callee signature indicates it won't retain, the caller skips the retain+release pair
  - Expected improvement: approaches ownership-model performance for code with naturally unique ownership
- **Architectural fit:** elision works as a pass that rewrites `retain(ptr, strategy)` and `release(ptr, strategy)` IR calls into no-ops where safe. Commitment #5 (strategy-dispatched memory operations) makes this pluggable — the elision pass is swappable between v1.0 and v1.x without any change to user code or to the rest of the compiler.
- **Prior art:** Swift's ARC optimizer, Koka's Perceus, Lobster's inferred ownership — all demonstrate RC + elision can approach ownership-model performance for many patterns.

**Sub-decision 9.8: Systems-level borrow checker depth**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Move-only (no borrows) — Val/Hylo style
  - (b) Borrows with inferred lifetimes (no annotations)
  - (c) Full Rust-style borrow checker with lifetime parameters
- **Decision:** **(b) with escape to (c) when inference is ambiguous; phased rollout across v1.x and v2.0**
- **Rationale:** Inference handles the common case (90%+) with zero annotation burden. When inference is genuinely ambiguous (e.g., two input borrows with different lifetimes where the return could be tied to either), the compiler emits a clear error and requires explicit lifetime parameters. This gives us Rust-level rigor where needed and Python-level ergonomics everywhere else.
- **Systems-level borrow checker rollout:**
  - **v1.0:** No systems level implemented. `systems` keyword reserved; code using it errors with "reserved for future use."
  - **v1.x phase 1:** Inferred lifetimes + **immutable borrows only**. No mutable aliasing. Every borrow is `&T`, never `&mut T`. Programmer can still mutate through ownership — take ownership via move, mutate, return. This covers most use cases (parsers, readers, pure computations, data transformations) with a dramatically simpler implementation than full Rust.
  - **v1.x phase 2:** Add **mutable borrows with non-lexical lifetimes**. Aliasing-XOR-mutation enforced. Handles patterns like in-place buffer updates, iterator chains with mutation, callback-based APIs that mutate state.
  - **v2.0:** **Full Rust-equivalent** where explicit `'a`, `'b` lifetime parameters unlock patterns inference can't handle (e.g., functions that return borrows tied to one of several inputs in a non-obvious way).
- **Inference examples (v1.x):**
  ```
  // All inferred — zero annotations needed
  systems fn longest(a: &string, b: &string) -> &string {
    if a.length > b.length { a } else { b }
  }
  // Compiler infers: return tied to one of the inputs (whichever was returned);
  // both inputs must outlive the return.
  
  systems fn first_match(items: &List<string>, pattern: &string) -> &string {
    // Compiler infers: return tied to items (extracted from it), not pattern
    items.find(x -> x.contains(pattern))
  }
  ```
- **Explicit annotation example (v2.0, when needed):**
  ```
  // Ambiguous — return could tie to either input
  systems fn choose<'short, 'long>(
    temp: &'short Buffer,
    persistent: &'long Cache
  ) -> &'long Entry {
    // explicit: return tied to 'long, not 'short
    persistent.lookup(temp.key)
  }
  ```
- **Escape hatches at every phase:** if a pattern can't be expressed at systems level, drop to bare level (v2.0+) or escalate back to app level. No code is forced to use systems level.
