- **Prior art:** Rust's NLL (non-lexical lifetimes) is the template for v1.x phase 2. Val/Hylo demonstrates move-only is viable but restrictive. Our phased approach delivers Val's simplicity first, Rust's expressiveness later.

**Sub-decision 9.9: Stack vs heap allocation**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Primitives stack, composites heap
  - (b) Size-based threshold (small structs stack)
  - (c) Escape analysis — everything stack unless it escapes
- **Decision:** **(c) escape analysis with (a) as the baseline**
- **Rationale:** Primitives always go on the stack unconditionally. Composites default to heap (refcounted) but the compiler runs escape analysis — if a value doesn't escape the enclosing scope, it allocates on the stack instead. Zero annotations; compiler determines placement. Go does this, LLVM has native support, it's a free perf win with no ergonomic cost.
- **What "escapes" means:**
  - Returned from the function → escapes (heap)
  - Stored in a field of a heap-allocated object → escapes (heap)
  - Captured by a closure that escapes → escapes (heap)
  - Sent to a task/channel → escapes (heap)
  - Never captured, never returned, used only within scope → stays on stack
- **Examples:**
  ```
  fn compute() -> int {
    let point = Point { x: 1.0, y: 2.0 }   // stack — never escapes
    point.x + point.y                       // freed at scope end, zero RC cost
  }
  
  fn build_path(start: Point, end: Point) -> Path {
    let mid = midpoint(start, end)          // stack — doesn't escape
    Path { points: [start, mid, end] }      // Path on heap (returned), points copied in
  }
  
  fn gather_points(n: int) -> List<Point> {
    let xs = []
    for i in 0..n {
      let p = Point { x: i.float, y: i.float * 2 }  // heap — captured into xs (escapes)
      xs.push(p)
    }
    xs
  }
  ```
- **Interaction with RC elision (9.7):** stack-allocated values never participate in RC at all. Escape analysis and RC elision work together — first escape analysis removes allocations from heap when possible, then elision removes retain/release operations on remaining heap values when possible.
- **Implementation:** LLVM `alloca` for stack, runtime `rc_alloc` for heap. Escape analysis runs as an LLVM pass after Avra's IR is lowered, reusing proven infrastructure. No novel analysis needed.
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0. Free win, no risk.

**Sub-decision 9.10: Drop ordering**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Reverse declaration order (LIFO)
  - (b) Arbitrary / compiler-optimized
- **Decision:** **(a) Reverse declaration order (LIFO)**
- **Rationale:** Standard across C++ and Rust. Matches how programmers mentally model lifetime — things declared later often depend on things declared earlier, so they must drop first for RAII patterns to work safely. Arbitrary ordering risks use-after-drop in nested resource scenarios and breaks predictability.
- **Example:**
  ```
  fn example() {
    let file = File.open("log.txt")       // declared first
    let logger = Logger.new(file)          // declared second, depends on file
    
    logger.write("hello")
    
    // Scope end — drops in reverse:
    // 1. drop(logger) — closes logger, flushes buffers
    // 2. drop(file)   — closes file (logger no longer needs it)
  }
  ```
- **Interaction with callee-cleans ABI (commitment #6):** callee takes ownership on entry; drops happen at scope end in reverse declaration order. This is the universal default across the language.
- **Scope boundaries:** drop ordering applies within any scope (function body, block expression, match arm, loop body). Each scope drops its values in reverse declaration order when control exits the scope.

**Sub-decision 9.11: defer vs Drop interaction**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Drop runs after defer
  - (b) Drop runs before defer
  - (c) Interleaved by declaration order
- **Decision:** **(c) Interleaved by declaration order — both defer and Drop obey reverse-declaration order at scope exit**
- **Rationale:** `defer` (ad-hoc cleanup) and `Drop` (type-level destructor) are both "run this at scope exit" mechanisms. Distinguishing their ordering artificially creates confusion. Unifying them under reverse-declaration order makes both fully interoperable — a defer statement can reference values that haven't dropped yet (because they're declared later), and destructors can rely on resources set up by earlier defers being still valid.
- **Both mechanisms exist because they serve different needs:**
  - **Drop** is the type-level mechanism. Used when a type has inherent cleanup semantics (File closes, Connection releases, Lock unlocks). The cleanup travels with the type; anyone using the type gets correct cleanup automatically. This is the primary mechanism for resource management.
  - **defer** is the block-level mechanism. Used for ad-hoc cleanup that doesn't correspond to a specific type's lifetime (logging a block's completion, rolling back a transaction on early return, restoring global state). Defer lets you express "no matter how we exit this scope, do X."
- **Example:**
  ```
  fn handle_request(req: Request) -> Response {
    let file = File.open("log.txt")          // position 1: Drop
    defer { metrics.record("request_done") } // position 2: defer
    let logger = Logger.new(file)            // position 3: Drop
    defer { connection_pool.release(conn) }  // position 4: defer
    
    process(req, logger)
    
    // Scope end, reverse order:
    // 4. connection_pool.release(conn)
    // 3. drop(logger) — flushes buffers
    // 2. metrics.record("request_done")
    // 1. drop(file) — closes file
  }
  ```
- **Why both:** using only Drop forces the programmer to define a wrapper type for every ad-hoc cleanup (verbose). Using only defer misses the type-level correctness guarantee (a File without Drop could leak on early return). Having both — with unified ordering — gives the best of each.
- **Versioning:** both ship in v1.0. Both use the same scope-exit machinery in the compiler.

**Sub-decision 9.12: Copy trait definition**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Implicit — primitives only
  - (b) Auto-derived for types with only Copy fields
  - (c) Explicit `@copy` annotation
- **Decision:** **(b) Auto-derived for types with only Copy fields**
- **Rationale:** Most user-defined Copy types are small structs of primitives (Points, Vectors, Colors, Rects). Forcing explicit annotation on every such type is ceremony that violates P3. Auto-derivation based on field types works cleanly and predictably — if everything you contain is Copy, you're Copy.
- **The rule:**
  - Primitives (`int`, `float`, `bool`, `char`, etc.) are Copy
  - Tuples of Copy types are Copy
  - Structs where every field is Copy are Copy automatically
  - Types with any non-Copy field are not Copy (move semantics; explicit `.clone()` required for duplication)
  - Containers (`List<T>`, `Map<K, V>`, `string`) are never Copy — they'd be expensive to copy, so force a move or explicit clone
  - Enums are Copy if all variants' fields are Copy
- **Examples:**
  ```
  type Point = { x: float, y: float }               // auto Copy
  type Color = { r: u8, g: u8, b: u8, a: u8 }       // auto Copy
  type Rect = { origin: Point, size: Point }         // auto Copy (transitively)
  type User = { name: string, age: int }             // NOT Copy (string isn't)
  type Transform = { scale: float, rotation: float } // auto Copy
  
  let p = Point { x: 1, y: 2 }
  let q = p                                           // p copied; both usable
  
  let u = User { name: "alice", age: 30 }
  let v = u                                           // u moved; u no longer accessible
  let w = u.clone()                                   // explicit clone if both needed
  ```
- **Override:** `@no_copy` annotation on a type that would otherwise auto-derive Copy opts out. Useful for types where identity matters semantically even though structurally they could be copied (e.g., `type UserId = int` — we don't want accidental duplication even though int is Copy).
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0. The Copy trait interacts with move semantics which are meaningful at systems level (v1.x); at app level (v1.0) Copy vs move is a minor distinction because everything is refcounted and duplicating a reference is cheap. Auto-derivation still applies at app level because it informs which values can be used after being passed to a function without needing `.clone()`.

**Sub-decision 9.13: Closure capture semantics**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Retain by default (share), `move` keyword for ownership transfer
  - (b) Move by default, `share` keyword for sharing
  - (c) Inferred from usage
- **Decision:** **(c) Inferred from usage with explicit `move` annotation as escape hatch**
- **Rationale:** For app-level code, closures should Just Work without annotations. The compiler analyzes each captured variable and picks the right capture mode based on how the closure uses it:
  - **Read-only access** → capture by reference (zero copy, closure doesn't extend lifetime)
  - **Mutation** → capture by mutable reference (still zero copy; borrow-checked at systems level)
  - **Escaping closure** (returned, stored, spawned) → capture by value (move for non-Copy, copy for Copy)
- **Examples:**
  ```
  let counter = 0
  let increment = () -> counter += 1     // captures counter by mut ref (inferred)
  increment()
  increment()
  // counter is now 2
  
  let config = Config.load()
  let handler = () -> handle(config)     // captures config by ref (read-only, inferred)
  
  spawn {
    process(data)                         // captures data by move (escapes into spawn)
  }
  ```
- **Explicit escape hatch:** when the programmer wants to override inference, `move` annotation forces ownership transfer:
  ```
  let data = load_data()
  let snapshot = move () -> data.process()  // force move even if closure doesn't escape
  // data is no longer accessible here — moved into closure
  ```
- **Why the escape hatch exists:** rare cases where the programmer wants a closure to take ownership early (e.g., to release the outer scope from keeping the value alive, or to make a closure safely sendable to a task). Inference can't always know this intent.
- **Versioning:** inference ships in v1.0 for app level. Systems-level closure capture adds borrow-checker enforcement in v1.x.

**Sub-decision 9.14: Closures crossing level boundaries**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Forbidden
  - (b) Captures copied/converted at boundary
  - (c) Closure carries its capture memory model (coloring)
- **Decision:** **(b) Captures copied/converted at boundary, with compile-time enforcement — v1.x+ feature**
- **Versioning:** not relevant to v1.0 (which has only app level). The rules below apply when v1.x ships systems level and onwards.
- **Rationale:** (a) is too restrictive — many natural patterns pass closures across levels (e.g., an app-level callback into a systems-level iteration). (c) via closure coloring creates a painful type-system surface (two kinds of closure at every position, like async-colored functions in some languages). (b) makes the boundary explicit and predictable — closures cross, their captures convert, the cost is visible.
- **The four cases:**
  
  **Case 1: App-level closure passed to systems function.** Captures are app-level (refcounted). Systems code sees a callable. Each invocation briefly enters app-level context to access captures. Small per-call overhead (context bookkeeping); otherwise transparent. Common pattern; works by default.
  
  **Case 2: Systems-level closure returned to app level.** Systems closures can only capture systems-level values. If such a closure escapes to app level (returned, stored in an app-level container), compiler deep-copies captures and refcounts the closure. One-time conversion cost at the boundary; the closure becomes an app-level closure from that point on.
  
  **Case 3: Closure captured by a spawn across levels.** Cannot pass borrows (borrow may dangle when enclosing scope exits while the spawned task is still running). Compiler forces captures to be owned (moved) or refcounted (copied). Clear compile error with guidance if the user tries to capture a borrow.
  
  **Case 4: Bare-level closure escaping to app level (forbidden).** Bare level is unsafe; escaping bare-level data requires explicit conversion. A closure capturing a raw pointer cannot cross into app level — compile error. User must either convert the pointer to an app-level type before closure creation, or keep the closure confined to bare level.

- **What this commits:**
  - Compiler tracks each closure's capture-level set
  - Boundary crossings trigger implicit conversion with visible cost (profilable)
  - Borrows cannot cross spawn boundaries (forced ownership or refcount)
  - Bare-level captures cannot escape to higher levels (explicit conversion required)
- **v1.0 behavior:** closures are all app-level. No boundary question exists. `systems`, `bare`, `hardware` keywords reserved; using them errors "reserved for future use."
- **v1.x implementation:** add the boundary machinery when systems level ships. Architectural commitment #7 (boundaries explicit in IR) makes this a purely additive feature — v1.0 code with only app-level closures continues to work without modification.

**Sub-decision 9.15: String representation**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Heap + RC always
  - (b) Heap + RC with small string optimization (SSO)
  - (c) Interned for known-static strings, heap+RC for dynamic
- **Decision:** **(b) Heap + RC with SSO; interning (c) deferred as a backend optimization**
- **Rationale:** Strings are everywhere in LLM-generated code (error messages, JSON, logging, HTTP). SSO is a major perf win for short strings at zero ergonomic cost. Swift does this, C++ `std::string` does this, Rust's `String` doesn't but most real codebases reach for crates that do. Interning is a further win but adds compiler complexity — deferrable without changing any semantics.
- **Memory layout:**
  - A `string` is a 24-byte value on 64-bit systems
  - If ≤ 23 bytes, stored inline in those 24 bytes with a 1-bit discriminator (typically the high bit of the length byte)
  - If > 23 bytes, the 24 bytes contain: heap pointer (8 bytes) + length (8 bytes) + capacity (8 bytes)
  - The discriminator bit is free (alignment gives us bits to spare)
- **Encoding:** UTF-8 internally, always. No UTF-16 or UCS-4 representations.
- **Indexing and length semantics:**
  - `string.length` returns **byte count** by default (cheap, O(1))
  - `string.chars().count()` for codepoint count (O(n) scan)
  - `string.graphemes().count()` for user-perceived character count (O(n) scan)
  - `s[i]` indexes by byte position and returns `u8` (not char) — explicit and efficient
  - `s.chars().nth(i)` for codepoint access (O(n))
  - Prevents the Python/JavaScript confusion where string indexing is ambiguous between bytes, codepoints, and graphemes
- **Example:**
  ```
  let s = "hello"          // 5 bytes, stored inline (SSO)
  s.length                  // 5
  
  let big = "a long string exceeding twenty-three bytes here"  // heap-allocated
  big.length                // 47
  
  let greek = "αβγ"         // 6 bytes in UTF-8, inline (SSO)
  greek.length              // 6 (bytes)
  greek.chars().count()     // 3 (codepoints)
  ```
- **Literal interning (deferred to v1.x):** identical string literals appearing in multiple source locations can be deduplicated at compile time into a single heap allocation. This is a backend optimization that doesn't change any observable semantics — adds later without breaking v1.0 code. Prior art: Java's string interning, Rust's `&'static str` for literals.
- **Versioning:** SSO ships in v1.0. Literal interning ships in v1.x when profiling justifies it.
- **Interaction with memory strategies:** the 24-byte string value is a composite whose heap portion (for non-SSO strings) participates in RC like any other heap allocation. At systems level (v1.x), strings use ownership — moves transfer the 24 bytes, owned strings drop their heap portion at scope end. Small (SSO) strings have no heap portion, so they're effectively free to move.

**Sub-decision 9.16: `with` expression and field sharing**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Deep copy — `user with { name: "x" }` copies everything
  - (b) Structural sharing — unchanged fields are shared via RC
  - (c) Copy-on-write per field
- **Decision:** **(b) Structural sharing in v1.0; deeper optimization path documented for v1.x+**
- **Rationale:** Deep copy has bad performance for types with large nested fields — if `User` has a `List<Post>` with 1000 posts, `with { name: "bob" }` under deep-copy semantics would copy all 1000 posts unnecessarily. Structural sharing fixes this: only the changed field is new; unchanged fields are shared (RC+1) with the original. O(number of fields in the type), not O(total size). Matches Clojure, Elm, Immer (JS), Swift (for enum-like value types). Copy-on-write (c) adds complexity without obvious wins over structural sharing — skip.
- **v1.0 semantics:**
  ```
  let user = User { name: "alice", email: "a@x.com", age: 30 }
  let renamed = user with { name: "bob" }
  ```
  Desugars to:
  ```
  User {
    name: "bob",          // new value
    email: user.email,    // shared (retain +1 at app level; move at systems level)
    age: user.age,        // primitive, copied (free)
  }
  ```
  Total cost: one new struct allocation + field-count retains. Original `user` unchanged.
- **Rules:**
  - `with { field: value }` creates a new value with specified fields changed
  - Unchanged reference-typed fields are shared (retain); primitive fields copied (free)
  - Original is unchanged (immutability preserved)
  - Type must match — `with` cannot change a field's type
  - Multiple fields can be updated: `user with { name: "bob", age: 31 }`
- **v1.x+ optimization path (documented, not implemented in v1.0):**

  Structural sharing in v1.0 still allocates a new struct for every `with` expression. For hot paths that produce many incremental updates, a more efficient representation could be added:
  
  **(1) Persistent data structures for collections.** `List<T>`, `Map<K,V>`, and other containers could use persistent data structures (hash-array-mapped tries, RRB-trees) where `.with(index, value)` is O(log n) with structural sharing at the internal node level. Clojure's collections are the reference implementation. This makes `with` on large collections genuinely cheap, not just "cheaper than deep copy."
  
  **(2) Record diffing for small incremental updates.** When the compiler sees a pattern like `loop { acc = acc with { count: acc.count + 1 } }`, it can compile the `with` into an in-place mutation of the existing struct (when the compiler proves `acc` has a unique reference). This is RC elision (9.7) applied to `with` — eliminates the allocation entirely in hot loops.
  
  **(3) Structural sharing with content-addressed deduplication.** For models that serialize over the wire (API responses, event logs), identical substructures could share storage globally via content addressing. This is aspirational (v2.0+) and connects to the distributed computation design.
  
  **(4) Escape analysis for `with` chains.** A chain like `user with { a: 1 } with { b: 2 } with { c: 3 }` currently creates three intermediate allocations. Escape analysis could collapse the chain when intermediate values don't escape, fusing them into a single allocation with all three fields updated.
  
  These optimizations ship as the profiling workload demands them. They're all RC-elision-adjacent optimizations that don't require new syntax or break v1.0 behavior.
- **Versioning:** structural sharing ships in v1.0 (simple, correct, adequate). Persistent collection types and `with`-chain fusion ship in v1.x based on profiling. Content-addressed deduplication is v2.0+ territory.
- **Interaction with immutability:** `with` is the primary mechanism for "changing" data in immutable code. Combined with structural sharing, it gives you the ergonomics of mutation with the semantics of immutability — the foundation that makes reasoning about Avra programs easy and makes LLMs correct on first try. Mutation (`mut`, `=`) remains available when truly needed, but `with` is the idiomatic default.

---

### Axis 9 Architectural Summary: Memory Model and Versioning Strategy

> This section consolidates the extended design discussion into a coherent architectural commitment. It covers: the scoped abstraction model rationale, the app↔systems boundary semantics, the performance envelope, versioning strategy, and the ten architectural commitments that v1.0 must honor to preserve evolution paths. This content feeds directly into the TRD.

#### The Core Thesis

Memory management in Avra is a **scoped property of the code context**, not a global language property. Four abstraction levels exist (app, systems, bare, hardware), each with a different memory strategy appropriate to its domain. Code transitions between levels via explicit block/function/module scoping. The compiler manages the transitions automatically; programmers write level-appropriate code without cross-level ceremony.

- **App level:** refcounting + cycle detection + arenas. 95% of code. Programmers write Python-like code, never annotate memory.
- **Systems level:** ownership with inferred lifetimes. Hot paths, libraries. Rust-equivalent performance. Minimal annotation required only when lifetime is ambiguous.
- **Bare level:** manual memory, raw pointers. Unsafe by design. Used for FFI boundaries, inline assembly, SIMD.
- **Hardware level:** static allocation, memory-mapped I/O, register maps. Embedded targets only.

This is **P6 paradox-collapse applied to memory**. No shipped production language makes memory management a scoped per-context property. It is one of Avra's signature features, enabling P5 vertical integration (mobile UI to registers) without compromising P3 ergonomics or P4 performance.

#### The Prior-Art Honest Assessment

Memory management has five primitive mechanisms, all known for 50+ years: tracing, counting, ownership, scoping, and generational references. No sixth primitive has been discovered; the mathematical constraints are fundamental. Recent research (Vale's HGM, Koka's Perceus, MLKit's region inference, Rust's NLL) produces refinements and new combinations, not new primitives.

**Avra's innovation is NOT a new primitive.** It is:
1. **Integration at the language level** — four strategies cooperating in one compiler via explicit scoping
2. **Invisibility at app level** — the LLM writes app-level code and never thinks about memory; boundaries are compiler-managed
3. **Domain-connected memory scheduling** — `service`, `task`, `view`, `deploy` blocks naturally imply memory scopes

This is shipping-level innovation (productization and ergonomic engineering), not research-level innovation. The distinction matters for expectation-setting and for the TRD: Avra commits to proven primitives integrated in a novel way.

#### The App↔Systems Boundary Model

The single rule that makes multi-level memory work:

> **App level is refcounted. Systems level is owned. The boundary is a borrow.**

Concretely:

**App → systems crossing:**
- Compiler sees app-level value used inside a `systems` block or passed to a `systems fn`
- Inserts an implicit borrow — the systems code gets a non-owning view for the duration
- App-scope refcount is held high by the enclosing scope for exactly the borrow's duration
- Zero copy, zero refcount tick during the crossing

**Systems → app crossing (return values):**
- `Image.from_buffer(buf)` at systems level produces an owned value
- When it escapes back to app level, compiler inserts refcount initialization
- The app-level caller receives a refcount-1 value
- One-time allocation cost; no ongoing overhead

**What's forbidden (compile errors):**
- Systems code storing an app-level borrow longer than the borrow permits
- Returning a systems-level borrow to app level (the systems value must be owned or the borrow must stay inside the block)
- Taking owned ownership of an app-level value without compiler-provable uniqueness (see Ownership Transfer below)
- Mixing same-value expressions across levels in ways that would outlive the borrow

**Level-crossing spawn:**
- Tasks and spawned closures CANNOT capture borrows across level boundaries (the borrow may dangle when the enclosing scope exits while the task is still running)
- Spawn across levels requires ownership-taking; the value is retained (at app level) or moved (at systems level)
- This is enforced at the `boundary_out` instruction in IR; the compiler knows which boundaries are spawn-crossing and rejects borrow-passing

**Cross-level ownership constraint:**
- Owned transfer across levels requires the type to exist at both levels
- Primitives (int, float, bool, small structs with only primitive fields) exist at all levels and transfer freely
- App-level types with RC-specific semantics (refcounted containers, auto-boxed recursive enums) cannot be owned at systems level — they can only be borrowed
- If systems code needs ownership of complex app data, it operates on a systems-level type (e.g., a different Buffer type) that the caller constructs explicitly

#### Ownership Transfer at Boundaries

When a systems function wants to consume an app-level value (taking full ownership, not borrowing):

The compiler applies a three-tier strategy:

1. **Compile-time uniqueness proof:** if the compiler can prove the value has a unique reference at the call site (e.g., just-constructed, not captured, not aliased through any visible path), it transfers ownership for free — no retain, no release, just a pointer move.

2. **Clear compile error with guidance:** if uniqueness cannot be proven, the compiler emits an actionable error: "Value may be aliased; systems fn requires owned ownership. Options: (a) restructure to make uniqueness visible, (b) add explicit `.clone()` to deep-copy, (c) change the systems signature to accept `&T` instead of `T`."

3. **No runtime uniqueness check.** Unlike Swift's `consuming`, Avra does not insert runtime refcount checks. Either the compiler proves it or the programmer fixes it explicitly. This keeps the model predictable and the generated code clean.

#### The Performance Envelope

Realistic expectations, honestly stated:

- **Pure systems-level code:** matches Rust. Same LLVM backend, same optimization opportunities. No structural disadvantage.
- **Pure app-level code:** 2-5% slower than equivalent Rust due to RC operations. 10-20× faster than Python.
- **Mixed code with systems blocks:** dominated by whichever level contains the hot path. Boundary crossings cost 5-20ns each.
- **Fine-grained boundary crossing in tight loops:** potentially 10-30% slower than Rust. Mitigated by inlining across boundaries when both sides are visible; structurally avoidable by putting the entire loop inside a systems block.
- **Generic code straddling levels:** compile error unless type-level discipline is maintained. Ergonomics trade-off, not performance trade-off.

Where Avra may beat Rust in specific patterns:
- Domain-informed optimization (model lifecycle = arena lifetime, eliminating RC entirely for request-scoped entities)
- Fewer defensive clones (RC sharing handles what Rust requires `.clone()` for)
- Deploy-time target specialization (different memory strategies per target environment; v1.x+ feature)

None of these make Avra categorically faster than Rust. They enable competitive or slightly better performance in specific domain-aware patterns.

#### Where the Model Has Edges

Honest enumeration of friction points (none are fatal; all have documented mitigations):

1. **Long-lived app storage of systems-level values:** one-time refcount-wrapping cost at the boundary. Visible in profiling if done in tight loops.
2. **Ownership transfer requiring uniqueness proof:** some refactoring required when the compiler can't prove uniqueness. Clear error messages guide the fix.
3. **Closures crossing levels:** boxed and refcounted; small perf hit at iterator boundaries. Mitigated by monomorphization enabling inlining.
4. **Cycles involving systems-level data:** handled correctly as long as values fully convert to refcounted form when crossing back to app level (boundary machinery responsibility).
5. **Mutation visibility across boundaries:** in v1.0 (single-threaded), trivially safe. In v1.x (multi-threaded), becomes an aliasing-XOR-mutation constraint solved by channels or level-aware locking.
6. **Bare-level unsafety:** by design. Same tradeoff as Rust's `unsafe`. Compiler provides no guarantees; programmer assumes responsibility.
7. **Recursive enums crossing levels:** auto-boxed at app level, explicit `Ref<T>` at systems level. If a systems function needs ownership of a recursive structure, the structure must exist at systems level. Not a limitation unique to Avra — same constraint exists in any multi-strategy system.
8. **Cache-line consequences of external headers:** per-object headers are fine for standalone heap objects; collections store elements inline without per-element headers. Two code paths in the allocator but transparent to users.

#### The Ten Architectural Commitments (v1.0 Lock-In)

These are the non-negotiable architectural invariants that v1.0 must ship with, to preserve the evolution path to systems/bare/hardware without breaking forward compatibility. They cost approximately 15-20% additional implementation effort over "hardcoded RC" but preserve decades of future flexibility.

1. **Every type has a traversal protocol.** Types are not "tagged with a strategy" — they have a generated traversal function that describes which fields need retain/release/drop operations. Primitives have empty traversals; strings have single-field traversals; composite types have recursive traversals. The traversal is invariant across memory strategies; what changes is which operation the strategy applies at each field.

2. **Object layouts are strategy-independent.** A `User` struct has the same field offsets regardless of memory management strategy. RC headers sit **external** to the object (at `ptr - HEADER_SIZE`), never embedded inside the struct layout. This means the same struct layout can be allocated with or without a header in v1.x+ without any layout change.

3. **Standalone objects have per-object headers; collection elements are inline.** `RcBox<User>` has a header; `List<User>` stores Users inline without per-element headers (the list itself has one header, elements share the list's lifetime). Two allocation paths in the runtime, transparent to the user.

4. **Allocations route through runtime functions.** `rc_alloc(layout)` in v1.0. Adding `systems_alloc(layout)` later is purely additive — no changes to existing code. The compiler picks the allocator based on the context of the allocation site.

5. **Memory operations (retain, release, drop) route through a strategy interface.** The IR always emits `retain(ptr, strategy)`, `release(ptr, strategy)`, `drop(ptr, strategy)`. In v1.0 the strategy is always `app`. In v1.x it can be `systems`, `bare`, or `hardware`. The dispatch is per-operation.

6. **Function ABI is callee-cleans at app level.** Callee takes ownership on entry, decides whether to retain longer or let drop at scope end. At level boundaries, the boundary machinery handles the conversion explicitly (app→systems = inject borrow; systems→app = inject refcount init).

7. **Boundaries are explicit in IR.** Every function call and return carries `boundary_out`/`boundary_in` instructions marking the level transition (or "same level" in v1.0). v1.0 has one kind of boundary; v1.x+ adds more. The IR form is stable from day one.

8. **Generic monomorphization is per (type, traversal) pair.** Generic functions that don't perform memory operations monomorphize once (shared across strategies). Generic functions that do perform memory operations monomorphize per traversal. In v1.0 all types have the same-strategy traversal, so this looks like normal monomorphization; in v1.x it becomes essential for correctness.

9. **The runtime is a statically-linked library with a stable API.** v1.0's runtime (RC operations, cycle detector, allocator) is shipped as a static library with a frozen API. No dynamic runtime versioning. Each program carries its own runtime version. This enables the runtime to evolve with breaking changes across major versions without breaking old binaries.

10. **Lexer reserves all future keywords.** `systems`, `bare`, `hardware`, `owned`, `borrow`, `move`, `level`, `unsafe`, `extern`, `async`, `spawn`, `await`, `channel`, `select` are all reserved in v1.0 even if not implemented. Using them in v1.0 code produces "reserved for future use" errors, not syntax errors. This preserves the ability to introduce them in v1.x+ without breaking any v1.0 code.

#### Versioning Strategy

**v1.0 ships app-level only.** Refcounting + cycle detection (targeted mark-and-sweep with drop-site-precise scheduling) + arenas for short-lived allocations. Single-threaded runtime with green-thread `spawn` for I/O concurrency. No systems, bare, or hardware level implementation; all keywords reserved, code using them errors with "reserved for future use."

**v1.0-reserved (parsed but errored):**
- `systems { }`, `bare { }`, `hardware { }` blocks
- `systems fn`, `bare fn`, `hardware fn` modifiers
- `level <name>` module-scoped declarations
- `owned T`, `&T`, `&mut T`, `Ref<T>`, `Box<T>` type syntax
- `extern fn`, `@c_abi`, FFI syntax
- `spawn cpu`, CPU-parallel spawn

**v1.x:** Systems level with ownership + inferred lifetimes. Simplified borrow model (no mutable aliasing in v1.x first cut; added later). App↔systems boundary machinery. Atomic RC inference for multi-threaded contexts. CPU-parallel spawn.

**v2.0:** Bare level implementation. FFI via bare level. Hardware level for embedded targets. Full multi-threaded runtime with inferred atomic RC across all spawn boundaries.

**v2.x+:** Advanced features — refinement types, contract annotations with SMT discharge, deploy-time target specialization, sophisticated cycle elimination via compile-time analysis.

**Explicit non-goals (not planned at any version):**
- Dependent types
- Full HKTs
- Unrestricted macros
- Whole-program memory-layout inference (MLKit-style region inference)

#### What This Means for v1.0 Implementation

The v1.0 compiler and runtime implement:
- A straightforward Swift-style ARC runtime (well-understood, 3-6 months of careful implementation work)
- Cycle detection using the targeted approach (compiler identifies cycle-capable types, only those get instrumented, sweeps at drop sites where orphaned cycles are structurally possible)
- Arenas for block-scoped temporaries (compiler identifies blocks with many short-lived allocations; all go to a bump allocator freed at block end)
- The ten architectural commitments above — these are discipline in IR design and runtime API shape, not novel research

The v1.0 implementation is **simpler than Rust's v1.0 compiler was** because it ships only one memory strategy. The architectural overhead is in keeping the interfaces clean so future strategies can plug in.

#### Why Not Just Adopt Rust's Model?

Considered and explicitly rejected. The reasons:

1. **It defeats the LLM-first thesis.** Rust's borrow checker requires ownership reasoning for every program. That's precisely what makes LLM-generated Rust code brittle. Avra's differentiation comes from programmers (including LLMs) writing Python-like code at app level and only reaching for ownership at systems level.

2. **It removes the signature feature.** Scoped abstraction levels is the most differentiated aspect of Avra. Adopting Rust's universal ownership removes it.

3. **Rust has a decade head start.** Competing with Rust at being Rust is unwinnable. Avra has to occupy different ground.

4. **The complexity is addressable.** What feels like "mess" in the design is the necessary engineering specificity of combining two strategies. Every multi-strategy language (Swift, Kotlin, Go+C interop) has this complexity; it's not unique to Avra.

5. **v1.0 doesn't need Rust's complexity.** v1.0 ships RC only. The Rust-equivalent systems level is a v1.x concern, designed-for but not built-yet.

#### References for TRD Elaboration

- Swift ARC: precedent for external headers and callee-cleans ABI
- Koka Perceus: precedent for IR-level retain/release that becomes no-ops via analysis
- Vale Hybrid Generational Memory: precedent for static-analysis-reduced runtime checks
- Rust Non-Lexical Lifetimes: precedent for inferred lifetime analysis at systems level
- MLKit region inference: cautionary tale about poisoning when regions are inferred without domain hints

#### Considered and Rejected Alternatives

This section documents the novel memory-management ideas explored during design and why each was rejected. Captured for TRD because future maintainers and contributors will ask "why didn't you try X?" The honest answer for each is here.

**Idea A — Time-bounded memory (TTL annotations).** Every allocation carries a time-to-live; the runtime frees expired allocations regardless of references. *Rejected:* most allocations have no natural time bound. Would degenerate into regions-with-extra-syntax. Could work as a layer over a base strategy but not as the base itself.

**Idea B — Type-as-lifetime (kind-based categorization).** Types declare their kind (`value`, `entity`, `event`, `resource`), and strategy follows from kind. *Rejected as the base model, but influences the final design:* the "traversal protocol" commitment is a lower-level version of this — types do encode how their memory should be handled, just at a more primitive level.

**Idea C — Reactive lifetime (observer-tracking).** Objects live as long as their subscribers; references don't extend lifetime, subscriptions do. *Rejected:* too specialized for reactive UI. Non-reactive code (compilation, scripts, background workers) becomes baroque. Could coexist as a reactive-subsystem strategy but not general.

**Idea D — Effect-directed memory (lifetimes as effects).** Functions declare memory effects (`reads(users)`, `extends(user, cache)`, `creates(result, caller)`); compiler proves lifetimes from declarations. *Rejected:* requires effect annotations everywhere, violating P3 zero ceremony. Would need aggressive inference to be acceptable, and inference for effect systems is research-grade with limited shipping precedent.

**Idea E — Ownership transfer as the only primitive.** No aliasing ever; every value has exactly one owner, compiler auto-inserts copies where needed. *Rejected as the base model but informs the systems level:* this is essentially what systems level becomes. Applying it universally would force app-level users to reason about ownership, defeating the LLM-first thesis.

**Idea F — Transactional memory as lifetime.** Every allocation is tentative until commit; commits promote to longer lifetimes, rollbacks discard atomically. *Rejected:* not all code is transactional. Compilers, background workers, long-running processes don't fit. Could be a model for service code specifically but not general.

**Idea G — Immutable + structural sharing with scoped mutation.** Default to immutability with structural sharing; explicit `mut()` blocks for local mutation. *Rejected as the base model but informs the language:* immutability-by-default IS the language default. Structural sharing is how `with` expressions work (see sub-decision 9.16). The "cycles can't form because you can't patch pointers" property is genuinely valuable but doesn't eliminate the need for a memory strategy — it just removes one class of problem.

**Domain-directed memory scheduling.** Use `service`, `task`, `view`, `deploy` blocks as implicit region boundaries. *Rejected as the base:* helper functions and library code don't live inside domain blocks; the fallback story was unclear and scaled poorly. Could inform optimizations (model lifecycle = arena lifetime is still planned as a compiler optimization at app level) but cannot be the universal strategy.

**Rust's borrow checker universally.** *Explicitly rejected.* See the "Why Not Just Adopt Rust's Model?" section above. The five reasons are concrete and the decision is locked.

**The meta-learning:** 50+ years of memory management research has produced exactly five primitive mechanisms. No sixth primitive has been discovered despite intense effort. Avra does not attempt to invent a sixth. It combines proven primitives in a novel integration — scoped per abstraction level, with domain-level semantic hints informing allocation strategy, and LLM-first ergonomics as the design constraint.

#### Cycle Detection: Drop-Site-Precise Scheduling

This is the one implementation detail that may be genuinely novel and warrants TRD elaboration.

**Standard targeted mark-and-sweep:** compiler identifies cycle-capable types at compile time (types whose field graphs can contain self-reference transitively). Only those types get instrumented. Sweeps run periodically.

**Drop-site-precise scheduling (Avra's refinement):** the compiler has full knowledge of which drop sites could create orphaned cycles — specifically, drops where the refcount goes from N > 0 to N > 0 (i.e., the drop didn't free the object, meaning there's still a reference somewhere, meaning a cycle is structurally possible). Only those drops trigger cycle-collection passes.

**Concrete semantics:**
```
fn example() {
  let a = Node.new()        // cycle-capable type; refcount = 1
  let b = Node.new()        // refcount = 1
  a.linked = b               // b.refcount = 2
  b.linked = a               // a.refcount = 2  — CYCLE FORMED
  
  // function ends
  // release(a): a.refcount 2 → 1. Not freed. Orphaned-cycle-possible.
  //   → schedule cycle sweep for a
  // release(b): b.refcount 2 → 1. Not freed. Orphaned-cycle-possible.
  //   → schedule cycle sweep for b (already scheduled, coalesce)
  
  // Cycle sweep runs: traces from a and b, finds a↔b cycle with
  // zero external references, frees both.
}

fn no_cycle_case() {
  let x = Point.new()        // not cycle-capable; no instrumentation
  let y = Wrapper.new(x)     // not cycle-capable (Wrapper doesn't contain Wrapper)
  // function ends
  // release(y): y.refcount 1 → 0. Freed. No sweep needed.
  // release(x): x.refcount 1 → 0. Freed. No sweep needed.
  // Zero cycle-detection cost.
}
```

**Properties:**
- Zero amortized cost for the common case (most types aren't cycle-capable; most drops reach zero normally)
- Deterministic: sweep runs when cycle is possible, not on a schedule
- Bounded work: sweep only traces the specific type's field graph, not the full heap
- Composable with elision: if the compiler can prove a particular drop cannot leave an orphaned cycle (e.g., the other references are known from visible code), no sweep is scheduled

**Prior art check:** I am not aware of a shipping language that does exactly this. Swift, Python, CPython, Nim, and Perceus-based languages use different combinations — periodic sweeps, manual weak refs, or whole-object-graph tracing. Drop-site-precise scheduling is a refinement that may warrant publication if it survives implementation contact with reality. TRD should treat it as "plan A, with periodic mark-and-sweep as fallback if drop-site precision proves too complex to implement correctly."

---

### Axis 10: Null and Optionality

**Sub-decision 10.1: Null representation**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `null` as a value of any type (Java-style, unsafe)
  - (b) `Option<T>` enum only (Rust)
  - (c) Nullable type syntax `T?` desugaring to Option (Kotlin, Swift)
  - (d) Both `T?` and `Option<T>` as aliases
- **Decision:** **(c) `T?` as syntactic sugar for `Option<T>`; pattern match as `value?` (present) and `null` (absent)**
- **Rationale:** `null` as a value of any type (a) is famously unsafe (the billion-dollar mistake); not considered. Pure `Option<T>` (b) is safe but verbose — `Option<User>` everywhere is ceremony. `T?` (c) is cleaner syntactically while compiling to the same underlying Option type — zero semantic difference, better ergonomics. Kotlin and Swift prove this works. Having both as separate aliases (d) is unnecessarily redundant.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  // T? is Option<T> syntactic sugar
  fn find_user(id: UUID) -> User? {
    // returns a user or null
  }
  
  // Pattern match
  let maybe_user = find_user(id)
  match maybe_user {
    u?    -> println(u.name)       // present, bind to u
    null  -> println("not found")  // absent
  }
  
  // while-let
  while let line? = stream.read_line() {
    process(line)
  }
  
  // if-let
  if let user? = find_user(id) {
    render(user)
  } else {
    render_404()
  }
  
  // Propagation via ? operator (postfix on expression)
  fn get_user_name(id: UUID) -> string? {
    let user = find_user(id)?      // unwrap or return null
    user.name
  }
  ```
- **The unified `?` story:**
  - **Type suffix:** `User?` means "User or null"
  - **Pattern suffix:** `user?` in a match arm means "value is present, bind it"
  - **Expression suffix:** `find_user(id)?` means "unwrap or propagate"
  - Same glyph, same intuition everywhere: `?` signals optionality.
- **Construction:**
  - A non-null value: just the value, written directly (`let user: User? = get_user()` or just assigning a `User` value — auto-widens)
  - Null: the `null` literal. Only valid in contexts where a `T?` type is expected. `let x: int = null` is a compile error; `let x: int? = null` is fine.
- **No `.some()` / `.none` variants:** `T?` is a first-class type with its own syntax, not a user-visible enum with variants. The compiler may represent it internally as a two-variant enum but programmers never write `.some(x)` or `.none`.
- **Unified propagation with Result (12.x):** `?` on `Result<T, E>` propagates errors; `?` on `T?` propagates null (returning null from the surrounding function, which must have a `U?` return type). The compiler type-checks which form applies.

**Sub-decision 10.2: Non-null guarantee**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Non-null by default, nullable opt-in via `?`
  - (b) Nullable by default, non-null opt-in
- **Decision:** **(a) Non-null by default; nullability opt-in via `?`**
- **Rationale:** Non-null by default is the correct, safe default. Every type declaration is guaranteed to hold a valid value; `?` is a deliberate opt-in to potential absence. The opposite (nullable-by-default) is the Java/C# mistake that has cost untold bug hours. With non-null-by-default, the compiler can prove that a `User` value is always a valid User, never a "maybe User." Invalidated states must be explicitly encoded in the type.
- **Example:**
  ```
  fn greet(user: User) -> string {
    user.name                // always valid, no null check needed
  }
  
  fn greet_maybe(user: User?) -> string {
    match user {
      u?    -> u.name
      null  -> "stranger"
    }
  }
  ```

**Sub-decision 10.3: Null-safe access operators**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `?.` for chaining, `??` for defaults, `!` for assertion
  - (b) Pattern matching only (`match x { Some(v) -> ..., None -> ... }`)
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — operators for common cases, pattern matching for exhaustive handling**
- **Rationale:** Pattern matching is explicit and exhaustive but verbose for simple propagation. Operators are concise for common idioms. Having both lets programmers pick the right tool per situation — operators for single-point access, pattern matching when both branches need meaningful logic.
- **Operators:**
  - **`?.` optional chaining:** `user?.email` returns `user.email` if `user` is `.some`, `.none` otherwise. Chainable: `user?.address?.city?.postcode`. Every step that hits `.none` short-circuits the chain.
  - **`??` null coalescing:** `name ?? "anonymous"` returns `name` if `.some(value)`, otherwise the default. Right-side is only evaluated when needed (short-circuit).
  - **`!` unwrap assertion:** `user!` extracts the inner value if `.some`, panics if `.none`. Used only when the programmer can prove the value is present but the type system can't. Style guidance: prefer `?.` or pattern match; use `!` sparingly.
- **Pattern matching (always available):**
  ```
  match user {
    .some(u) if u.verified -> process(u)
    .some(u) -> notify_unverified(u)
    .none -> request_signup()
  }
  ```
- **Combined example:**
  ```
  let display_name = user?.profile?.display_name ?? "anonymous"
  // - user?.profile: accesses profile only if user is .some
  // - ?.display_name: accesses display_name only if profile is .some
  // - ?? "anonymous": falls back if any step was .none
  ```

**Sub-decision 10.4: Smart narrowing after null check**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Flow-sensitive typing — `if x != null { x.method() }` works
  - (b) Require explicit unwrapping or pattern match
- **Decision:** **(a) Flow-sensitive typing — compiler narrows type within branches**
- **Rationale:** TypeScript and Kotlin have proven flow-sensitive narrowing is both safe and dramatically more ergonomic than forcing pattern matches for every nullable check. After the compiler sees `if user != .none`, it knows `user` is `.some(U)` within the branch and lets you access it as `U`. Zero runtime cost (the check already happened); massive ergonomic win.
- **Example:**
  ```
  fn send_email(user: User?) {
    if user != .none {
      // Inside this branch, user is narrowed from User? to User
      send(user.email)            // direct field access, no unwrap
    }
  }
  
  // Equivalent using explicit pattern match
  fn send_email_verbose(user: User?) {
    match user {
      .some(u) -> send(u.email)
      .none -> {}
    }
  }
  ```
- **What narrows type:**
  - `if x != .none { ... }` — x becomes non-null in the if-branch
  - `if x == .none { return } ; ...` — x is non-null after the early return
  - `let Some(v) = x else { return }` — v is bound to the inner value
  - Pattern match arms narrow appropriately
- **Interaction with `?.`:** flow narrowing and optional chaining are complementary. Narrowing is for branches where the programmer wants direct access; `?.` is for single-point propagation.

**Sub-decision 10.5: `?` operator on nullable**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Works for null propagation (early-return null) if fn returns `T?`
  - (b) `?` only for Result — nullable uses `??` and `?.`
- **Decision:** **(a) `?` suffix works for null propagation when enclosing function returns `T?`**
- **Rationale:** Consistent with Rust's `?` on Result, extended to Option. Common idiom: "if this step fails to find something, short-circuit and return `.none` from the enclosing function." Forcing pattern matching or nested `?.` chains for this case is ceremony. The `?` suffix is cheap, clear, and widely understood from Rust/Swift precedent.
- **Semantics:** `expr?` unwraps if `.some(v)`, returns `.none` from the enclosing function if `.none`. Only valid when the enclosing function's return type is `T?` (or `Result<T, E>` — axis 12 extension).
- **Example:**
  ```
  fn get_user_email(id: UUID) -> string? {
    let user = find_user(id)?          // returns .none if user not found
    let profile = user.profile?         // returns .none if no profile
    profile.email                        // returns as-is (already .some'd by unwrap chain)
  }
  
  // Equivalent with nested match
  fn get_user_email_verbose(id: UUID) -> string? {
    match find_user(id) {
      .none -> .none
      .some(user) -> match user.profile {
        .none -> .none
        .some(profile) -> .some(profile.email)
      }
    }
  }
  ```
- **Interaction with error handling (axis 12):** `?` is the unified propagation operator for both `T?` (Option) and `Result<T, E>`. The compiler determines which based on the expression type. Mixing them in the same function requires explicit conversion at the boundary.
- **What `?` doesn't do:** `?` doesn't work as a general "if failed, do something else" operator. For that, use `??` (default value) or `match` (arbitrary logic).

---

### Axis 11: Mutability and Aliasing

**Sub-decision 11.1: Default mutability**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Immutable by default, `mut` opts in
  - (b) Mutable by default, `const`/`let` opts out
- **Decision:** **(a) Immutable by default; `mut` opts in**
- **Rationale:** Immutable-by-default is foundational to Avra's design and foundational to LLM correctness. It's how:
  - **Structural equality works** — immutable values can be compared by content without concern about mutation during comparison
  - **RC stays efficient** — immutable values don't need synchronization for their RC operations, and cycle detection is simpler because the type graph can't change
  - **`with` expressions make sense** — structural sharing is safe only if values never change
  - **Reasoning scales** — reading code requires no mental model of "who might be mutating this when" at every access
  - **LLMs succeed** — the dominant failure mode of LLM-generated code is racy mutation; eliminating the default removes the whole class
- Mutation remains available via explicit `mut` for local state machines, counters, and performance-critical buffer operations. The cost of typing three extra characters at mutation sites is negligible; the cognitive cost of defaulting to mutation is enormous.
- **Example:**
  ```
  let user = User { name: "alice" }       // immutable binding and value
  // user.name = "bob"                    // ERROR: cannot mutate
  // user = User { name: "bob" }          // ERROR: cannot rebind
  
  mut counter = 0
  counter += 1                             // OK — mut allows mutation
  
  let updated = user with { name: "bob" }  // immutable update via structural sharing
  ```

**Sub-decision 11.2: Compile-time constants**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `const` keyword separate from `let`
  - (b) `let` doubles as const when expression is constant
  - (c) Compiler infers constness
- **Decision:** **(a) `const` keyword for compile-time constants, distinct from `let`**
- **Rationale:** `const` and `let` serve different semantic roles. `const` declares compile-time-evaluated values that can be used in type positions (e.g., array sizes, enum discriminants), inlined at every use site, and crossed between modules with guaranteed identity. `let` declares runtime immutable bindings — evaluated once per execution, bound to a local scope, heap-allocated if not primitive. Conflating them obscures the distinction and prevents `const` from being usable where compile-time evaluation is required.
- **Examples:**
  ```
  const MAX_USERS: int = 1000                // compile-time
  const PI: float = 3.14159265358979
  const APP_NAME = "avra"                     // type inferred
  
  // const can be used in type positions
  type Buffer = Array<u8, MAX_USERS>          // v1.x+ const generics
  
  // let is runtime
  let now = Time.now()                        // cannot be const — not compile-time
  let user = User.load(id)                    // runtime value
  
  // const expressions are evaluated at compile time
  const HALF_MAX = MAX_USERS / 2              // computed once, embedded as literal
  ```
- **Rules:**
  - `const` expressions must be pure and use only compile-time-known inputs
  - `const` values have static lifetime (accessible anywhere, no scope bound)
  - `const` can be used in type parameters (const generics, v1.x+)
  - `let` expressions can be any runtime expression
  - Both are immutable (as per 11.1); `mut` applies only to `let`-like bindings
- **Error case:**
  ```
  const now = Time.now()  // ERROR: not a compile-time expression
  //          help: use `let` for runtime values
  ```

**Sub-decision 11.3: Interior mutability**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not supported — mut is the only mutation
  - (b) Via wrapper types (`Cell`, `RefCell`, `Mutex`)
  - (c) Inferred (automatic for thread-local state)
- **Decision:** **(b) Via wrapper types — explicit opt-in**
- **Rationale:** Interior mutability (the ability to mutate a value through a shared reference) is sometimes necessary — caches, lazy initialization, thread-local state, pooled resources. But it's a deviation from the "immutable by default" invariant that underpins Avra's reasoning story. Making it explicit via wrapper types ensures the programmer acknowledges the deviation at the type level; code reviewers and LLMs can see `Cell<T>` in a signature and know to reason about mutation. Inferred interior mutability (c) hides the deviation and defeats the reasoning benefit.
- **Wrapper types provided (stdlib):**
  - **`Cell<T>`** — single-threaded interior mutability; get/set through shared ref; v1.0
  - **`Mutex<T>`** — multi-threaded mutation with locking; v1.x+ when concurrency lands
  - **`Lazy<T>`** — initialize-once, then read-only; v1.0
  - **`OnceCell<T>`** — set-once, then immutable; v1.0
- **Example:**
  ```
  type Cache = {
    entries: Cell<Map<string, User>>    // explicit: entries can mutate through shared ref
  }
  
  fn cache_user(cache: Cache, user: User) {
    let updated = cache.entries.get() with { [user.id]: user }
    cache.entries.set(updated)
    // Note: cache itself is immutable (let), but cache.entries internal can change
  }
  ```
- **Style guidance:** reach for interior mutability only when the alternative (passing `mut` through all code paths) would be dramatically worse. Prefer immutable updates with `with` for most state changes.
- **Versioning:** `Cell`, `Lazy`, `OnceCell` ship in v1.0. `Mutex` and thread-safe variants ship in v1.x with concurrency.

**Sub-decision 11.4: Aliasing rules for mut**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Exclusive access required (Rust-style)
  - (b) Shared aliasing allowed (Python-style)
  - (c) No aliasing problem because mutation is rare
- **Decision:** **Level-dependent — (b) shared allowed at app level (v1.0 single-threaded), (a) exclusive at systems level (v1.x), (a) exclusive at app level v1.x+ when concurrency lands**
- **Rationale:** The correct rule depends on the level:
  - **v1.0 app level (single-threaded green threads):** shared aliasing is safe because there's only one execution at a time. A `mut` value can be accessed through multiple references without races because the runtime serializes access by construction. This preserves ergonomics — closures can mutate shared state, observer patterns work naturally, no borrow-checker friction.
  - **v1.x systems level:** exclusive access per Rust NLL. Rust-equivalent semantics at systems level is where the performance comes from; aliasing-XOR-mutation is the invariant that enables ownership-based optimization.
  - **v1.x+ app level with CPU-parallel spawn:** the `mut` aliasing rule tightens. Values that might be touched by multiple threads must be wrapped in `Mutex<T>` or accessed through channels. The compiler analyzes which types escape to `spawn cpu` and enforces stricter rules on them.
- **Why not Rust's rule at app level in v1.0:** forcing aliasing-XOR-mutation on single-threaded app code is unnecessary friction. Most app-level mutation patterns (observer callbacks, counter updates, cache updates) are trivially safe in a single-threaded runtime and hostile to the LLM-first thesis if made to fight a borrow checker.
- **Example (v1.0 app level):**
  ```
  mut counter = 0
  let increment = () -> counter += 1    // captures counter by mut ref
  let reset = () -> counter = 0
  
  increment()                            // OK — single-threaded
  reset()
  increment()
  // counter is 1, multiple aliases all valid
  ```
- **Example (v1.x systems level — what WOULDN'T compile):**
  ```
  systems {
    mut buffer = Buffer.new(1024)
    let view1 = &mut buffer              // exclusive borrow taken
    let view2 = &mut buffer              // ERROR: buffer already mutably borrowed
  }
  ```
- **Migration path v1.0 → v1.x+:** when CPU parallelism lands, code that was safe in v1.0 (single-threaded) may need Mutex or channel wrapping to cross `spawn cpu` boundaries. Compiler errors at the boundaries will guide the upgrade; existing single-threaded code continues to work unchanged.

**Sub-decision 11.5: Deep vs shallow immutability**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Deep — `let` means the entire value tree is immutable
  - (b) Shallow — `let` only means the binding can't be reassigned
  - (c) Controlled by type (some types deeply immutable)
- **Decision:** **(a) Deep — `let` enforces full immutability of the value tree**
- **Rationale:** Shallow immutability (Rust's choice) creates confusion: "I declared let, but I can still modify the fields?" This inconsistency is a well-documented source of bugs and wrong mental models. Deep immutability matches programmer intent — if I say a value is immutable, every accessible path from it is immutable. Combined with structural sharing (9.16) and the `with` expression, this gives Clojure-level reasoning clarity without Clojure's dynamic-typing cost.
- **Rule:** for a value bound with `let`:
  - The binding cannot be rebound
  - Fields cannot be mutated: `user.name = "bob"` is an error
  - Nested fields cannot be mutated: `user.address.city = "NYC"` is an error
  - Container contents cannot be mutated: `user.posts.push(p)` is an error
  - Interior-mutable wrappers (`Cell<T>`, `Mutex<T>`) are the documented exception — their explicit type declares the mutation intent
- **For mutation:** declare the binding with `mut` and mutation is allowed at any depth. The `mut` is on the binding; deep mutation follows.
- **Example:**
  ```
  let user = User {
    name: "alice",
    address: { street: "1 Main", city: "SF" },
    posts: [],
  }
  
  user.name = "bob"              // ERROR: binding is immutable
  user.address.city = "NYC"      // ERROR: deep immutability
  user.posts.push(new_post)      // ERROR: deep immutability
  
  // Correct approach: structural update via `with`
  let updated = user with {
    address: user.address with { city: "NYC" },
    posts: user.posts.concat([new_post]),
  }
  
  // Or, if mutation is actually wanted:
  mut user = User { ... }
  user.address.city = "NYC"      // OK — mut allows deep mutation
  user.posts.push(new_post)      // OK
  ```
- **Interaction with Cell/Mutex:** types with interior mutability explicitly opt out of deep immutability for their contents. `Cell<T>` in a `let` value can still be `.set()` — the wrapper's type tells you it can.
- **Interaction with sharing:** deep immutability makes aliasing safe by construction. If `let a = b` and both are deeply immutable, no mutation through either alias can affect the other. This is what enables structural sharing in `with` expressions to be safe.

---

### Axis 12: Error Handling

**Sub-decision 12.1: Primary error mechanism**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Exceptions (Java, Python)
  - (b) Result types (Rust, Swift)
  - (c) Multiple returns (Go)
  - (d) Error unions (Zig)
- **Decision:** **(b) Result types — `Result<T, E>`**
- **Rationale:** Errors as values; explicit, typed, composable. Exceptions add invisible control flow that defeats LLM reasoning, and their unwinding interacts badly with refcount operations (every frame unwound is potential double-decrement or leak). Multiple returns (Go) lose type information — `err` is too generic. Error unions (Zig) work but the union semantics are well-modeled as a Result with a union E. Result is the correct, type-safe, composable primitive that aligns with Avra's existing Option (10.1) for a unified "fallible result" story.
- **Construction:** `.ok(value)` and `.err(error)` variants; dot-contextual inference makes construction clean.
- **Example:**
  ```
  fn parse_int(s: string) -> Result<int, ParseError> {
    if s.is_empty() { return .err(ParseError.empty) }
    // ... parsing logic ...
    .ok(result)
  }
  ```

**Sub-decision 12.2: Error propagation syntax**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `?` operator for early return on Err
  - (b) `try` keyword
  - (c) Explicit match only
- **Decision:** **(a) `?` operator for early propagation**
- **Rationale:** Unified with Option's `?` (decision 10.5). The same propagation operator for both `T?` and `Result<T, E>` — the compiler picks the behavior based on the expression type. `try` keyword (Swift) is more visually heavy. Explicit match only forces verbose nesting. `?` is clean, familiar from Rust, and now has extended semantics (see 12.10 for propagation context accumulation).
- **Semantics:** `expr?` on a `Result<T, E>` unwraps to `T` on `.ok`, early-returns the `.err` from the enclosing function. Enclosing function must return a `Result<_, E>` (or a `Result<_, E | ...>` where E is a member of the union).
- **Example:**
  ```
  fn load_config(path: string) -> Result<Config, AppError> {
    let text = read_file(path)?        // propagates IoError
    let parsed = parse_json(text)?      // propagates ParseError
    let valid = validate(parsed)?       // propagates ValidationError
    .ok(valid)
  }
  ```
- **Context accumulation (see 12.10):** each `?` propagation automatically appends propagation-site info to the error's trace — free stack-trace-lite.

**Sub-decision 12.3: Error type unification**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Common error enum with `From` impls (Rust)
  - (b) Trait-based erasure (Rust's anyhow)
  - (c) Union error types (`Result<T, IoError | ParseError>`)
  - (d) Inferred error type (`Result<T, _>`)
- **Decision:** **(c) Union error types with automatic widening at the `?` propagation site**
- **Rationale:** This is the **genuinely novel** part of Avra's error model. Rust's `From` impls are substantial boilerplate. `anyhow`-style erasure loses type information and makes programmatic handling painful. Avra uses union error types as a first-class concept — `Result<T, IoError | ParseError>` means "returns T, might fail with IoError or ParseError."
- **Automatic widening:** when a function calls sub-operations with different error types, the compiler widens the enclosing function's error union automatically:
  ```
  fn load_and_parse(path: string) -> Result<Config, IoError | ParseError> {
    let text = read_file(path)?        // read_file returns Result<string, IoError>
    let parsed = parse_json(text)?      // parse_json returns Result<Json, ParseError>
    .ok(validate(parsed))
  }
  // No From impls. No anyhow. The union widens automatically to include both.
  ```
- **Consumers pattern-match or narrow:**
  ```
  match load_and_parse("config.json") {
    .ok(config) -> use_config(config)
    .err(IoError(e)) -> fall_back_to_defaults(e)      // narrowed to IoError
    .err(ParseError(e)) -> report_bad_config(e)       // narrowed to ParseError
  }
  ```
- **Relationship to discriminated unions (8.4):** error unions ARE discriminated unions — each member has a distinguishable tag (the nominal type identity). Exhaustive matching works; adding a new error variant to the union is a breaking change that surfaces at call sites.
- **Shrinking with `?` on a larger union:** the enclosing function's error type must be a superset of the called function's. Compiler handles the arithmetic.
- **Narrowing with catch (12.5):** catch blocks can catch specific variants and "remove" them from the union of the enclosing result.
- **Why this is better than Rust's model:** no `thiserror` boilerplate, no manual `From` derivation, no `Box<dyn Error>` erasure. The type system does the work.

**Sub-decision 12.4: Error types — nominal or structural**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Nominal (`type IoError = ...`) — identity matters
  - (b) Structural (`shape Error = { message: string }`) — shape matters
  - (c) User chooses per type
- **Decision:** **(a) Nominal with shared `Error` trait**
- **Rationale:** Errors need stable identity for programmatic matching and for the discriminated-union error types (12.3) to work. Structural errors would make `match err { .ok(v) -> ..., .err(IoError(e)) -> ... }` impossible — the compiler couldn't distinguish `IoError` from `ParseError` if they had the same shape. Nominal identity also enables `@derive Error` to generate appropriate `kind()` values from the type name.
- **All errors implement the `Error` trait (see 12.10).** This provides a uniform interface for display, structured context, source chains, and presentation — regardless of the specific error type.
- **Example:**
  ```
  type IoError = enum {
    not_found(path: string)
    permission_denied(path: string)
    disk_full
  }
  
  @derive Error
  impl IoError {
    // automatic: message(), kind() = "io.not_found" etc.
  }
  
  type ParseError = enum {
    unexpected_token(found: string, expected: string, line: int)
    unterminated_string(line: int)
  }
  
  @derive Error
  impl ParseError { }
  ```

**Sub-decision 12.5: Inline error handling — catch**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `catch { default }` block after expression
  - (b) `expr orelse default` (Zig)
  - (c) Match only
- **Decision:** **(a) `catch { ... }` block after fallible expression**
- **Rationale:** The common case is "try this; if it fails, compute a default or take recovery action." Match is too verbose for this common case; `orelse` (Zig) only handles the trivial default case without recovery logic. `catch { }` provides a scoped handler that can include arbitrary recovery code.
- **Syntax:** `let value = fallible_expr() catch { recovery_expr }` — if the expression is `.ok(v)`, `value = v`. If `.err(e)`, the catch block runs and its value is bound to `value`.
- **Two forms (see 12.6):** with and without binding the error value.
- **Example:**
  ```
  // Simple default
  let port = parse_int(env_var) catch { 8080 }
  
  // With recovery logic
  let config = load_config(path) catch { 
    log.warn("using defaults")
    Config.default()
  }
  
  // With error binding (see 12.6)
  let config = load_config(path) catch (e) {
    log.error(`config load failed: ${e.message()}`)
    panic("cannot continue without config")
  }
  ```
- **Union narrowing:** catch can narrow the error union. If you catch only one variant, the remaining variants must still be handled:
  ```
  let data = fetch(url) catch (e: NetworkError) {
    // only NetworkError caught; ParseError still propagates if union was NetworkError | ParseError
    cached_data()
  }
  ```

**Sub-decision 12.6: catch binding syntax**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Implicit `it` — `catch { log(it) }`
  - (b) Explicit `(e)` — `catch (e) { log(e) }`
  - (c) Both — `catch` for no binding, `catch (e)` for binding
- **Decision:** **(c) Both forms — `catch { }` without binding, `catch (e) { }` with explicit binding**
- **Rationale:** Errors deserve an explicit name when referenced. Implicit `it` works fine for closure parameters (which are inline, unambiguous) but error values often need to be logged, reported, wrapped, or otherwise manipulated — an explicit name improves readability. When the error doesn't need to be referenced, the no-binding form is cleaner.
- **Examples:**
  ```
  // No binding — just provide default
  let port = parse_int(s) catch { 8080 }
  
  // Explicit binding — inspect or log
  let config = load(path) catch (e) {
    log.warn(`load failed: ${e.message()}`, context: e.context())
    Config.default()
  }
  
  // Typed binding for narrowing
  let data = fetch(url) catch (e: NetworkError) {
    if e.is_transient() { retry() } else { panic("unrecoverable") }
  }
  ```
- **Naming convention:** use `e` by default for the error; longer names (`err`, `error`, `parse_err`) when clarity warrants.

**Sub-decision 12.7: Scope cleanup — defer**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `defer` only
  - (b) `defer` + `errdefer` (Zig-style, only runs on error)
  - (c) Drop trait only (type-level cleanup)
  - (d) All three
- **Decision:** **(d) All three — Drop, defer, and errdefer compose under unified LIFO ordering**
- **Rationale:** Each serves a distinct purpose:
  - **Drop (type-level, primary):** cleanup that travels with a type. Files close, locks release, connections return to pool. Programmers never forget cleanup because the type carries it. Decided in 9.11.
  - **defer (block-level, always runs):** ad-hoc cleanup at scope exit regardless of path. Logging that a block ran, restoring globals, releasing ad-hoc resources. Always runs on both success and error paths.
  - **errdefer (block-level, error-only):** rollback logic. Only runs when the scope exits via error (propagation or panic). Crucial for transactional patterns: commit on success, rollback on failure.
- **Ordering (from 9.11):** all three obey LIFO at scope exit. Drop operations, defer blocks, and errdefer blocks (if the scope exits via error) all interleave in reverse declaration order.
- **Example:**
  ```
  fn transfer_funds(from: UUID, to: UUID, amount: Money) -> Result<Void, TransferError> {
    let tx = db.begin_transaction()?                 // Drop: auto-rollback on error via Drop
    defer { log.info(`transfer attempt for ${from} -> ${to}`) }
    errdefer { metrics.incr("transfers.failed") }    // only on error
    
    let sender = db.lock_account(from)?              // Drop releases lock
    let receiver = db.lock_account(to)?
    
    sender.debit(amount)?
    receiver.credit(amount)?
    tx.commit()?                                      // committing prevents auto-rollback
    
    .ok(void)
    // On success: log runs, locks release (Drop reverse order), tx already committed
    // On error:   errdefer increments metrics, log runs, locks release, tx auto-rolls back
  }
  ```
- **Interaction:** `errdefer` runs only when the scope exits via `?` propagation, explicit `.err(...)` return, or panic. Normal `.ok(...)` return skips errdefer blocks.
- **Why both defer and errdefer:** always-run cleanup is common (logging, metrics). Error-only cleanup is also common (rollback, cleanup of partial work). Having both means rollback logic doesn't need `if last_result.is_err() { ... }` checks.

**Sub-decision 12.8: Panic behavior**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Always fatal — no recovery
  - (b) Catch at spawn boundary (spawned task panic becomes error to parent)
  - (c) Catch anywhere
- **Decision:** **(b) Catch at spawn boundary only — panics kill the current task; parent task receives a panic-as-error**
- **Rationale:** Panics are for "this should never happen" bugs — unreachable code, invariant violations, programming errors. They are NOT for expected failures (use Result). Catch-anywhere (c) recreates exception semantics through the back door and defeats the value proposition of explicit error types. Always-fatal (a) is too restrictive — crashing the entire program because one background task hit a bug is bad server behavior.
- **Semantics:**
  - Panic in synchronous code propagates up the stack, running Drop/defer/errdefer along the way
  - Panic at task scope (inside `spawn { }` body) bounds at the spawn — parent task receives a `TaskPanic` error (containing the panic message and trace), can decide whether to escalate
  - Panic with no task boundary above (main task or top-level) aborts the program
- **Example:**
  ```
  spawn {
    let x = might_panic()      // if this panics...
    process(x)
  }
  // ...parent task observes .err(TaskPanic(msg, trace)), not a crash
  
  match task.await() {
    .ok(result) -> use_result(result)
    .err(TaskPanic(msg, _)) -> log.error(`task crashed: ${msg}`)
  }
  ```
- **Panic vs Result:** clear guidance in CLAUDE.md: use Result for anything a caller might reasonably recover from or need to inspect. Use panic for invariants the code itself guarantees — array out-of-bounds after a length check, unreachable match arms, assertion failures.
- **`unreachable` keyword:** compiler intrinsic that panics with a specific message. Used to mark match arms and code paths the programmer asserts can never execute.

**Sub-decision 12.9: `?` in closures**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Returns from closure
  - (b) Returns from enclosing function
  - (c) Compile error — require explicit handling
- **Decision:** **(a) `?` in a closure returns from the closure, not the enclosing function**
- **Rationale:** Closures are their own scope boundaries with their own return type. `?` respecting the closure boundary matches how programmers read closure code — locally. Having `?` leak to the enclosing function would make closures' error behavior surprising and non-local. If the closure's return type is compatible with propagation, `?` works; if not, it's a type error.
- **Example:**
  ```
  let parse_numbers = (inputs: List<string>) -> List<Result<int, ParseError>> {
    inputs.map(s -> {
      let n = parse_int(s)?    // early-returns from this closure
      .ok(n * 2)                // produces Result<int, ParseError>
    })
  }
  
  // The closure itself returns Result<int, ParseError>
  // The outer function returns List<Result<int, ParseError>>
  // `?` propagates only to the closure boundary
  ```
- **Closure return type inference:** when a closure uses `?`, its inferred return type becomes a Result type. If that's incompatible with the call site (e.g., `.map` expecting `(T) -> U`), the compiler produces a helpful error.
- **For propagating through:** if the programmer wants the error to flow to the enclosing function, they should collect the closure's Results and iterate:
  ```
  let results = inputs.map(s -> parse_int(s))    // List<Result<int, ParseError>>
  for r in results {
    let n = r?                                    // propagates to enclosing fn
    process(n)
  }
  ```

**Sub-decision 12.10: Error trait requirements**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Any type works as E
  - (b) Must implement Error trait (Display + source chain)
  - (c) Display only
- **Decision:** **(b) Must implement `Error` trait — significantly expanded beyond minimal Display + source to make errors world-class**
- **Rationale:** Errors in most languages are designed as "something bad happened with a message." Avra errors are designed as **first-class structured data** that LLMs and humans can reason about, recover from, present appropriately, and trace through causal chains. The `Error` trait defines a rich interface; `@derive Error` provides sensible defaults for all of it.

#### Mandatory trait members

**`message() -> string`** — human-readable summary, one line. For CLI output, logs, quick inspection.

**`kind() -> string`** — stable hierarchical identifier like `"io.not_found"` or `"parse.unexpected_token"`. Dotted namespacing enables hierarchical matching (`kind.starts_with("io.")`) without building class hierarchies. This is the **killer feature** for programmatic error handling — provider-agnostic categorization that LLMs and tools can reason about.

#### Optional members with defaults

**`cause() -> Error?`** — the underlying error that triggered this one. Default: `.none`. Override when wrapping another error to preserve causal chain.

**`context() -> Map<string, Value>`** — structured key-value metadata. `{ "path": "/tmp/x", "expected_type": "json" }`. Queryable, loggable, serializable. Default: empty map. Adding context is a method (`err.with("key", value)`) that composes through `?` propagation.

**`call_site() -> Location?`** — file + line + function where the error was created. **Auto-populated by the compiler** at `.err(...)` construction sites. Free stack-trace-lite without manual plumbing.

**`trace() -> List<Location>`** — accumulated propagation trace. **Every `?` propagation appends the propagation site.** Rich debuggability without exception-style unwinding. Default: just the call_site.

**`severity() -> Severity`** — `.debug`, `.info`, `.warning`, `.error`, `.fatal`. Guides presentation and logging. Default: `.error`.

**`is_transient() -> bool`** — can this operation be retried? Default: `false`. Enables generic retry logic (`retry_if_transient(fn)`) without per-error-type plumbing.
