**`suggestions() -> List<string>`** — recovery advice for humans and LLMs. For `FileNotFound`, defaults include `["check if the path is correct", "check file permissions"]`. For custom errors, the programmer provides suggestions. Invaluable for LLM error recovery.

#### Presentation

**`format(style: FormatStyle) -> string`** — render for a specific context. Styles:
- `.compact` — one line, CLI-friendly: `"io.not_found: /tmp/x: file not found"`
- `.pretty` — multi-line with source chain, for interactive terminals
- `.json` — structured, for log aggregators and APIs
- `.markdown` — for documentation and agent inspection

Default implementations cover all styles using the other trait methods. Types can override for custom presentation.

#### Three genuinely novel features worth highlighting in the TRD

**1. Propagation accumulates context automatically.** The `?` operator is compiler-instrumented to append propagation-site info to `trace()` and preserve all context. When `read_file` fails deep in a call chain, the final error naturally carries the complete propagation story with zero manual plumbing — no `.context("reading config")` chains anywhere.

**2. Hierarchical `kind` strings.** Dotted identifiers like `"io.not_found"`, `"parse.unexpected_token.identifier"`, `"net.timeout"` enable prefix-matching patterns that work across unrelated error types from different providers. No inheritance hierarchies, no `anyhow`-style erasure, just data.

**3. Structured `context()` queryable by tools.** Error data lives in a Map, not in prose. LLMs inspecting errors see `{ "path": "/tmp/x", "kind": "io.not_found" }` and know exactly what to try next. Logging systems can index and aggregate. Agent loops can make informed retry decisions.

#### `@derive Error` generates everything

```
@derive Error
type IoError = enum {
  not_found(path: string)
  permission_denied(path: string)
  disk_full
}

// Generated automatically:
// - message() derived from variant name and fields
// - kind() = "io.not_found", "io.permission_denied", "io.disk_full"
// - call_site() populated at .err(...) construction
// - trace() auto-accumulates via ? propagation
// - suggestions() derived from variant name (best-effort defaults)
// - severity() defaults to .error
// - format() default implementations for all styles
```

Programmers can override any method on their type; defaults are a starting point, not a constraint.

#### Context accumulation example

```
fn process_file(path: string) -> Result<Data, AppError> {
  let text = read_file(path)?      // context auto-accumulates { "path": path }
  let parsed = parse_json(text)?    // context auto-accumulates { "operation": "parse_json" }
  let valid = validate(parsed)?     // context auto-accumulates validation info
  .ok(valid)
}

// If read_file fails, the final error has:
// {
//   kind: "io.not_found",
//   message: "file not found",
//   context: { "path": "/tmp/config.json" },
//   trace: [
//     { fn: "read_file",     at: "std/fs.av:18" },
//     { fn: "process_file",  at: "app.av:42" }
//   ],
//   suggestions: [
//     "check if the path is correct",
//     "check file permissions"
//   ]
// }
```

No manual `.context()` chains. The `?` operator does it. The trace is free. LLMs see structured data, not prose.

**Sub-decision 12.11: attempt/fallback/retry — language or library**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Core syntax
  - (b) Standard library (`@std/core`)
  - (c) Third-party provider
- **Decision:** **(b) Standard library functions in `@std/core`**
- **Rationale:** Retry, timeout, fallback, and similar patterns are general enough to deserve stdlib support but not core-syntax-level. Baking them into the language (a) overcommits to specific semantics; third-party (c) fragments the ecosystem. Stdlib gives a blessed-but-replaceable implementation.
- **Provided in `@std/core`:**
  ```
  fn retry<T, E>(attempts: int, op: () -> Result<T, E>) -> Result<T, E>
    where E: Error  // uses E.is_transient() to decide whether to retry
  
  fn retry_with_backoff<T, E>(
    attempts: int, 
    base_delay: Duration, 
    op: () -> Result<T, E>
  ) -> Result<T, E>
  
  fn with_timeout<T, E>(
    timeout: Duration, 
    op: () -> Result<T, E>
  ) -> Result<T, TimeoutError | E>
  
  fn fallback<T, E1, E2>(
    primary: () -> Result<T, E1>, 
    secondary: () -> Result<T, E2>
  ) -> Result<T, E2>   // tries primary, falls back to secondary if primary fails
  ```
- **Example:**
  ```
  use @std/core.{retry, with_timeout, fallback}
  
  let data = retry(3, () -> fetch_data(url))?       // retry if is_transient
  let result = with_timeout(5.seconds, () -> heavy_computation())?
  let value = fallback(() -> primary_source(), () -> cache_lookup())?
  ```
- **Error trait integration:** these functions leverage `is_transient()` (from 12.10) to make intelligent retry decisions without per-error-type configuration.

**Sub-decision 12.12: FFI error mapping**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Manual wrapping — user writes the conversion
  - (b) Annotation-based: `extern fn foo(...) -> int @error_if(it < 0, IoError)`
  - (c) Provider-declared mapping
- **Decision:** **(b) Annotation-based — FFI signatures declare error conversion inline**
- **Rationale:** Manual wrapping (a) requires every FFI binding to write boilerplate error conversion — fine for one-off, terrible for wrapping large C APIs. Provider-declared mapping (c) separates the error conversion from the function signature, which hurts local reasoning. Annotation-based (b) puts the error semantics right next to the function signature where they belong, and the compiler generates the Result-returning wrapper.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  extern fn open(path: CString, flags: int) -> int 
    @error_if(it < 0, IoError.from_errno)
  
  // Compiler generates wrapper:
  // fn open(path: CString, flags: int) -> Result<int, IoError> {
  //   let result = extern_open(path, flags)
  //   if result < 0 { .err(IoError.from_errno(errno())) } 
  //   else { .ok(result) }
  // }
  ```
- **Annotation variations:**
  - `@error_if(condition, error_constructor)` — single-condition conversion
  - `@error_map(fn)` — arbitrary mapping function applied to the return value
  - `@error_null(ErrorType)` — shorthand for pointer-returning APIs that use NULL for errors
- **Versioning:** ships when FFI lands (v2.0). The `@error_if` annotation namespace is reserved in v1.0; using it errors with "FFI reserved for future use."
- **Why this matters for wrapping large C APIs:** wrapping something like libcurl or libxml requires hundreds of FFI bindings. With annotation-based error conversion, each becomes a single line with the error semantics visible. Without it, each would require a hand-written wrapper — days of work to wrap a medium-sized library.

---

### Axis 13: Effect Tracking

**Sub-decision 13.1: General effect system**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None — only errors tracked (current)
  - (b) Async coloring
  - (c) Capability types as values passed to functions
  - (d) Full effect polymorphism (Koka-style)
- **Decision:** **(a) None — errors are the only first-class tracked effect**
- **Rationale:** Full effect polymorphism (Koka-style) is research-grade and ergonomically hostile — especially for LLMs, which struggle with effect rows. Async coloring (b) creates the function-coloring problem (Bob Nystrom's "What Color Is Your Function") that every modern language is trying to escape. Capability types as values (c) have a place for security boundaries but not as general effect tracking. Errors handled via Result types (axis 12) cover 90% of what effect systems provide, with dramatically better ergonomics.
- **What IS tracked (cross-reference):**
  - **Errors** → Result types with union error widening (12.3)
  - **Async** → not coloring, implicit via green-thread spawn (9.4)
  - **Capabilities** → provider manifest declarations, compile-time verified (13.2)
  - **Purity** → opt-in `@pure` annotation (13.3)
  - **Non-determinism** → not tracked; pragmatic choice (13.5)
- **Non-goal:** full effect polymorphism. Code should read like it does today in Python/TypeScript, with Result types as the single extra rigor. Trying to track every effect at the type level defeats P3 (zero ceremony).

**Sub-decision 13.2: Provider capability enforcement**

- **Options:**
  - (a) TOML declarations only, advisory
  - (b) TOML declarations verified for Avra code, WASM sandbox for native
  - (c) Full capability types at the provider boundary
- **Decision:** **(b) Manifest declarations verified for Avra-code providers; sandbox for native-code providers**
- **Rationale:** Advisory declarations (a) are security theater — nothing enforces them. Capability types at the boundary (c) require pervasive type-system changes that conflict with decision 13.1. (b) provides real enforcement via two complementary mechanisms: Avra-code providers are checked by the compiler (static analysis of their imports and operations against their declared capabilities), native-code providers run in a WASM sandbox that enforces declared capabilities at runtime.
- **Manifest format (in `forge.toml` for each provider):**
  ```toml
  [provider]
  name = "http-client"
  version = "1.0.0"
  
  [capabilities]
  network = ["outbound"]           # outbound network calls
  filesystem = []                  # no filesystem access
  env = ["read:HOST", "read:PORT"] # specific env vars only
  process = []                     # no subprocess spawning
  ```
- **Enforcement:**
  - **Avra-code providers:** compiler statically verifies the code only uses capabilities declared in the manifest. A provider declaring `network = ["outbound"]` cannot import `@std/fs`. Compile error on violation.
  - **Native-code providers (C/Rust/etc):** loaded into a WASM sandbox with only the declared capabilities exposed. Runtime violation = trap.
- **Capability taxonomy:** `network` (in/out), `filesystem` (read/write/paths), `env` (specific vars), `process` (spawn/signal), `clock`, `random`, `unsafe-ffi`, etc. Specific taxonomy to be detailed in axis 17 (Provider System).
- **User-facing:** users see what each provider can do when installing. Deploy artifacts auto-generate minimum-permission manifests (see 13.6).
- **Versioning:** v1.0 ships manifest declarations with compile-time verification for Avra-code providers. WASM sandbox for native providers ships in v2.0 with the broader FFI system.

**Sub-decision 13.3: Purity annotations**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None
  - (b) `@pure` opt-in — compiler verifies no IO/mutation/non-determinism
  - (c) Pure by default, `@impure` opt-out
- **Decision:** **(b) `@pure` opt-in, compiler-verified**
- **Rationale:** No tracking (a) is a missed opportunity — memoization, UI rendering, and deterministic testing all benefit from knowing a function is pure. Pure-by-default (c) is too restrictive for a general-purpose language where most functions do I/O. Opt-in `@pure` provides the benefit for functions that warrant it without ceremony for everything else.
- **Semantics:** `@pure fn foo(x: int) -> int` tells the compiler this function is pure. The compiler verifies:
  - No I/O operations (file, network, stdout, etc.)
  - No mutation of external state (globals, interior-mut cells)
  - No calls to non-pure functions
  - No access to clock, random, or other non-deterministic sources
  - All arguments are consumed by value or by immutable reference
- **Example:**
  ```
  @pure
  fn distance(p1: Point, p2: Point) -> float {
    let dx = p2.x - p1.x
    let dy = p2.y - p1.y
    sqrt(dx * dx + dy * dy)
  }
  
  @pure
  fn log_distance(p1: Point, p2: Point) -> float {
    println("computing")          // ERROR: println is impure
    distance(p1, p2)
  }
  ```
- **Uses:**
  - **Memoization:** `@memo` annotation requires a `@pure` function (safety guarantee)
  - **UI rendering:** render functions are `@pure` to guarantee reactivity works correctly
  - **Testing:** `@pure` functions have trivially reproducible tests
  - **Optimization:** the compiler can constant-fold or reorder `@pure` calls aggressively
- **Transitivity:** `@pure` is transitive — a `@pure` function can only call other `@pure` functions. The compiler propagates purity through the call graph.
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0. Simple static analysis; no runtime cost.

**Sub-decision 13.4: Async coloring**

- **Options:**
  - (a) No coloring — implicit async (Go-style green threads)
  - (b) async/await with coloring
  - (c) Monadic async
- **Decision:** **(a) No coloring — implicit concurrency via green threads**
- **Rationale:** The function-coloring problem (Bob Nystrom's famous essay "What Color Is Your Function?") has haunted Rust, JavaScript, C#, Python, and others — dividing all code into "async" and "sync" with painful interop. Go's green-thread model proves this is unnecessary — every function can be called from any concurrent context, with I/O cooperatively yielding the task. This is what decision 9.4 already committed: single-threaded runtime with green-thread `spawn` for I/O concurrency in v1.0, evolving to multi-threaded in v1.x.
- **What this means for programmers (including LLMs):**
  - No `async` keyword. No `await` keyword.
  - `spawn { ... }` creates a task; it runs concurrently with the current task
  - I/O operations (file read, network request, database query) are regular function calls
  - The runtime automatically yields at I/O calls so other tasks can run
  - Code looks synchronous, executes concurrently
- **Example:**
  ```
  fn fetch_user_profile(id: UUID) -> UserProfile {
    // Looks synchronous — executes with non-blocking I/O
    let user = db.find_user(id)              // yields while DB responds
    let posts = db.find_posts_by(user.id)    // yields while DB responds
    let friends = social.friends_of(user.id) // yields while API responds
    
    UserProfile { user, posts, friends }
  }
  
  // Parallelism via spawn:
  fn fetch_multiple(ids: List<UUID>) -> List<UserProfile> {
    let tasks = ids.map(id -> spawn { fetch_user_profile(id) })
    tasks.map(t -> t.await())                 // wait for all concurrent tasks
  }
  ```
- **Related decisions:**
  - Spawn semantics (9.4): single-threaded green-threading v1.0, CPU-parallel `spawn cpu` in v1.x
  - Level crossings (9.14): closures capturing borrows can't cross spawn boundaries — forces ownership
  - Aliasing (11.4): v1.0 allows shared mut (safe in single-threaded), v1.x+ tightens for multi-threaded

**Sub-decision 13.5: Non-determinism tracking**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not tracked — clock/random are plain functions
  - (b) Capabilities passed explicitly (Clock, RNG as values)
  - (c) Annotations (`@nondeterministic`)
- **Decision:** **(a) Not tracked at the type level; deterministic alternatives provided via dependency injection patterns**
- **Rationale:** Tracking non-determinism as a type-level effect is theoretically clean (Haskell does it through IO, Koka through effects) but adds significant annotation burden that conflicts with decision 13.1 (no general effect system). The practical need — making code deterministically testable — is better served by dependency injection patterns than type-system enforcement.
- **Provided mechanisms (not enforced):**
  - `Time.now()`, `Time.sleep(duration)` — real clock
  - `Random.int()`, `Random.float()`, `Random.shuffle(xs)` — real RNG
  - For testing: `TestClock`, `TestRandom` in `@std/test` — seeded/frozen versions passed where needed
  - Providers that need determinism (e.g., for replay) accept a Clock parameter in their capability declaration
- **Example (test pattern):**
  ```
  // Production code uses the global clock
  fn compute_expiry(ttl: Duration) -> Timestamp {
    Time.now() + ttl
  }
  
  // Testable version via dependency injection
  fn compute_expiry_with(clock: Clock, ttl: Duration) -> Timestamp {
    clock.now() + ttl
  }
  
  // In tests
  test "expiry is correct" {
    let frozen = TestClock.at(Timestamp.from_iso("2025-01-01T00:00:00Z"))
    let result = compute_expiry_with(frozen, 1.hour)
    assert result == Timestamp.from_iso("2025-01-01T01:00:00Z")
  }
  ```
- **Why not capability-passing (b):** threading a Clock through every function that might eventually need time is heavy ceremony. Most functions don't care about testability that deep. Dependency injection at the layer that needs it strikes the balance.
- **Why not annotations (c):** `@nondeterministic` as an annotation-transitive effect has the same problem as purity tracking — becomes pervasive once it enters the call graph. Opt-in without enforcement defeats the point.
- **Relation to `@pure` (13.3):** a `@pure` function cannot call `Time.now()` or `Random.int()` — the compiler rejects this as a purity violation. So non-determinism IS tracked indirectly through purity annotations where the programmer opts in.

**Sub-decision 13.6: Deploy-time permission extraction**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Conservative — grant all permissions
  - (b) Inferred from provider manifests declared in code
  - (c) Inferred from capability types
- **Decision:** **(b) Inferred from provider manifests in the import graph**
- **Rationale:** Conservative (a) is a security disaster — every deploy artifact would have maximum permissions. Capability types (c) require pervasive type-system changes that conflict with 13.1. Manifest inference (b) is the practical sweet spot: at deploy time the compiler walks the import graph, unions the capability declarations of every reachable provider, and emits a minimum-permission deploy manifest.
- **How it works:**
  1. Programmer writes `deploy` block in code (e.g., AWS Lambda, Fly.io, Kubernetes)
  2. Compiler traces the import graph of every function reachable from the deploy target
  3. For each imported provider, reads its manifest declaration of capabilities
  4. Unions all capabilities into a deploy-required-capabilities set
  5. Emits platform-specific deploy artifacts (IAM policy, Kubernetes RBAC, systemd unit, etc.) with exactly those capabilities
- **Example:**
  ```
  // Code imports:
  use @std/http_server    // needs: network.listen
  use @std/db/postgres    // needs: network.outbound:postgres
  use s3_provider          // needs: network.outbound:https, env.read:AWS_*
  
  deploy prod {
    target: aws_lambda
    runtime: avra_1
  }
  
  // Generated IAM policy (conceptual):
  // - lambda:* for the function itself
  // - network egress to database host
  // - network egress to S3 endpoints
  // - env vars AWS_REGION, AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID, etc.
  // NOT granted: filesystem write, process spawn, unsafe-ffi
  ```
- **Override mechanism:** if the programmer needs to add a capability not declared by any import (e.g., using a hatch-escape), they must declare it explicitly in the deploy block:
  ```
  deploy prod {
    target: aws_lambda
    additional_capabilities: [filesystem.write("/tmp/*")]  // explicit opt-in
  }
  ```
- **Security property:** a provider cannot quietly acquire more capabilities than declared in its manifest. The compile-time verification (13.2) plus deploy-time extraction gives end-to-end guarantee that deployed artifacts have minimum permissions.
- **Versioning:** inference ships when the deploy system ships (v1.x+). Core manifest declarations ship in v1.0.

---

## Part 4: Verification and Interop

The outer edges — how the compiler proves things, how Avra talks to other languages, how code is organized.

---

### Axis 14: Refinement and Dependent Types

**Sub-decision 14.1: Refinement types**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None
  - (b) Simple refinements (`type Positive = int where it > 0`)
  - (c) Rich refinements with SMT discharge
  - (d) Full liquid types
- **Decision:** **Staged — (a) for v1.0, (b) for v1.x, (c) for v2.0+; liquid types never**
- **Rationale:** Refinement types are valuable for expressing domain invariants (non-empty strings, positive integers, percentages in [0, 100]) and for bounds-safety guarantees without runtime checks. But the implementation complexity varies dramatically:
  - **Simple refinements (b)** — the compiler accepts `where` clauses and inserts runtime checks at construction sites. Easy to implement, clear runtime cost. Useful for domain modeling.
  - **Rich refinements with SMT (c)** — the compiler discharges refinement obligations statically via Z3. Eliminates runtime cost where provable. Significant implementation investment (paper-level research plus engineering).
  - **Liquid types (d)** — inference of refinements, precisely tracked through the program. Research-grade, hard for LLMs to reason about, hard to get right. Ruled out permanently.
- **Staged rollout:**
  - **v1.0:** no refinements. `where` keyword reserved. Programmers use wrapper types and constructors (`NonEmptyString.try_from(s)?`) to encode invariants.
  - **v1.x:** simple refinements with runtime checks. `type Age = int where it >= 0 && it < 150`. Construction checks `it` against the predicate; violation produces a structured `RefinementError`.
  - **v2.0:** SMT-backed static discharge. The compiler attempts to prove refinement obligations at compile time. When proof succeeds, no runtime check is inserted. When it fails, runtime check stays. Same user code works across the transition — performance improves transparently.
- **Example (v1.x form):**
  ```
  type NonEmptyString = string where it.length > 0
  type Percentage = float where 0.0 <= it && it <= 100.0
  type UserId = UUID where it.version == 4
  
  let name: NonEmptyString = NonEmptyString.try_from("alice")?
  let score: Percentage = 85.5.try_into()?
  ```
- **LLM-friendliness:** simple refinements read like docstrings. SMT discharge is invisible to the programmer — they write the same code, the compiler either proves the check statically or leaves it runtime. No annotation of proofs required.

**Sub-decision 14.2: Contract annotations**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None
  - (b) `@requires` / `@ensures` with runtime checks
  - (c) `@requires` / `@ensures` with SMT static discharge (Z3)
- **Decision:** **Staged — (b) runtime checks in v1.x, (c) SMT discharge in v2.0+**
- **Rationale:** Contract annotations provide function-level pre/postconditions that complement refinement types. A function can declare "I require this about my arguments and guarantee this about my result." Starting with runtime checks (b) gets the ergonomic surface shipped; upgrading to SMT discharge (c) is transparent optimization.
- **Staged rollout:**
  - **v1.0:** reserved. Annotations parse but error with "reserved for future use."
  - **v1.x:** `@requires` and `@ensures` with runtime checks. Failed precondition in debug builds → panic with diagnostic; in release builds → configurable (elided, logged, or panic).
  - **v2.0+:** SMT-backed discharge via Z3. Compiler tries to prove `@requires` is satisfied at every call site and `@ensures` is satisfied at every return. Proven conditions generate no runtime code. Unproven conditions fall back to runtime checks with a compile-time warning so programmers can see what the prover couldn't handle.
- **Example:**
  ```
  @requires(divisor != 0)
  @ensures(it * divisor == dividend)
  fn divide(dividend: int, divisor: int) -> int {
    dividend / divisor
  }
  
  let x = divide(10, 2)         // OK
  let y = divide(10, 0)         // v1.x debug: panic; v2.0: compile error if prover catches
  ```
- **The `it` pronoun in `@ensures`** refers to the function's return value — consistent with Avra's `it` conventions elsewhere.
- **Interaction with refinement types (14.1):** refinements are type-level predicates; contracts are function-level predicates. Both discharge via the same SMT backend in v2.0+. A function taking `Positive` and returning `Positive` has its refinement checks replaced by contract checks at the call boundary; the compiler optimizes the composition.
- **Connection to Z's prior learnings:** this is exactly the "crystallization stack" Z sketched — the language handles levels 3-4 (implementation contracts and proved correctness) while surrounding workspace handles 1-2 (goals and specs). Contracts bridge the two.

**Sub-decision 14.3: Dependent types**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None — incompatible with accessibility
  - (b) Limited (const generics for sizes)
  - (c) Full dependent types (Idris, Agda)
- **Decision:** **(a) None for full dependent types; (b) const generics over integers already covered in decision 5.5**
- **Rationale:** Full dependent types (c) are explicitly ruled out as incompatible with Avra's accessibility and LLM-first goals. The complexity of dependent-type-proofs — where types depend on runtime values — requires training and discipline far beyond what the language targets.
- **What we DO have:**
  - Const generics over integers (decision 5.5) — enables `Array<T, N>`, `Matrix<T, R, C>`, `BitSet<N>`. v1.x+.
  - Refinement types (14.1) — values constrained by predicates. v1.x simple, v2.0 SMT.
  - Contract annotations (14.2) — function-level pre/postconditions. v1.x runtime, v2.0 SMT.
- **What we explicitly rule out:**
  - Types indexed by arbitrary runtime values
  - Proof-carrying code (requiring programmers to write proofs)
  - Totality/termination as types (deferred to 14.4)
  - Curry-Howard correspondence at the type level
- **Why this matters:** Idris, Agda, Coq, and Lean are beautiful research languages. Their users are experts. Avra is designed for LLMs and for developers who want practical verification without a PhD in type theory. Refinements + contracts + SMT cover 95% of real correctness proofs that industry code needs, with far less conceptual load.

**Sub-decision 14.4: Totality checking**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not checked
  - (b) Opt-in per function (`@total`)
  - (c) Checked by default for `@pure` functions
- **Decision:** **(b) Opt-in via `@total`, deferred to v2.0+**
- **Rationale:** Totality (guaranteeing a function terminates on all inputs) is valuable for specific domains — reactive view rendering (must terminate to commit), SMT-discharged contracts (must terminate to be trusted), lifted/`@derive` functions (run at compile time, must terminate). Not valuable enough for universal default. Checking totality for all `@pure` functions (c) would over-constrain — some pure functions (e.g., iterative solvers, stream consumers) legitimately run indefinitely.
- **Semantics:** `@total fn foo() -> T` tells the compiler this function terminates on all inputs. Compiler attempts to verify by:
  - Checking recursive calls use strictly decreasing arguments
  - Checking loops have bounded iteration (verified by SMT in v2.0+)
  - Checking no calls to non-total functions
  - Rejecting unbounded recursion, infinite loops, or calls that can't be proved total
- **When compiler can't prove totality:**
  - Clear error pointing to the specific recursive call or loop
  - Programmer can provide a termination metric: `@total(decreases = size(xs))`
  - Escape hatch: `@total(unchecked)` for programmer-asserted totality (like `unsafe` — rare, documented)
- **Versioning:** reserved in v1.0. Implementation depends on SMT infrastructure (shared with contracts/refinements). Ships in v2.0+.
- **Use cases for `@total`:**
  - Reactive view functions — must terminate to commit frame
  - `@derive` macros — run at compile time, infinite loop = compiler hang
  - Contract-guaranteed functions — SMT-discharged contracts assume termination
  - Cryptographic invariants where hang = DoS vulnerability

---

### Axis 15: FFI and Foreign Type Modeling

**Sub-decision 15.1: FFI target languages**

- **Options:**
  - (a) C ABI only
  - (b) C ABI + runtime bridges (Go, JVM) + interpreter embedding (Python, JS)
  - (c) Avra-specific FFI protocol
- **Decision:** **(b) Tiered FFI — C ABI core, runtime bridges for Go/JVM, interpreter embedding for Python/JS**
- **Rationale:** Interop is a make-or-break feature for a new language. Limiting to C ABI only (a) excludes critical ecosystems (Python's numpy, Go's concurrency primitives, JVM's enterprise libraries). Avra-specific protocol (c) isolates Avra from the existing ecosystem and requires every library to be rewritten. Tiered approach (b) covers the full interop surface with appropriate mechanisms per target.
- **Tier 1 — C ABI (v1.x):**
  - Direct function calls through C calling convention
  - Trivially interoperates with C, Rust, Swift, Zig, any language exposing a C interface
  - Zero runtime overhead; calls are native
  - Visible costs (allocation, copy for strings/structs as needed)
- **Tier 2 — Runtime bridges (v2.0+):**
  - **Go bridge:** uses `cgo` exports; Avra binary embeds Go runtime or links against compiled Go shared library
  - **JVM bridge:** JNI-style, Avra↔JVM calls with explicit marshaling
  - Heavier runtime cost than C ABI; appropriate for large library dependencies, not hot paths
- **Tier 3 — Interpreter embedding (v2.1+):**
  - **Python:** CPython C API; Avra can call Python functions, receive Python objects as opaque handles
  - **JavaScript:** V8 / QuickJS embedding; similar opaque-handle model
  - Highest overhead; data crosses interpreter boundary. Used for scripting integration, notebook environments, ML workloads.
- **Visibility:** all FFI boundaries are annotated, their costs are documented, and they appear clearly in code so programmers (and LLMs) see when they're crossing language boundaries.
- **Versioning:** v1.0 reserves all FFI syntax; v1.x ships C ABI; v2.0+ adds higher tiers progressively based on user demand.

**Sub-decision 15.2: C type modeling**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Explicit C types (`c_int`, `c_char`)
  - (b) Automatic mapping to Avra types
  - (c) Both, user chooses
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — explicit C types available in `@std/c`, automatic mapping in `extern` declarations**
- **Rationale:** Explicit C types (a) are verbose and interrupt the language's otherwise-clean type surface. Pure automatic mapping (b) hides ABI details that sometimes matter (signedness edge cases, platform-dependent widths). Having both lets programmers pick the right tool: automatic mapping for the 95% case where `int32 ↔ int32_t` is fine, explicit when ABI details matter.
- **Automatic mapping in `extern` declarations:**
  ```
  extern fn strlen(s: CString) -> int
  // Compiler translates int → size_t for the C ABI
  
  extern fn add(a: i32, b: i32) -> i32
  // Explicit-size types map directly
  ```
- **Explicit C types from `@std/c`:**
  ```
  use @std/c.{c_int, c_long, c_size_t, CString}
  
  extern fn sqrtf(x: c_float) -> c_float
  extern fn read(fd: c_int, buf: *u8, count: c_size_t) -> c_ssize_t
  ```
- **Rules:**
  - Avra's `int` maps to platform pointer-sized int in `extern` declarations (usually i64)
  - `i32`, `i64`, `u8`, etc. map to fixed-width C types directly
  - `string` (Avra's heap-allocated string) requires explicit conversion to `CString` at the boundary
  - `CString` is null-terminated UTF-8, matches C's `const char*`
- **Versioning:** ships with the FFI system in v1.x (C ABI tier).

**Sub-decision 15.3: Callback / trampoline mechanism**

- **Options:**
  - (a) C function pointers only, no closure captures
  - (b) Compiler-generated trampolines for closures
  - (c) Runtime-generated trampolines (libffi)
- **Decision:** **(b) Compiler-generated trampolines for closures crossing to C ABI**
- **Rationale:** C function pointers only (a) is too restrictive — most useful C APIs take callbacks with user data (qsort, glfw, libcurl). Runtime-generated trampolines via libffi (c) add a heavy runtime dependency and JIT-like security concerns. Compiler-generated trampolines (b) hit the sweet spot: the compiler statically generates a tiny C-callable function per closure type that unpacks the user_data pointer and invokes the Avra closure.
- **How it works:**
  ```
  // Avra code:
  let threshold = 10
  c_sort(items, (a, b) -> a - b)       // closure captures nothing in this case
  c_sort(items, (a, b) -> (a % threshold) - (b % threshold))  // captures threshold
  ```
- **Compiler generates:**
  - A C-callable function with the C ABI signature
  - The closure's captures are boxed into a `user_data` struct
  - The trampoline unpacks `user_data`, calls the Avra closure, returns the result
  - The generated code follows the target C API's `void* user_data` convention
- **C APIs that don't support user_data:** closure capture is rejected with a compile error and clear guidance: "this C API's callback does not accept user data; closure cannot capture variables. Use a pure function or global state."
- **Memory safety:** trampolines hold the closure's captures alive for the duration of the C API's use. For synchronous callbacks this is the lexical scope of the call; for asynchronous (e.g., event loops) the programmer must manage lifetime explicitly (via `Rc<T>` or static lifetime).
- **Versioning:** ships with C ABI FFI in v1.x.

**Sub-decision 15.4: FFI memory ownership**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Unspecified — user handles
  - (b) Annotations on extern fns declaring ownership transfer
  - (c) Ownership inferred from signature
- **Decision:** **(b) Annotations — ownership declared explicitly per extern function**
- **Rationale:** Unspecified ownership (a) is a recipe for memory leaks and double-frees — exactly the bugs Avra is trying to prevent. Inference (c) cannot reliably determine ownership from C signatures alone (`int foo(char* s)` — does foo take ownership of `s`?). Explicit annotations (b) force programmers to document the contract, and the compiler generates the correct memory management.
- **Annotation vocabulary:**
  - `@takes_ownership(param)` — the C function takes ownership of the parameter; Avra does NOT free it
  - `@returns_ownership` — the C function returns a pointer Avra must eventually free
  - `@borrows(param)` — C function borrows the parameter for the duration of the call; Avra retains ownership
  - `@returns_borrowed(from: param)` — returned pointer's lifetime is tied to a parameter
  - `@free_with(fn_name)` — the C function for freeing a returned value (e.g., `@free_with(free)`, `@free_with(sqlite3_close)`)
- **Example:**
  ```
  // strdup: returns a new string caller must free
  extern fn strdup(s: CString) -> CString
    @returns_ownership
    @free_with(free)
  
  // strlen: borrows the string for the call
  extern fn strlen(s: CString) -> c_size_t
    @borrows(s)
  
  // sqlite3_open: returns a handle requiring cleanup
  extern fn sqlite3_open(path: CString, db: *mut *SQLite3) -> c_int
    @returns_ownership(db)
    @free_with(sqlite3_close)
  ```
- **Compiler behavior:**
  - `@returns_ownership` wraps the returned value in an Avra `Owned<T>` with the specified `@free_with` as drop
  - `@borrows` ensures the Avra value outlives the C call (compile error if not)
  - `@takes_ownership` moves the value and prevents further Avra use
- **Versioning:** ships with C ABI FFI in v1.x.

**Sub-decision 15.5: Opaque types across FFI**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Raw pointers (`*void`)
  - (b) Newtype-wrapped opaque handles
  - (c) Compiler-managed opaque types with Drop
- **Decision:** **(c) Compiler-managed opaque types with automatic Drop**
- **Rationale:** Raw pointers (a) lose all type information and make double-free trivial. Newtypes (b) give type safety but still require manual Drop management. Compiler-managed opaque types (c) combine type safety with automatic resource cleanup — the most appropriate for a language that already has automatic memory management.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  opaque type SQLite3 @free_with(sqlite3_close)
  opaque type FILE @free_with(fclose)
  opaque type CurlHandle @free_with(curl_easy_cleanup)
  ```
- **Semantics:**
  - `opaque type T` creates a type that Avra code cannot inspect — only hand to C functions
  - Values are always heap pointers (the compiler generates zero-size wrapper with the pointer)
  - Drop automatically calls the `@free_with` function when the value goes out of scope
  - Values can be borrowed, moved, stored in structs, or returned — all the normal Avra memory semantics apply
- **Example:**
  ```
  opaque type SQLite3 @free_with(sqlite3_close)
  
  extern fn sqlite3_open(path: CString, db: *mut *SQLite3) -> c_int
    @returns_ownership(db)
  
  extern fn sqlite3_prepare(db: *SQLite3, sql: CString, stmt: *mut *Statement) -> c_int
    @borrows(db)
    @returns_ownership(stmt)
  
  fn query_users(db_path: string) -> Result<List<User>, DbError> {
    let db_path_c = CString.from(db_path)
    let db = sqlite3_open_wrapped(db_path_c)?
    // db is an Avra value holding the opaque SQLite3 pointer
    // When this function returns, Drop calls sqlite3_close(db) automatically
    
    // ... use db ...
  }
  ```
- **Why this matters:** wrapping a library like SQLite or libcurl normally requires careful `defer` pairings throughout user code. With compiler-managed opaque types, the cleanup is guaranteed and the programmer cannot forget it.
- **Versioning:** ships with C ABI FFI in v1.x.

**Sub-decision 15.6: String encoding across FFI**

- **Options:**
  - (a) UTF-8 null-terminated C strings
  - (b) Pointer + length pairs
  - (c) Both, declared per extern
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — declared per extern function; `CString` default, `CStr` for explicit length**
- **Rationale:** C APIs use both conventions depending on the library era and purpose. Null-terminated strings (a) work for most traditional C APIs. Pointer+length pairs (b) are common in modern C APIs and for binary-safe strings. Avra provides both types; the extern declaration picks the appropriate one.
- **Types:**
  - **`CString`** — null-terminated UTF-8, matches `const char*` in traditional C APIs
  - **`CStr`** — pointer + length, matches `const char*, size_t` pairs in modern APIs
- **Conversion from Avra `string`:**
  - `CString.from(s)` — allocates a null-terminated copy; Avra retains ownership (auto-freed at scope end) unless passed with `@takes_ownership`
  - `CStr.from(s)` — creates a pointer+length view into the Avra string; no allocation; borrows for the call
- **Example:**
  ```
  extern fn strlen(s: CString) -> c_size_t
  extern fn memcmp(a: CStr, b: CStr) -> c_int
  
  let hello = "hello"
  let len = strlen(CString.from(hello))        // allocates, frees after
  let cmp = memcmp(CStr.from(hello), CStr.from("world"))  // no allocation
  ```
- **Encoding errors:** `string` is always valid UTF-8 (invariant from 9.15). `CString.from` fails only if the string contains embedded null bytes, in which case it returns an error.
- **Versioning:** ships with C ABI FFI in v1.x.

**Sub-decision 15.7: FFI error mapping**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Manual Result wrapping
  - (b) `@error_if` annotations on return values
  - (c) Provider-declared error mapping
- **Decision:** **(b) `@error_if` annotations — consistent with decision 12.12**
- **Rationale:** Duplicate of decision 12.12 from a different angle. Annotation-based error mapping is the right answer: C APIs have many error-signaling conventions (negative return, NULL pointer, out-parameter errno), and the annotation declares the convention per extern. Compiler generates the Result-returning wrapper automatically.
- **See full details in decision 12.12.** Key annotations:
  - `@error_if(condition, error_constructor)` — e.g., `@error_if(it < 0, IoError.from_errno)`
  - `@error_null(ErrorType)` — for NULL-returning APIs
  - `@error_map(fn)` — arbitrary mapping
- **Composition with other FFI annotations:** `@error_if` stacks with `@returns_ownership`, `@borrows`, etc. Compiler handles the composition correctly.
- **Example:**
  ```
  extern fn open(path: CString, flags: c_int) -> c_int
    @error_if(it < 0, IoError.from_errno)
  
  // Generated wrapper signature:
  // fn open(path: CString, flags: c_int) -> Result<c_int, IoError>
  ```
- **Versioning:** ships with C ABI FFI in v1.x.

---

### Axis 16: Module and Coherence Model

**Sub-decision 16.1: Module identity**

- **Options:**
  - (a) File path IS the module path
  - (b) Explicit module declarations in files
  - (c) Package-level modules (multiple files collapsed)
- **Decision:** **(a) File path IS the module path**
- **Rationale:** Simplest mental model, zero ceremony, zero ambiguity. The file `src/auth/login.av` exports the module `auth.login`. No separate declaration needed, nothing can drift between file location and module identity. LLMs cannot produce inconsistent file-to-module mappings because there's only one relationship. Explicit declarations (b) create the "which is right if they disagree?" problem. Package-level modules (c) make tooling and imports harder to predict.
- **Rules:**
  - File path relative to package root determines module path: `src/auth/login.av` → `auth.login`
  - Directory with `mod.av` treats the directory as the module: `src/auth/mod.av` → `auth`, `src/auth/login.av` → `auth.login`
  - Package name (from `forge.toml`) is implicitly prepended: `@myapp/auth.login` from outside the package
  - Hyphens in filenames are errors (use underscores); enforces valid identifiers
- **Examples:**
  ```
  // src/auth/login.av — module auth.login
  export fn attempt_login(email: string, password: string) -> Result<Session, AuthError> { ... }
  
  // src/main.av — module (package root)
  use auth.login.{attempt_login}
  ```
- **No `mod.av` means the directory has no "index"** — subpaths work but `import auth` would be an error; must be `import auth.login` or similar. Consistent with most module systems.

**Sub-decision 16.2: Visibility**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `export` keyword on declarations
  - (b) `pub` keyword (Rust)
  - (c) Convention-based (capitalization — Go)
  - (d) `expose { ... }` block declaring public surface
- **Decision:** **(a) `export` keyword at the declaration site**
- **Rationale:** Explicit at the declaration reads naturally and matches TypeScript/JavaScript conventions that LLMs are deeply familiar with. `pub` (Rust) works but is a less common word across languages. Convention-based (Go) is invisible and error-prone — a rename accidentally changes visibility. `expose { }` block separates declaration from visibility, which hurts local reasoning when reading a file.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  // Private by default
  fn helper(x: int) -> int { x + 1 }
  
  // Public via export
  export fn add(a: int, b: int) -> int { a + b }
  
  export type User = { name: string, email: string }
  
  export trait Validator { ... }
  
  // Re-export (see 16.7)
  export use some.module.{item}
  ```
- **Granularity:** `export` applies to `fn`, `type`, `shape`, `trait`, `enum`, `const`, `model`, `service`, and other top-level declarations.
- **Fields:** fields of exported types are public by default (shapes are structural anyway). For nominal types with private fields, use per-field visibility (see interaction with 3.7).

**Sub-decision 16.3: Import syntax**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `use path.{item, item as alias}`
  - (b) `import { item } from "path"`
  - (c) `from path import item`
- **Decision:** **(a) Rust-style `use` — `use path.{items}` and `use path`**
- **Rationale:** Rust-style `use` is terser than JavaScript's `import { } from ...`, reads naturally left-to-right, and matches the convention of a heavily-statically-typed systems language. The path-first ordering (source first, items second) pairs well with tree-like namespace layouts. LLMs are deeply familiar with this syntax from Rust's training data.
- **Forms:**
  ```
  use math                                   // whole module (access via math.add)
  use math as m                              // whole module with alias
  use math.{add, subtract}                   // named items
  use math.{add as plus}                     // with alias
  use math.{add, subtract, PI}               // multiple items
  use math.*                                 // glob (style-discouraged, used sparingly)
  use @std/http.{Http, Request, Response}    // from external package
  use @myapp/auth.{Session, User}            // nested paths
  use @std/collections.{
    List,
    Map,
    Set,
  }                                          // multi-line for readability
  ```
- **Path syntax:**
  - Dotted paths for local modules: `use auth.login.{attempt_login}`
  - `@package/module` for external packages: `use @std/http.{Http}`
  - No string quotes — paths are identifiers, not strings. Keeps refactoring tools working.
  - No `./` or `../` — all paths are absolute from the package root. Eliminates the JS footgun of paths breaking when files move.
- **Semantics:**
  - `use` statements go at the top of files
  - Shadowing an imported name is an error (avoids silent conflicts)
  - Unused `use` statements are a warning (not error — common during development)
- **Style:**
  - Multi-line braces preferred for 3+ items
  - Glob imports (`use x.*`) are allowed but linted — use sparingly
  - Group related `use` statements together

**Sub-decision 16.4: Package naming**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `@std/http`, `@community/auth` namespaced
  - (b) Flat names
  - (c) Git-URL based
- **Decision:** **(a) Namespaced with `@` prefix**
- **Rationale:** Namespacing solves multiple problems at once:
  - **Typosquatting protection:** `@anthropic/ai` can't be squatted by a random author; namespaces are owned
  - **Provenance clarity:** at a glance, `@std/http` is stdlib, `@anthropic/ai` is Anthropic-authored, `@myorg/internal` is a private namespace
  - **Avoids flat-namespace collisions:** npm's flat namespace means `leftpad` can only be one package forever
  - **Matches programmer familiarity:** npm scoped packages, GitHub org/repo notation, Rust's crate namespacing aspirations
- **Namespace categories:**
  - `@std/*` — official stdlib (reserved, curated by Avra maintainers)
  - `@anthropic/*` — Anthropic-authored packages (reserved)
  - `@community/*` — community-curated packages with some review process
  - `@<user>/<package>` — individual-authored (tied to registry account)
  - `@<org>/<package>` — organization-authored
  - `@local/*` — local development packages (not published)
- **Examples:**
  ```
  use @std/http.{Http}
  use @anthropic/ai.{generate}
  use @myorg/internal-tooling.{setup}
  use @local/dev_utils.{helpers}
  ```
- **Interaction with registry (16.8):** namespaces are reserved at registry signup; misuse or squatting can be reported and reclaimed.

**Sub-decision 16.5: Trait coherence — orphan rules**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Strict (Rust) — you own either the trait or the type
  - (b) Loose — any impl allowed
  - (c) Named instances (Haskell-style)
- **Decision:** **(a) Strict — orphan rule enforced (must own trait or type)**
- **Rationale:** The orphan rule guarantees there is exactly ONE impl of a given trait for a given type across the entire program. Loose rules (b) allow two crates to each provide their own impl, and when both are in the dependency graph the behavior is ambiguous — this is a class of bugs that's painful to debug. Named instances (Haskell-style) solve the problem but require users to specify which instance to use at every call site, which is poor ergonomics.
- **The rule:** to implement trait `T` for type `U`, you must own either `T` or `U` (or both). If both come from other packages, you cannot provide a new impl.
- **Workaround (newtype pattern):**
  ```
  // Can't: impl OtherTrait for OtherType
  // Because we don't own either
  
  // Workaround: wrap in a local newtype
  type MyWrapper = OtherType
  impl OtherTrait for MyWrapper { ... }      // legal, we own MyWrapper
  ```
- **Impact on ecosystem:** this forces the ecosystem to either (a) publish "glue crates" that own both trait and type, or (b) use newtype wrappers. Rust's ecosystem has adapted; Avra's should too.
- **Versioning:** strict from day one in v1.0. Loosening this later is backward-compatible; tightening it wouldn't be, so start strict.

**Sub-decision 16.6: Circular imports**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Error — detect and reject
  - (b) Allowed — lazy resolution
  - (c) Allowed within a package, disallowed across
- **Decision:** **(c) Allowed within a package, disallowed across packages**
- **Rationale:** Within a package, circular imports often arise naturally from closely-related types that reference each other (e.g., `User` → `Post` → `User`). Forbidding them (a) forces awkward type extraction and hurts local reasoning. Across packages, circular dependencies are almost always an architectural smell indicating wrong layering — rejecting them prevents dependency graphs from becoming tangled.
- **Intra-package circularity:** the compiler processes package files as a unit, resolving types and traits in two passes — first collect all declarations, then resolve bodies. Mutual reference is natural.
- **Cross-package circularity:** forbidden. If package A depends on B and B depends on A, the compiler errors with a clear message listing the cycle: "Package cycle detected: @myapp -> @utils -> @myapp. Cross-package cycles indicate layering issues; extract shared types to a third package."
- **Examples:**
  ```
  // Within a package — OK
  // src/models/user.av
  use models.post.{Post}
  type User = { name: string, posts: List<Post> }
  
  // src/models/post.av
  use models.user.{User}
  type Post = { content: string, author: User }
  
  // Across packages — ERROR
  // @myapp depends on @utils
  // @utils depends on @myapp
  // Compiler: "Package cycle detected"
  ```

**Sub-decision 16.7: Re-exports**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not allowed — explicit re-declaration only
  - (b) `export use path.{item}` re-exports
  - (c) Transitive by default
- **Decision:** **(b) Explicit re-exports via `export` on import**
- **Rationale:** Not allowed (a) makes it impossible to build curated facades or aggregate public APIs — every user of a lib must know the full internal structure. Transitive by default (c) creates hidden coupling — users import from `X` and start using something that transitively came from `Y`, then `X` drops `Y` and user code breaks. Explicit re-exports (b) are opt-in: package authors curate what they want to expose.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  // src/lib.av — the package's public surface
  export use auth.login.{attempt_login, Session}
  export use auth.signup.{create_account}
  export use models.user.{User}
  
  // Users of the package:
  use @myapp/main.{attempt_login, User}
  // ^ cleaner than having to know internal structure like `@myapp/auth.login`
  ```
- **Semantics:**
  - `export use path.{item}` imports from `path` AND re-exports it at the current module
  - Re-exported items are identical to the originals — no wrapping, no renaming (unless `as`)
  - Re-export chains work: B re-exports from A, C re-exports from B — the item flows through
- **Style:** packages should have a `lib.av` or module root that curates the public API via re-exports. Users shouldn't have to know the internal layout.

**Sub-decision 16.8: Package registry**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Centralized registry (crates.io / npm)
  - (b) Decentralized Git-based (Go modules)
  - (c) Hybrid — central registry + Git fallback, content-addressed storage
- **Decision:** **(c) Hybrid — centralized registry with content-addressed immutable storage, Git-URL fallback**
- **Rationale:** Centralized-only (a) creates a single point of failure and gatekeeping issues — npm and crates.io have both had crises. Git-only (b) makes discovery, version resolution, and reproducibility harder than it should be. Hybrid (c) gives us the best of both: a curated registry for the common case, Git URLs as an escape hatch for private/internal/experimental packages.
- **Architecture:**
  - **Central registry (`registry.avra.dev`)** — curated namespace, stores package metadata and manifests
  - **Content-addressed immutable storage** — each published version is hashed; the hash is the canonical reference; versions can never be retroactively modified (prevents the npm-style "I deleted my package" incident)
  - **Git-URL dependencies** — `forge.toml` can reference a package by Git URL for private or unpublished packages
  - **LLVM bitcode caching** — the registry stores pre-compiled bitcode for common targets, shrinking build times dramatically
- **Manifest examples (in `forge.toml`):**
  ```toml
  [dependencies]
  http = "@std/http@1.2.0"                          # registry
  auth = "@myorg/auth@2.0.0"                        # registry
  internal = { git = "https://github.com/myorg/internal.git", rev = "abc123" }  # git
  local = { path = "../shared-lib" }                # local path (dev)
  ```
- **Immutability guarantee:** once `@myorg/auth@2.0.0` is published, its content hash is fixed forever. You cannot republish a different artifact under the same version. Bug fixes go to `2.0.1`. This protects against supply-chain attacks and ensures reproducible builds.
- **Security:** registry signatures, mandatory 2FA for publishers in reserved namespaces, optional cryptographic attestation of builds.
- **Versioning:** registry ships when the package system ships (v1.0). Bitcode caching is an optimization that can be added later without changing semantics.

**Sub-decision 16.9: Versioning**

- **Options:**
  - (a) SemVer enforced automatically
  - (b) Compiler-enforced floor with author control
  - (c) No versioning — always latest
- **Decision:** **(b) Compiler-enforced floor with author control**
- **Rationale:** Fully-automated SemVer (a) is a nice idea but practice shows it's never quite right — authors always need to override ("this is technically breaking but nobody was using that API"). No versioning (c) makes stability impossible. Compiler-enforced floor with author control hits the balance: the compiler prevents disaster (you can't call a minor version breaking), the author retains judgment about release timing and minor tweaks.
- **How it works:**
  - Authors bump versions manually following SemVer
  - Compiler verifies that patch versions contain no ABI changes, minor versions contain no ABI-breaking changes, major versions can contain anything
  - If the compiler detects breakage in an inappropriate version bump, it errors: "Changes to exported API require major version bump (current: 1.2.3 → proposed: 1.3.0). Breaking changes found: [list]"
  - Author can override with `@allow_breaking_change(reason: string)` — documented, visible in commits
- **What the compiler checks:**
  - Exported function signatures (type changes, added/removed parameters)
  - Exported type definitions (added fields without defaults, removed fields, type changes)
  - Trait definitions (added methods without defaults, removed methods)
  - Re-exports (item removed from exposed surface)
- **What it doesn't check (human judgment):**
  - Semantic behavior changes that don't touch types
  - Performance characteristics
  - Documentation changes
- **Connection to immutable registry (16.8):** once published, a version is immutable. If you mess up the version bump, you can't fix the existing version — you must publish a new one. The compile-time check prevents this from happening in the first place.
- **Override semantics:** `@allow_breaking_change` on a release tells the compiler "I know this is technically breaking; I'm shipping it anyway." This creates a prominent commit-log entry and notifies downstream users during upgrade. Not a free pass — teams use it for pragmatic releases, not routine breakage.

---

## Part 5: Ecosystem and Platform

Beyond the language — the platform decisions that make Avra a substrate rather than just a language.

---

### Axis 17: Provider System

**Sub-decision 17.1: Provider mechanism**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Libraries — import functions
  - (b) Providers — register keywords and compile-time behavior
  - (c) Both — libraries for data, providers for syntax
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — libraries for normal code, providers for compile-time extension**
- **Rationale:** Most code is libraries — import functions, call them. Providers are the special subset that extends the language itself: registering keywords, implementing derive macros, generating compile-time code, hooking into build processes. Separating the two concepts makes clear what kind of code is doing what. You don't need the provider machinery for a JSON parser; you do need it for an ORM that generates DB schemas from `@derive Model`.
- **What a library does:**
  - Exports functions, types, traits, constants
  - Used via normal `import` statements
  - Runs entirely at runtime
  - Example: `@std/json`, `@std/http`, `@community/regex`
- **What a provider additionally does:**
  - Registers keywords (e.g., `view`, `model`, `service`)
  - Implements `@derive` macros (e.g., `@derive Model` generates DB + REST + OpenAPI + UI)
  - Generates compile-time code (e.g., OpenAPI schema from a service declaration)
  - Hooks into the build process (e.g., compile CSS, generate bindings)
  - Example: `@std/models`, `@std/ui`, `@std/http_server`
- **Every provider is a library too:** providers include library-level exports alongside their compile-time hooks. Users import from them as normal packages.
- **Why this matters for the ecosystem:** "provider" is not a scary concept — it's just a library with extra capabilities that opts into compile-time participation. New providers can be authored by anyone with the capability permissions granted.

**Sub-decision 17.2: Provider manifest format**

- **Options:**
  - (a) TOML (`provider.toml`)
  - (b) Avra itself (`provider.av`)
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — `forge.toml` for metadata, Avra code for registration logic**
- **Rationale:** Config-as-data (TOML) is right for things like name/version/dependencies/capabilities — declarative, tool-readable, diff-friendly. Config-as-code (Avra) is right for anything involving logic — keyword registration, derive behavior, compile-time hooks. Using both lets each format do what it's best at. Pure TOML (a) is too limiting for complex registrations; pure Avra (b) makes tools read-and-parse Avra to extract simple metadata.
- **`forge.toml` (metadata):**
  ```toml
  [provider]
  name = "@std/http_server"
  version = "1.0.0"
  authors = ["Anthropic"]
  
  [provider.registers]
  keywords = ["service", "route"]
  derives = ["Endpoint"]
  build_hooks = ["compile_time"]
  
  [capabilities]
  network = ["listen"]
  env = ["read:PORT", "read:HOST"]
  
  [dependencies]
  @std/http = "1.0.0"
  ```
- **`provider.av` (registration logic):**
  ```
  use @compiler.{Keyword, DeriveBody, parse_fn}
  
  // Register the `service` keyword with its parsing and codegen
  @provider_register(Keyword)
  export fn register_service() -> Keyword {
    Keyword {
      name: "service",
      parser: parse_service_block,
      emit: emit_service_code,
    }
  }
  
  @provider_register(DeriveBody)
  export fn derive_endpoint(t: TypeInfo) -> AvraCode {
    // Generate the HTTP endpoint code from the service declaration
    ...
  }
  ```
- **Separation of concerns:** TOML tells tooling "what is this package"; Avra tells the compiler "what does this package do."

**Sub-decision 17.3: Provider keyword registration**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Static — provider declares keywords in manifest
  - (b) Dynamic — provider plugin runs at compile time
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — static declaration for the common case, dynamic registration for advanced cases**
- **Rationale:** Simple keyword providers (register one keyword, map it to a parser) don't need a full plugin runtime — a static declaration in `forge.toml` is enough. Complex providers (register N keywords based on configuration, conditionally register based on features) need dynamic registration via a compile-time plugin. Supporting both keeps simple cases simple and powerful cases possible.
- **Static declaration (common):**
  ```toml
  [provider.registers]
  keywords = ["service", "route"]
  derives = ["Endpoint"]
  ```
  The compiler matches these against exported functions in `provider.av` by convention: `register_service`, `register_route`, `derive_endpoint`.
- **Dynamic registration (advanced):**
  ```
  // In provider.av
  @provider_main
  export fn initialize(ctx: CompilerContext) -> ProviderBundle {
    let keywords = if ctx.has_feature("http2") {
      [register_service(), register_route(), register_h2_push()]
    } else {
      [register_service(), register_route()]
    }
    
    ProviderBundle {
      keywords,
      derives: [derive_endpoint()],
      build_hooks: [],
    }
  }
  ```
- **When to use each:**
  - Static when: keyword set is fixed; no logic involved; maximum tool-friendliness
  - Dynamic when: keywords depend on features/configuration; need to generate registrations programmatically; need to inspect the compilation context
- **Compile-time execution:** dynamic providers run within a restricted Avra subset (enforced by the compiler — pure, total, no I/O by default). Capability declarations (17.5) control what a dynamic provider can do.

**Sub-decision 17.4: Provider native code language**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Rust only
  - (b) Any language via C ABI
  - (c) Avra itself (after self-hosting)
- **Decision:** **(b) Any language via C ABI, with Avra as first-class (post self-hosting)**
- **Rationale:** Rust-only (a) is pragmatic for the bootstrap compiler but too restrictive long-term — we want to wrap existing ecosystems (LLVM in C, DB clients in C++, ML runtimes in Python via embedding). Any-language via C ABI (b) is the industry-standard answer. Avra-itself (c) becomes the preferred choice for new providers once the language is self-hosted, but we cannot mandate it — bootstrapping is a multi-year process and providers must be authorable from day one.
- **Mechanics:**
  - **Native providers** compile to a shared library (`.so`, `.dylib`, `.dll`) exposing a C ABI
  - The compiler dlopens the provider during compilation and calls registered entry points
  - Avra providers use the same C ABI internally (compiled as shared library) — no special path for Avra-written providers
  - Interpreter-hosted providers (v2.1+, e.g., Python-written providers) work through the FFI interpreter embedding tier
- **Language mix in practice:**
  - **Avra-written providers** are first-class and preferred — they can use the full language surface and benefit from provider ecosystem improvements directly
  - **Native providers in C/Rust/Zig** are used for wrapping existing ecosystems (LLVM, SQLite, crypto libraries) where the value is in the existing library, not in the binding code
  - **Interpreter-hosted providers** (v2.1+, e.g., Python or JavaScript) enable scripting and ML ecosystem integration
- **Guarantees:** regardless of implementation language, providers present the same registration interface to the compiler. An Avra consumer cannot tell whether a provider is written in Avra or C.
- **Versioning:** C ABI support ships in v1.x (when FFI ships). Avra-written providers are first-class from v1.0 — the compiler itself is built from providers, after all.

**Sub-decision 17.5: Provider security model**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Trust-based (npm model)
  - (b) Capability declarations, WASM sandbox for untrusted
  - (c) Full capability types in language
- **Decision:** **(b) Capability declarations + WASM sandbox for native providers — consistent with 13.2**
- **Rationale:** Trust-based (a) is the npm model that has repeatedly failed: malicious package updates, typosquatting, supply-chain attacks. Full capability types in the language (c) would require pervasive type-system changes that conflict with 13.1. Capability declarations + WASM (b) is the pragmatic middle ground that we already chose for 13.2.
- **How it works (mirrors 13.2):**
  - Every provider declares its capabilities in `forge.toml` under `[capabilities]`
  - Avra-written providers: static verification — compiler rejects use of a capability not declared
  - Native providers (C/Rust/Zig): compiled to WASM (cross-compile path); run in WASI sandbox with declared capabilities enforced by the host
  - Trusted/blessed providers (`@std/*`, `@anthropic/*`): may run as native code with kernel-level enforcement (cgroups/seccomp) rather than WASM for performance
- **Compile-time execution:** providers running at compile time (deriving types, generating code) have tighter default sandboxing — by default, no I/O, no network, no filesystem access. Providers requiring these (e.g., a codegen provider reading a schema file) must declare the capability and be approved.
- **Deploy-time capability propagation:** decision 13.6 applies — a deployed binary's capabilities are the union of every provider's declared capabilities. Users see the full picture before deploying.
- **Reserved namespaces:** `@std/*` providers receive blanket trust for their declared capabilities (curated by Avra maintainers). Community providers require user approval on first use: "Provider `@community/sqlite-bindings` requires capabilities: filesystem.read, filesystem.write, process.spawn. Allow? [y/N/review]"

**Sub-decision 17.6: Provider compile-time vs runtime**

- **Options:**
  - (a) All provider code runs at runtime
  - (b) Providers can run at compile time (code generation)
  - (c) Both — providers declare which
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — provider declares each export's phase**
- **Rationale:** A provider like `@std/http` is pure runtime — you import functions, you call them. A provider like `@std/models` is mixed: `@derive Model` runs at compile time to generate DB/REST/UI code, but the generated code runs at runtime. A provider like `@std/regex` might run at compile time for statically-known patterns (building optimized matchers into the binary) AND at runtime for dynamic patterns. Forcing providers into one phase or the other (a or b) would rule out entire classes of useful providers.
- **Declaration:** the provider's `forge.toml` and annotations declare the phase of each export:
  ```toml
  [provider.exports]
  runtime = ["Http", "Request", "Response", "serve"]
  derive = ["Endpoint"]        # runs at compile time
  build_hook = ["generate_openapi_schema"]  # runs during build
  ```
  Or in code:
  ```
  @runtime export fn serve(app: App) -> Never { ... }
  @compile_time export fn derive_endpoint(t: TypeInfo) -> AvraCode { ... }
  @build_hook export fn generate_openapi(spec: Spec) -> File { ... }
  ```
- **Phase separation:**
  - **Compile-time code** runs within the compiler's sandboxed runtime; has access to AST, type information, source; cannot do I/O without explicit capability
  - **Build-hook code** runs between compilation and linking; can generate auxiliary files (schemas, bindings, manifests); has filesystem capabilities limited to the build output directory
  - **Runtime code** is the normal case — compiled into the final binary, runs at program execution
- **Cross-phase communication:** compile-time code can emit Avra source that becomes runtime code. Build hooks can emit files that runtime code loads. This is how an ORM provider can generate DB schemas at compile time AND run queries at runtime — different exports in different phases.
- **Why this axis matters:** the three-phase model (compile/build/run) is what makes providers more powerful than libraries — they participate in the build pipeline, not just execute at runtime.

---

### Axis 18: Concurrency Model

**Sub-decision 18.1: Concurrency primitive**

- **Options:**
  - (a) OS threads
  - (b) Green threads / fibers (Go)
  - (c) async/await
  - (d) Actor model
- **Decision:** **(b) Green threads / fibers, Go-style — implicit M:N scheduler, no function coloring**
- **Rationale:** OS threads alone (a) are too heavyweight to spawn thousands cheaply — every HTTP request can't be a thread. async/await (c) creates "function coloring" — every call site must know whether the callee is async, and the distinction metastasizes through the call graph. Actor model (d) is too opinionated; actors can be built on top of green threads but forcing actors as the only primitive excludes many patterns. Green threads (b) are the best default: cheap to spawn, no coloring, blocking looks synchronous because it is (from the fiber's perspective).
- **How it works:**
  - Every Avra program runs on an M:N scheduler (M green threads over N OS threads)
  - I/O operations (network, disk, sleep) yield the fiber — the scheduler moves on to another ready fiber
  - CPU-bound work runs to completion; preemption happens at function call boundaries (cheap safepoint insertion) to prevent starvation
  - Green threads are cheap (~8KB stack initial, grows on demand); spawning millions is fine for connection-per-fiber patterns
- **Connection to memory model (9.x):** v1.0 is single-threaded from the fiber's perspective — non-atomic refcounts work because only one fiber sees the object at a time. Boundaries between fibers are value-crossing boundaries (channels, explicit sharing via `Arc<T>`).
- **Comparison to prior art:**
  - Go: the reference implementation of this model; we borrow heavily
  - Erlang/Elixir: similar M:N model but more actor-oriented
  - Rust async: we explicitly reject the coloring tax
  - Java Loom: validates the "virtual threads in a statically-typed language" approach
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0. The scheduler and runtime are part of the static-linked Avra runtime.

**Sub-decision 18.2: Async syntax**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Implicit — no async keyword
  - (b) Explicit async/await
  - (c) `spawn` for explicit concurrency, sync-looking for sequential
- **Decision:** **(c) `spawn` for explicit concurrency; `Task<T, E>` mirrors `Result<T, E>`; `|>` pipe operator for task pipelines; `Actor<Msg>` library pattern for addressable tasks**
- **Rationale:** All code is written in a sync-looking style. There is no `async` keyword, no `await` keyword. `spawn { ... }` is the only primitive for creating concurrency. Task handles carry both success and error types, mirroring `Result<T, E>`. For common patterns — pipelines and actors — we provide an operator (`|>`) and a library pattern (`Actor<Msg>`) rather than conflating task and channel into one primitive.
- **Layered design rationale:**
  - **Primitives (language):** `spawn`, `Task<T, E>`, `Channel<T>` are separate orthogonal concepts
  - **Operator sugar (language):** `|>` pipes values through a chain of task-backed stages
  - **Library patterns (`@std/actor`, `@std/pipeline`):** `Actor<Msg>` wraps a task + mailbox channel for addressable message passing; `Pipeline` composes stages with configurable buffering/fan-out
  - Conflating task and channel into a single primitive (every task is a channel) was considered and rejected: it forces every task to pay for a mailbox, restricts tasks to one message type without sum types, and makes the common "compute in parallel, join result" case awkward.
- **Basic syntax:**
  ```
  // Sequential — looks like ordinary code
  let response = http.get(url)?
  let body = response.read_body()?
  
  // Concurrent — explicit spawn
  let task: Task<Response, HttpError> = spawn {
    http.get(url)?
  }
  
  let response = task.await?       // wait for result, propagate error
  // OR
  let response = task.result(timeout: 5.seconds)?
  
  // Spawn many
  let tasks = urls.map((url) -> spawn { http.get(url)? })
  let responses = Task.all(tasks).await?   // wait for all
  let first = Task.race(tasks).await?      // first to complete
  let winner = Task.any(tasks).await?      // first success (ignore failures)
  ```
- **`Task<T, E>` API (mirrors `Result<T, E>` in error handling):**
  - `task.await` — block until complete; returns `Result<T, E>` or auto-propagates with `?`
  - `task.result(timeout: Duration)` — with timeout; returns `Result<T, TaskError<E>>` where `TaskError` adds timeout and cancellation variants
  - `task.cancel()` — request cancellation
  - `task.is_done()` — non-blocking check
  - `Task.all([tasks])` — combine into single task of `List<T>`; fails on first error
  - `Task.all_settled([tasks])` — combine into `List<Result<T, E>>`; never fails
  - `Task.race([tasks])` — first to complete (success or failure)
  - `Task.any([tasks])` — first success; returns aggregated errors if all fail
- **Pipe operator `|>` for task pipelines:**
  ```
  // Sequential value pipe (pure transformations)
  let result = data |> parse |> validate |> normalize
  
  // Concurrent pipe — each stage is spawned, auto-wired via channels
  let results = source_stream
    |> spawn parse          // parser task, channel-connected to source
    |> spawn validate       // validator task
    |> spawn store          // storage task
    |> collect              // terminal stage, accumulates results
  ```
  The `|> spawn` form desugars to: create a channel between stages, spawn the function as a task that receives from the previous channel and sends to the next. `|>` without `spawn` is a pure value pipe (equivalent to `f(g(x))`).
- **Error propagation through pipes:** any stage that fails cancels downstream stages via structured concurrency (18.7) and propagates the error to the pipeline's collector. The caller sees one `Result` covering the whole pipeline.
- **Actor library pattern (`@std/actor`):**
  ```
  use @std/actor.{Actor, ActorBehavior}
  
  type ChatMessage = 
    | Join(user: User)
    | Leave(user: User)
    | Send(user: User, text: string)
    | Shutdown
  
  let room: Actor<ChatMessage> = Actor.spawn((msg) -> match msg {
    Join(u)       -> broadcast_join(u)
    Leave(u)      -> broadcast_leave(u)
    Send(u, t)    -> broadcast_message(u, t)
    Shutdown      -> Actor.exit()
  })
  
  room.tell(Join(alice))           // fire-and-forget
  let reply = room.ask(GetUsers).await?   // request-response via hidden reply channel
  ```
  `Actor<Msg>` is a regular library type — just a task bundled with a mailbox channel. No language-level primitive.
- **Closure-returning spawn:** the `{ }` block is a closure; it follows all closure semantics (captures inferred, `move` escape hatch, cross-level rules from 9.22).
- **No function coloring:** any function can be called from inside `spawn`; any function can call `task.await`. The distinction between "async" and "sync" code vanishes — there is only code, and whether you spawn it.

**Sub-decision 18.3: Parallelism primitive**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `parallel { ... }` block for concurrent execution
  - (b) `spawn cpu` for dedicated threads
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — `parallel { }` for data parallelism, `spawn cpu { }` for dedicated CPU work**
- **Rationale:** Green threads (18.1) assume most concurrency is I/O-bound. CPU-bound work needs real OS thread parallelism to actually use multiple cores. The question is how to expose that. Two patterns handle different cases:
  - `parallel { a(); b(); c() }` — fork-join on an OS thread pool. Use when you have several CPU-bound operations that should run concurrently and be joined.
  - `spawn cpu { ... }` — dedicate an OS thread for long-running CPU work. Use for sustained background computation, not fork-join.
- **`parallel` block:**
  ```
  parallel {
    let hash_a = compute_hash(file_a)
    let hash_b = compute_hash(file_b)
    let hash_c = compute_hash(file_c)
  }
  // After the block: all three hashes computed, in parallel, on a thread pool.
  ```
  The block is syntactic sugar that:
  - Submits each statement to the CPU thread pool
  - Joins all results before exiting the block
  - Propagates errors (if any statement fails, the block fails with the first error; others continue and are discarded)
- **`spawn cpu` variant:**
  ```
  let task = spawn cpu {
    mine_bitcoin_until_cancelled()
  }
  ```
  Runs on a dedicated OS thread, not the green-thread scheduler. `Task<T>` API from 18.2 still applies.
- **Parallelism map/reduce:** common pattern gets a dedicated helper:
  ```
  let hashes = files.parallel_map((f) -> compute_hash(f))
  let total = numbers.parallel_reduce(0, (acc, n) -> acc + n)
  ```
- **Thread pool configuration:** `ParallelRuntime.configure(workers: int)` at startup; defaults to `num_cpus()`. Runtime pool is shared across all `parallel` blocks and `spawn cpu` calls.
- **Connection to memory model:** when memory model upgrades from single-thread to CPU parallelism (see 9.x architectural section), refcount operations become atomic. The compiler does the upgrade transparently.

**Sub-decision 18.4: Message passing**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Channels only
  - (b) Actors only
  - (c) Shared memory with locks
  - (d) Channels + shared memory with locks
- **Decision:** **(d) Channels as primary, shared memory via typed sync wrappers as escape hatch**
- **Rationale:** "Share memory by communicating, don't communicate by sharing memory" (Go's famous line) is the right default. Channels (a) alone are enough for most code. Actors (b) are an opinionated pattern that can be built on channels — forcing actors is too prescriptive. Shared memory with locks (c) alone leads to the traditional deadlock-prone concurrent programming we want to avoid. Channels + shared-via-wrappers (d) gives both: clean message passing by default, typed sync primitives when you need them.
- **Channels:**
  ```
  let (send, recv) = Channel.new<Message>(capacity: 100)
  
  spawn {
    while let msg? = recv.receive() {
      process(msg)
    }
    // Loop exits when recv.receive() returns null (channel closed)
  }
  
  send.send(Message.UserRegistered(user))
  send.close()                    // closes the channel; receivers see null and loop exits
  ```
- **Shared memory primitives (`@std/sync`):**
  - `Mutex<T>` — exclusive access; `mu.lock { |data| ... }` scope-bound
  - `RwLock<T>` — many readers or one writer
  - `Atomic<T>` — lock-free for primitive types (`AtomicInt`, `AtomicBool`, `AtomicRef<T>`)
  - `Arc<T>` — atomic reference-counted sharing across fibers
- **Example of safe shared memory:**
  ```
  let counter: Arc<Atomic<int>> = Arc.new(Atomic.new(0))
  
  for i in 0..100 {
    let c = counter.clone()
    spawn { c.fetch_add(1) }
  }
  // Wait for all spawned tasks...
  counter.load() // 100
  ```
- **Channel semantics:**
  - Bounded (capacity > 0) — backpressure when full
  - Unbounded (capacity 0 or unspecified) — grows as needed (use carefully)
  - Synchronous (capacity = 0 rendezvous) — send blocks until receive
  - Closed channels drain remaining messages then return end-of-stream
- **Connection to memory model:** sending `T` over a channel transfers ownership (or increments refcount if shared). Boundaries are explicit in the code — the channel send/receive is the sync point. See memory model 9.x for cross-fiber ownership rules.

**Sub-decision 18.5: Select mechanism**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `select { ... }` for multi-channel wait (Go)
  - (b) Combinator-based (`ch1.or(ch2).receive()`)
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(a) `select { ... }` block — Go-style multi-way wait**
- **Rationale:** Go's `select` block is one of the cleanest concurrency primitives in any language. It reads declaratively, supports multiple operations (send, receive, timeout, default), and composes well. Combinator-based selection (b) is uglier and doesn't compose as naturally for more than two channels. Supporting both (c) is unnecessary complexity — `select` covers everything combinators would.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  select {
    msg = user_events.receive() -> {
      handle_user_event(msg)
    }
    msg = system_events.receive() -> {
      handle_system_event(msg)
    }
    sent = output.send(pending_data) -> {
      pending_data = next_data()
    }
    _ = Timer.after(5.seconds) -> {
      timeout_occurred()
    }
    default -> {
      no_op()
    }
  }
  ```
- **Semantics:**
  - Each arm is a channel operation (send/receive) or a timer or `default`
  - When multiple arms are ready, one is chosen non-deterministically (prevents starvation)
  - `default` fires if no arm is ready immediately (makes the select non-blocking)
  - Without `default`, select blocks until one arm is ready
- **Common patterns:**
  - **Timeout wrapping:**
    ```
    select {
      msg = ch.receive() -> use(msg)
      _ = Timer.after(5.seconds) -> handle_timeout()
    }
    ```
