  - **Non-blocking check:**
    ```
    select {
      msg = ch.receive() -> process(msg)
      default -> continue_loop()
    }
    ```
- **Closed channels:** `recv.receive()` returns `T?` — the value if present, `null` when the channel is closed and drained. Select arms pattern-match like any other optional:
  ```
  select {
    msg = ch.receive() -> match msg {
      val?  -> use(val)
      null  -> exit_loop()
    }
  }
  ```
  This unifies channel-closed signaling with the general `T?` pattern — no special `ChannelClosed` type, just null.

**Sub-decision 18.6: Streams vs channels**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Streams only (lazy pull)
  - (b) Channels only (eager push)
  - (c) Both with conversion
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — channels for parallel producer/consumer across fibers; streams for lazy pipelines on one fiber; bidirectional conversion**
- **Rationale:** Streams and channels are often confused because both carry sequences of values, but they have fundamentally different concurrency structure. They solve different problems, neither subsumes the other, and Avra exposes both.
- **Channel = queue between two running fibers:**
  A channel is a synchronized buffer between a producer fiber and a consumer fiber. Both sides are actively running on their own fibers; the channel is the handoff point. Use channels when you want producer and consumer to run in parallel.
  ```
  let (send, recv) = Channel.new<Line>(capacity: 100)
  
  spawn {
    for line in read_file(path) {
      send.send(parse_line(line))      // producer actively pushes on its own fiber
    }
    send.close()
  }
  
  while let line? = recv.receive() {
    database.insert(line)              // consumer actively pulls on its own fiber
  }
  // → reading and inserting overlap; throughput bounded by the slower side
  ```
- **Stream = lazy sequence on one fiber:**
  A stream is a description of "how to produce the next element." No second fiber runs. Nothing happens until the consumer asks for a value. Use streams when you want a pipeline of transformations to run sequentially on one fiber — simpler semantics, no synchronization overhead.
  ```
  let stream = Stream.range(0, 1_000_000)
    .map((x) -> x * 2)
    .filter((x) -> x > 1000)
  // NOTHING has run yet. `stream` is a recipe, not work-in-progress.
  
  for x in stream {
    process(x)                         // NOW each x is computed on-demand
  }
  // → all work happens on the current fiber, pulled lazily. No parallelism.
  ```
- **Concrete difference — same data, different concurrency:**
  ```
  // Channel version: 2 fibers, producer and consumer overlap
  let (s, r) = Channel.new<int>(capacity: 10)
  spawn { for x in 0..1000 { s.send(expensive_compute(x)) } }
  while let v? = r.receive() { consume(v) }
  // → expensive_compute and consume run in parallel
  
  // Stream version: 1 fiber, lazy pipeline
  for v in (0..1000).stream().map(expensive_compute) {
    consume(v)
  }
  // → expensive_compute and consume run sequentially, interleaved on one fiber
  ```
- **Conversions (both directions):**
  ```
  // Channel → stream: treat an incoming channel as a lazy sequence
  let stream = ch.into_stream()
  
  // Stream → channel: eagerly produce a stream into a buffered channel
  let (send, recv) = Channel.new<int>(capacity: 100)
  spawn { stream.drain_into(send) }
  // now recv receives eagerly-produced values
  ```
- **When to use which:**
  - **Stream:** transforming a collection, parsing a file, building a pipeline of pure functions on the current fiber, bounded small data
  - **Channel:** communicating between spawned tasks, fan-in/fan-out, event streams, backpressure across fiber boundaries, any pipeline that benefits from parallelism
- **Relationship to `|>` pipe operator (18.2):** the `|> spawn stage` form builds channel-backed parallel pipelines. Plain `|>` without `spawn` composes stream-style transformations. Same operator, different realizations based on whether stages are spawned.
- **Versioning:** both ship in v1.0. `@std/stream` and `@std/channel` in the core library.

**Sub-decision 18.7: Cancellation**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Cooperative — check context manually
  - (b) Context propagation (Go)
  - (c) Structured concurrency (scopes cancel children)
- **Decision:** **(c) Structured concurrency — scopes cancel children; explicit `Task.cancel()` for unstructured cases**
- **Rationale:** Structured concurrency is the emerging consensus (Kotlin coroutines, Swift structured concurrency, Python Trio/Curio, Java Loom `StructuredTaskScope`). Go's cooperative `ctx.Done()` pattern (b) is better than nothing but requires every function to manually check; forgetting to check leads to goroutine leaks. Purely manual cancellation (a) leads to even worse leaks. Structured concurrency (c) makes leaks structurally impossible: tasks spawned in a scope MUST complete (or cancel) before the scope exits. The compiler enforces this.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  scope concurrent {
    // These three tasks all complete before the block exits
    let task_a = spawn { work_a() }
    let task_b = spawn { work_b() }
    let task_c = spawn { work_c() }
    
    let results = Task.all([task_a, task_b, task_c]).await?
    process(results)
  }
  // After the scope: all spawned tasks have completed or been cancelled
  
  // If work_a fails:
  //   - scope begins cancellation
  //   - task_b and task_c are signaled to cancel
  //   - scope exits once all tasks have completed their cancellation
  //   - the error propagates out of the scope
  ```
- **Cancellation delivery:**
  - Blocking operations (channel receive, timer, I/O) throw `Cancelled` when the task is cancelled
  - CPU-bound code checks cancellation at safepoints (function calls, loop heads); the compiler inserts these automatically
  - Code can query explicitly via `Task.current().is_cancelled()`
- **Escape from structured:** when you genuinely need detached tasks (background workers, daemons) that outlive their spawn site:
  ```
  let detached = spawn detached {
    daemon_work()
  }
  // detached continues even after the current scope exits
  // caller is responsible for eventually joining or cancelling
  ```
  This is a deliberate opt-out — normal `spawn` is structured.
- **Why this matters:** eliminates the most common concurrency bug class (leaked tasks, zombie goroutines). If a scope exits cleanly, you are guaranteed no tasks it spawned are still running. This composability is the foundation for all higher-level patterns.

---

### Axis 19: Deploy and Infrastructure

**Sub-decision 19.1: Deploy as language feature**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not — use external tools (Terraform, K8s)
  - (b) Deploy block as compiler contract
  - (c) Deploy generated from code topology
- **Decision:** **(b + c) Deploy block as compiler contract, with topology automatically extracted from code**
- **Rationale:** Treating deploy as external (a) is the status quo — you write code, then separately write Terraform/Kubernetes/Dockerfiles, and the two drift. This is where most production bugs and security incidents originate. Making deploy a language feature eliminates that drift class entirely. The compiler knows what services exist (service declarations), what they depend on (imports), what capabilities they need (provider manifests), what memory/CPU they need (annotations). All this is already in the program. The deploy block ties it together and makes the compiler generate correct deployment artifacts.
- **What a deploy block is:**
  ```
  deploy production {
    target: aws
    region: "us-east-1"
    
    service api {
      instances: 3
      memory: 512.mb
      cpu: 0.5
    }
    
    service worker {
      instances: 1
      memory: 1.gb
      cpu: 1.0
    }
    
    database postgres_main {
      engine: postgres_15
      storage: 100.gb
    }
    
    secrets {
      DATABASE_URL: from_aws_secret("db_url")
      API_KEY: from_env("API_KEY")
    }
  }
  ```
- **The contract property:** the deploy block tells the compiler "these are the runtime topologies you must produce." The compiler then:
  - Verifies every imported provider's capabilities are declared in the deploy block
  - Verifies every referenced service exists in code
  - Extracts topology (who calls whom) from the program
  - Generates platform-specific deploy artifacts (IAM policies, K8s manifests, Terraform, etc.)
  - Validates the deploy is feasible (memory budgets, connectivity, etc.)
- **Deployment as continuous lifecycle:** deploying is not a one-shot event — the deploy block is part of the program, versioned with code, and re-generated on each build. Changing `instances: 3` to `instances: 5` and rebuilding produces a new deploy plan that the deployment layer applies. No separate IaC repo.
- **Versioning:** deploy system reserved in v1.0 (`deploy` keyword); ships in v1.x with the first target platforms. The language syntax is stable from v1.0; implementations of targets can evolve.

**Sub-decision 19.2: Infrastructure targets**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Provider-plugin for each target (AWS, Hetzner, Docker)
  - (b) Built-in targets in compiler
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — built-in targets for popular platforms, provider-plugin model for extension**
- **Rationale:** Built-in targets (b) ship a good default experience but limit ecosystem growth. Plugin-only (a) means every user has to manually wire up their target provider, which is worse UX for common platforms. Both (c) gives us curated built-ins (`@std/deploy/*`) with first-class quality, plus the provider plugin mechanism for anything else.
- **Built-in targets (v1.x ships at least these):**
  - `@std/deploy/local` — single-machine Docker / systemd
  - `@std/deploy/docker_compose` — generates docker-compose.yml
  - `@std/deploy/aws` — Lambda, ECS, or EC2 depending on service config
  - `@std/deploy/fly` — Fly.io
  - `@std/deploy/kubernetes` — generates K8s manifests
- **Community / third-party targets:**
  - `@community/deploy/hetzner`
  - `@community/deploy/railway`
  - `@community/deploy/gcp`
  - Any organization can build a private internal target provider for their platform
- **Target selection:** `deploy production { target: aws_lambda }` — the `target` identifier resolves to a provider that's been imported. Each target provider knows how to translate the generic deploy block into platform-specific artifacts.
- **What targets must implement:**
  - Topology materialization (services, databases, queues → platform primitives)
  - Capability enforcement (translate capability declarations to IAM/RBAC)
  - Secret wiring (translate secret declarations to platform secret stores)
  - Observability wiring (translate observability config to platform telemetry)
- **Interoperability:** multiple deploy blocks can coexist in one program (`deploy production { target: aws }`, `deploy staging { target: fly }`, `deploy local { target: docker_compose }`). The CLI picks one when building: `avra deploy production`.

**Sub-decision 19.3: Topology extraction**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Manual — programmer lists services
  - (b) Automatic — compiler extracts from imports and connections
  - (c) Both with override
- **Decision:** **(c) Automatic extraction with manual override — compiler derives topology, programmer annotates exceptions**
- **Rationale:** Manual topology (a) means duplicating information already in the code — services are declared as `service foo { }`, databases as `database bar { }`, connections via imports. Forcing users to also list them in the deploy block invites drift. Pure automatic (b) can't handle every nuance (this service needs more RAM, this database should be isolated). Automatic + override (c) lets the compiler do the tedious work and the programmer declare the exceptions.
- **What the compiler extracts automatically:**
  - Every `service name { }` declaration in code → a runtime service
  - Every `database name { }` declaration → a managed database
  - Every imported provider's capabilities → required permissions
  - Every `spawn cpu { }` at service-initialization → CPU worker thread requirement
  - Every connection via Channel/HTTP/RPC between services → network edge
  - Every `secret("key")` call → required secret binding
- **What the programmer overrides in the deploy block:**
  ```
  deploy production {
    service api {
      // extracted: exists, uses http_server, reads DATABASE_URL
      instances: 3              // override default of 1
      memory: 512.mb            // override default
      cpu: 0.5                  // override default
    }
    
    service worker {
      instances: 1
      memory: 1.gb
    }
    
    // Implicitly: all extracted services that aren't listed use defaults
  }
  ```
- **Safety property:** if the deploy block references a service that doesn't exist in code, compile error. If the code has a service not mentioned in the deploy block, defaults are used. If a service uses a capability not declared (and no override allows it), compile error.
- **Inspection tool:** `avra deploy --plan production` prints the full extracted topology. Users can see exactly what will be deployed before applying.
- **LLM-friendliness:** LLMs can read the deploy block to understand the architecture at a glance, without reading the whole codebase. This is the "one-source-many-projections" principle (P12) applied to infrastructure.

**Sub-decision 19.4: Secrets handling**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Environment variables only
  - (b) First-class `secret("key")` with provider backends
  - (c) Vault integration via provider
- **Decision:** **(b) First-class `secret("key")` literal with pluggable provider backends**
- **Rationale:** Environment variables alone (a) are the modern default but lack structure — secrets get logged, end up in error messages, leak into traces, and there's no audit trail for access. Vault-specific integration (c) couples the language to one tool. First-class `secret("key")` with pluggable backends (b) lets the language treat secrets as a distinct type with built-in safety properties, while the deploy block chooses the actual backing store (env var, AWS Secrets Manager, Vault, GCP Secret Manager).
- **Syntax and type:**
  ```
  let db_password: Secret<string> = secret("DATABASE_PASSWORD")
  let api_key: Secret<string> = secret("API_KEY")
  
  // Secret<T> is not the same type as T — cannot accidentally log or pass around
  // println(db_password)  // compile error: Secret<string> does not implement Display
  
  // Explicit extraction to cross into normal value space
  let conn = postgres.connect(url, user, db_password.expose())
  ```
- **Deploy-block wiring:**
  ```
  deploy production {
    secrets {
      DATABASE_PASSWORD: from_aws_secret("prod/db-password")
      API_KEY:           from_env("PROD_API_KEY")
      JWT_SIGNING_KEY:   from_vault("secret/jwt")
    }
  }
  
  deploy local {
    secrets {
      DATABASE_PASSWORD: from_env("DATABASE_PASSWORD")
      API_KEY:           literal("dev-key-not-for-prod")  // explicit dev-only
    }
  }
  ```
- **Safety properties enforced by the compiler:**
  - `Secret<T>` does NOT implement `Display`, `Debug`, or any auto-derivation of formatting traits — cannot be accidentally printed or serialized
  - `Secret<T>` is excluded from auto-generated logs, tracing spans, and error messages
  - Extraction via `.expose()` is a deliberate, grep-able action; CI/review tooling can flag unusual uses
  - `.expose_for(purpose: string)` variant adds required documentation and produces an audit log entry in environments with auditing enabled
- **Provider backends:**
  - `from_env(key)` — environment variable
  - `from_aws_secret(path)` — AWS Secrets Manager
  - `from_vault(path)` — HashiCorp Vault
  - `from_gcp_secret(name)` — GCP Secret Manager
  - `from_azure_keyvault(vault, name)` — Azure Key Vault
  - `literal(value)` — inline value, only allowed in dev/test deploy blocks; error in prod
  - Third-party providers can add new backends via the provider system
- **Rotation:** backends that support rotation can refresh secret values at runtime. The `Secret<T>` holder retrieves the latest value on each `.expose()` call when configured for rotation.
- **Versioning:** ships with the deploy system in v1.x. The `secret()` function and `Secret<T>` type are reserved in v1.0.

**Sub-decision 19.5: Observability**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Library-based (opt-in instrumentation)
  - (b) Auto-instrumented at network boundaries
  - (c) Compiler-generated spans for every function
- **Decision:** **(b) Auto-instrumented at network boundaries, with layered escape hatches at every granularity**
- **Rationale:** Library-based (a) gets forgotten — code ships without instrumentation, then production breaks and nobody knows why. Every-function (c) is too noisy and slow. Auto at network boundaries (b) is the sweet spot: you get rich telemetry for free across every network hop, but internal function calls don't pollute traces. The critical refinement is that every layer of magic must be **observable and disable-able** — users with strong opinions must have full control.
- **Default magic (zero configuration):**
  - Compiler auto-instruments at all network boundaries: HTTP handlers (request in, response out), DB queries (query start, row count, duration), channel sends/receives, spawn lifecycles, RPC calls, provider-declared I/O
  - Generates OpenTelemetry-compatible spans with proper parent-child relationships
  - Trace context propagates automatically across service boundaries via HTTP headers and channel metadata
  - Default exporter ships telemetry to a locally-running collector in dev; auto-configured for the deploy platform in prod (CloudWatch on AWS, Cloud Logging on GCP, etc.)
  - Developers see rich traces, metrics, logs without writing a line of instrumentation code
- **Layered escape hatches:**
  - **Per function:** `@no_trace fn internal_helper() { ... }` suppresses auto-instrumentation on this function
  - **Per service:** `service worker { observability: off }` in deploy block disables all auto-instrumentation for a service
  - **Per build:** `[observability] auto_instrument = false` in `forge.toml` disables everything; user handles observability manually
  - **Swap backend:** `[observability] backend = "datadog"` (or "honeycomb", "jaeger", etc.) — compiler wires to different exporter
  - **Augment:** `import @std/observability.{ Span, Metric, Log }` for explicit custom spans, business metrics, structured logs when you want more than the auto-instrumentation provides
  - **Fully replace:** `[observability] managed = false` disables the managed stack; user imports their preferred OpenTelemetry SDK directly
- **Visibility:** `avra explain observability [service]` lists every auto-instrumented boundary and the metadata it captures. Nothing is hidden from the programmer.
- **Cost semantics:** auto-instrumentation adds negligible overhead at network boundaries (the I/O dominates anyway). No per-function overhead by default. If user opts into per-function tracing manually, they pay the cost knowingly.
- **Why this matters:** observability is where "magic is invisible and uncontrollable" would lose every opinionated user (every team has strong views on this). The design principle here is explicit: **magic by default, escape hatch at every granularity, visibility into what's happening.**

**Sub-decision 19.6: Local dev simulation**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Run everything in one process
  - (b) Docker compose generated from deploy block
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — single-process by default, auto-escalate to docker-compose when topology requires it**
- **Rationale:** Single-process (a) is the fastest dev loop but breaks when services need different runtimes (one uses Postgres, another uses Redis, another is an FFI binding to a specific native library). Docker-compose (b) is slower but handles any topology. The right answer is both, with the compiler picking based on what the deploy block describes.
- **Single-process mode (default):**
  ```
  avra dev
  ```
  Compiles all services into one binary; they run as separate fibers in one process. Databases are spun up as embedded instances where possible (SQLite instead of Postgres, in-memory Redis). Network calls between services short-circuit to direct function calls. Startup time measured in milliseconds.
- **Auto-escalate to compose:** when single-process mode can't accommodate the topology (e.g., deploy block requests Postgres explicitly, or two services need conflicting native dependencies), the compiler generates a docker-compose.yml and runs `docker compose up`. Same command (`avra dev`), different execution strategy. The developer doesn't choose; the compiler picks.
- **Escalation rules:**
  - Deploy block requests a specific database engine → compose with that engine
  - Two services use conflicting provider capabilities → compose with isolation
  - Provider declares it needs a dedicated container → compose
  - User explicitly passes `--mode=compose` → compose
  - Otherwise → single process
- **Dev/prod parity:**
  - In single-process mode, the compiler inserts simulated delays and possible failures on network boundaries (configurable in `forge.toml`) so devs experience network reality
  - Observability (19.5) wires to a local collector in both modes; traces look the same
  - The same code runs in both modes — no `if dev` branches required
- **Hot reload:** both modes support hot reload. Single-process mode reloads in ~100ms; compose mode reloads the affected service container.
- **Data persistence:** compose mode uses named Docker volumes; single-process mode uses a local `.avra/dev_data/` directory. Both persist across restarts unless explicitly reset with `avra dev --fresh`.
- **Versioning:** ships with the deploy system in v1.x.

**Sub-decision 19.7: Eject path**

- **Options:**
  - (a) No eject — Avra manages deploy
  - (b) `forge deploy --eject` generates raw YAML/Terraform
  - (c) Both (managed and ejected side by side)
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — managed deploy by default, clean eject path to raw artifacts when teams outgrow it**
- **Rationale:** Mandating managed deploy (a) is an existential risk: real teams will hit scale, compliance, or organizational constraints that managed deploy can't handle, and "rewrite from scratch" is not an acceptable answer. Pure eject (b) makes the managed path feel like a demo rather than a production tool. Both (c) treats managed as the golden path AND treats eject as a respectable door. Users always know they can leave.
- **Eject command:**
  ```
  avra deploy --eject production > deploy.tf       # Terraform HCL
  avra deploy --eject production --format=yaml     # K8s manifests
  avra deploy --eject production --format=cdk      # AWS CDK TypeScript
  ```
  Produces the complete deployment artifact in the target format, ready for the team's existing CI/CD. The generated output is idiomatic (not machine-mangled) — teams can read, review, and modify it by hand.
- **Eject is one-way by design:** once a team commits to managing their own Terraform (for example), they stop running `avra deploy` and start running `terraform apply`. The Avra deploy block stays in source as documentation of intent, but the source of truth for infrastructure becomes the ejected artifacts. Attempting to manage both simultaneously is not supported — pick one.
- **Partial eject — "managed with exceptions":**
  ```
  deploy production {
    target: aws
    
    service legacy_api {
      managed: false
      eject_to: "infra/legacy_api.tf"        // this service uses hand-written Terraform
    }
    
    service modern_api {
      // managed by Avra
    }
  }
  ```
  Some services managed by Avra, others by external tools. Useful for incrementally migrating legacy infrastructure into Avra management, or incrementally ejecting from it.
- **Design commitment:** the managed path and the ejected path are both first-class. The language doesn't punish teams for eventually outgrowing the managed path.
- **Versioning:** ships with the deploy system in v1.x. Each target platform supports its own eject formats (AWS → Terraform + CloudFormation + CDK; K8s → YAML; Fly → fly.toml; etc.).

---

### Axis 20: Error System and Diagnostics

**Sub-decision 20.1: Error code format**

- **Options:**
  - (a) F-codes (`F0012`) with registry
  - (b) Descriptive strings only
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — stable F-code identifier plus descriptive message for every error**
- **Rationale:** F-codes (a) are essential for stable identifiers — docs link to them, LLMs train on them, build systems filter by them, community knowledge bases key off them. Descriptive messages (b) are what humans read first and understand. Neither alone is enough: codes without descriptions are cryptic; descriptions without codes are unsearchable. Both together are the right answer, adopted by Rust (`E0308`), TypeScript (`TS2322`), and every mature compiler.
- **Format:**
  ```
  error[F0308]: type mismatch
    ┌─ src/auth.av:42:14
    │
  42│   let user: User = get_user_id(id)
    │             ^^^^   --------------
    │             │      │
    │             │      expected `UUID`, but returns `User`
    │             expected `User` here
    │
    = help: did you mean to call `get_user(id)` instead of `get_user_id(id)`?
    = see also: https://docs.avra.dev/errors/F0308
  ```
- **F-code allocation:**
  - `F0001`–`F0999`: lexer and parser errors
  - `F1000`–`F1999`: type checker
  - `F2000`–`F2999`: borrow/memory checker
  - `F3000`–`F3999`: resolution (imports, modules, visibility)
  - `F4000`–`F4999`: provider system errors
  - `F5000`–`F5999`: effect/capability errors
  - `F6000`–`F6999`: refinement/contract verification
  - `F7000`–`F7999`: codegen errors
  - `F8000`–`F8999`: deploy-time errors
  - `F9000`–`F9998`: warnings (same system, different severity)
  - `F9999`: internal compiler error (ICE) — see 20.6
- **Stability:** an F-code, once assigned, never changes. The message and help text can be improved, but the code is forever. Renaming is handled by adding a new code and deprecating the old.
- **Registry:** `docs.avra.dev/errors/FXXXX` is the canonical page for each error — explanation, examples, common fixes, links to relevant issues. The compiler auto-generates the registry from source annotations.
- **Versioning:** the system ships in v1.0. The initial set grows organically as the compiler matures.

**Sub-decision 20.2: Machine-readable output**

- **Options:**
  - (a) JSON only (machine-first)
  - (b) Text default, JSON flag
  - (c) Both always emitted
- **Decision:** **(b) Text default for humans, JSON via `--format=json` for machines**
- **Rationale:** JSON-only (a) is hostile to humans in the terminal. Both-always (c) produces noise — humans see extra JSON after every build. Text-default with JSON-on-demand (b) matches every mature developer tool (cargo, tsc, eslint) and is exactly what LLMs and LSP clients expect. The diagnostic tool surface is identical; only the encoding differs.
- **Human-facing text:** colored, ranged, with `│` margin rulers, underlines, and help text. See 20.1 example.
- **Machine-facing JSON (`--format=json`):**
  ```json
  {
    "diagnostics": [
      {
        "code": "F0308",
        "severity": "error",
        "message": "type mismatch",
        "primary": {
          "file": "src/auth.av",
          "line": 42,
          "column": 14,
          "span": {"start": {"line":42,"col":14}, "end": {"line":42,"col":18}},
          "label": "expected `User` here"
        },
        "secondary": [
          {
            "file": "src/auth.av",
            "line": 42,
            "column": 22,
            "span": {"start": {"line":42,"col":22}, "end": {"line":42,"col":36}},
            "label": "expected `UUID`, but returns `User`"
          }
        ],
        "expected": "UUID",
        "actual": "User",
        "suggestions": [
          {
            "confidence": "high",
            "description": "call `get_user(id)` instead of `get_user_id(id)`",
            "replacement": {
              "file": "src/auth.av",
              "span": {"start":{"line":42,"col":22},"end":{"line":42,"col":33}},
              "text": "get_user"
            }
          }
        ],
        "causality": [...],
        "docs_url": "https://docs.avra.dev/errors/F0308"
      }
    ]
  }
  ```
- **Stable schema:** the JSON format is versioned (`"schema_version": "1"`). Breaking changes bump the major schema version. Tools can pin to a schema version for stability.
- **LSP integration:** the language server consumes the same JSON (not its own separate format). One source of truth for diagnostics across CLI, LSP, and agent integrations (21.x).
- **Versioning:** both formats ship in v1.0. Schema version 1 is stable.

**Sub-decision 20.3: Suggestion confidence**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Binary — suggest or don't
  - (b) Graded (0.0-1.0) — high-confidence suggestions auto-fixable
  - (c) Always suggest with disclaimer
- **Decision:** **(b) Graded confidence — `high`, `medium`, `low` — with different UX treatment per level**
- **Rationale:** Binary confidence (a) conflates "this is almost certainly right" with "this might help" — users can't tell which to trust. Always-suggest-with-disclaimer (c) trains users to ignore the disclaimer. Graded confidence (b) maps suggestion certainty to appropriate UX: high-confidence autofixed, medium-confidence presented as choices, low-confidence offered as hints.
- **Three levels:**
  - **`high`** — the fix is provably correct (e.g., typo of a known identifier, missing import for a uniquely-named symbol, structural fix that preserves semantics). Auto-fixable by `avra fix` without user prompt. LSP offers as a default quick-fix.
  - **`medium`** — the fix is very likely correct but involves judgment (e.g., two possible similar function names, refactoring that changes structure). Offered as a choice; requires explicit acceptance.
  - **`low`** — the fix is a hint worth considering but unverified (e.g., "this might be what you meant", "try restructuring this way"). Shown to humans as help text; not applied automatically by tools.
- **How confidence is determined:**
  - Heuristic rules encoded in the compiler per error type
  - Typos: Levenshtein distance to existing identifiers — distance 1 = high, distance 2 = medium, distance 3+ = low
  - Missing imports: single unambiguous candidate = high, multiple candidates = medium, no candidates = low (no suggestion at all)
  - Structural fixes: conservative — require a proof that the fix preserves types to be high-confidence
- **Example (JSON):**
  ```json
  "suggestions": [
    { "confidence": "high", "description": "add missing import", ... },
    { "confidence": "medium", "description": "or did you mean `get_user`?", ... },
    { "confidence": "low", "description": "maybe use pattern matching here", ... }
  ]
  ```
- **Caution:** the compiler should err toward lower confidence. Aggressive "high" marking that produces incorrect autofixes destroys trust faster than any other compiler bug.
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0. Confidence levels may be refined over time based on user feedback and telemetry.

**Sub-decision 20.4: Autofix**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None
  - (b) `--autofix` applies high-confidence suggestions
  - (c) LSP-integrated code actions
  - (d) All of the above
- **Decision:** **(d) Autofix available everywhere — CLI, LSP, and agent API**
- **Rationale:** Autofix is a multiplier on the language's machine-friendliness (P11) and is critical for LLM workflow (21.x). It must work wherever code is edited: CLI (human runs `avra fix`), LSP (IDE offers code actions), and programmatic API (agents apply fixes as part of structured error-to-fix loops). None (a) leaves trivial fixes as manual toil. CLI only (b) skips LSP integration. LSP only (c) skips CI and scripting. All of the above is the right answer.
- **CLI:**
  ```
  avra check                      # report, no fixes
  avra fix                        # apply all high-confidence autofixes
  avra fix --include=medium       # also apply medium-confidence with prompts
  avra fix --dry-run              # show what would change without applying
  avra fix src/auth.av            # scoped to one file
  ```
- **LSP code actions:** IDE exposes high-confidence fixes as default quick-fix (light bulb or equivalent). Medium-confidence fixes listed under "more actions" menu. Low-confidence fixes not offered as actions — they appear only in the diagnostic's help text for humans to consider.
- **Agent API:**
  ```
  // Structured error-to-fix loop for LLM agents
  let diagnostics = avra.check(project)
  let high_confidence_fixes = diagnostics.suggestions.filter((s) -> s.confidence == .high)
  let result = avra.apply_fixes(project, high_confidence_fixes)
  // Agent can iterate: check → apply → check until converged
  ```
- **Fix atomicity:** each fix is a self-contained edit. Applying fix A should never invalidate fix B unless B explicitly depends on A. The compiler groups conflicting fixes and applies them in dependency order.
- **Transactional safety:** `avra fix` never partially applies. If any fix fails mid-stream, the entire run rolls back. Users either get all the fixes they asked for or none.
- **Git integration:** `avra fix --commit` creates a separate commit for the fixes with a standardized message. Useful for PR review where human changes and autofixes should be clearly separated.
- **Versioning:** CLI and agent API ship in v1.0. LSP integration ships with the language server in v1.0 or early v1.x.

**Sub-decision 20.5: Causality chains ("because")**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not implemented — single error per site
  - (b) Show why the error occurred (trace through type derivation)
  - (c) Multi-level causality with depth limit
- **Decision:** **(c) Multi-level causality chains with depth limit — "because" tracing through inference derivation**
- **Rationale:** Compilers typically report the error at the site where it manifests, but the actual cause often lies elsewhere — an earlier type annotation propagated through inference, an imported type with different semantics than expected, a generic parameter instantiated incorrectly. Showing only the surface error (a) forces users to mentally replay the type derivation. Single-level cause (b) is better but often insufficient. Multi-level causality with a depth limit (c) is the killer feature: the compiler shows the derivation trail, bounded to avoid overwhelming output.
- **Example:**
  ```
  error[F1024]: cannot use `user.email` in numeric context
    ┌─ src/billing.av:47:23
    │
  47│   let total = fee * user.email
    │                     ^^^^^^^^^^ `string` is not numeric
    │
    = because: `user.email` has type `string`
    = because: `User.email` is declared `string` at src/models/user.av:12
    = because: imported from `@myapp/models.user`
    = help: convert to int with `user.email.parse()?` if intentional
  ```
- **Structure:**
  - The `because` chain shows the inference derivation from the error site back to the root cause
  - Each level is a brief explanation with a location
  - Depth cap: 3 levels by default (configurable via `--causality-depth=N`)
  - Full derivation available with `--causality-depth=full` for debugging compiler diagnostics
- **What goes in the chain:**
  - Type inference steps (how did the compiler derive the type at the error site?)
  - Import resolution (which module, which declaration?)
  - Generic instantiation (what was `T` bound to?)
  - Trait selection (which impl was chosen, and why?)
- **In JSON:** `"causality": [{"level":0, "reason":"...", "location":{...}}, ...]`
- **LLM value:** multi-level causality is especially valuable for LLMs. Given a one-line error, the LLM might guess wrong about the cause. Given the full chain, the LLM can reason about the root cause directly and propose correct fixes. This is a central P11 (machine-readable) capability.
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0 with depth 3 default. Additional inference steps may be added as the type system evolves.

**Sub-decision 20.6: Internal compiler errors**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Raw Rust panic acceptable
  - (b) Always wrapped in `F9999` diagnostic
  - (c) Crash to safe state with telemetry
- **Decision:** **(b) Always wrapped in `F9999` with bug report instructions and captured context**
- **Rationale:** Raw panics (a) look like the compiler exploded — users don't know what to do, can't report effectively, and lose trust. Crash-to-telemetry (c) has privacy concerns (sends compiler-visible source code to a server) and requires infrastructure overhead. Wrapped ICE with clear user guidance (b) is the professional-tooling standard: users understand the compiler hit a bug, know exactly what to do, and can submit a high-quality report.
- **User experience:**
  ```
  error[F9999]: internal compiler error
    ┌─ src/auth.av:42:14
    │
  42│   let user: User = get_user_id(id)
    │             ^^^^^
    │
    = this is a bug in the Avra compiler, not your code
    = version: 0.3.7 (commit abc123de)
    = phase: type checker
    = context: resolving trait `Display` for type `Option<User>`
    
    How to report:
      1. Run `avra bug-report` to generate a minimal repro
      2. File at https://github.com/anthropic/avra/issues/new?template=ice
         with the generated reproducer attached
      3. Or copy this panic message + the command you ran
  ```
- **`avra bug-report` helper:**
  - Attempts to produce a minimal reproducer by progressively removing code until the ICE goes away
  - Captures compiler version, build flags, environment info
  - Produces a self-contained ZIP ready to attach to an issue
  - Asks before including any source — privacy-respecting
- **Captured context (when ICE fires):**
  - Compilation phase (lexer / parser / typechecker / borrow / codegen / link)
  - Inputs being processed (file, span, type being checked, etc.)
  - Stack trace (for compiler developers)
  - Graceful shutdown — no corrupted outputs left behind
- **Graceful degradation:** after an ICE in one file, the compiler attempts to continue processing other files (reporting additional errors may help the user triangulate). Complete failure only if ICE occurs before any file-level processing.
- **Privacy:** the compiler never uploads telemetry automatically. All reporting is explicit and user-initiated via `avra bug-report`.
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0. The ICE wrapper is trivial infrastructure; no excuse not to have it from day one.

---

### Axis 21: LLM and Agent Integration

**Sub-decision 21.1: Context export**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None — LLM reads source directly
  - (b) `forge context` command exports project structure
  - (c) LSP/MCP server as the primary interface
- **Decision:** **(b + c) `avra context` for static project briefings, LSP/MCP server for interactive agent work**
- **Rationale:** LLMs-read-source-directly (a) works but is inefficient — the LLM must reconstruct architecture from first principles every time. Two complementary mechanisms together cover the real workflows:
  - **`avra context`** — a one-shot export for giving an LLM a concise briefing of the project. Used when starting a chat, seeding a prompt, or onboarding a new agent session.
  - **LSP/MCP server** — interactive interface for agents doing real work, querying types, applying fixes, navigating symbols.
- **`avra context` output:**
  ```
  avra context                      # entire project summary
  avra context src/auth             # scoped to a directory
  avra context --symbols            # just the type/function signatures
  avra context --deploy             # deploy topology only
  avra context --budget=4000        # target token budget
  ```
  Produces a structured markdown document with:
  - Project name, version, and purpose (from `forge.toml`)
  - Dependency graph summary
  - Module structure (directory tree, abbreviated)
  - Public API surface (exported types, traits, functions with signatures)
  - Deploy topology (services, databases, secrets — names and types, no values)
  - Known error codes and conventions used in this project
  - A "gotchas" section populated from project-specific annotations (see 30.x)
- **LSP/MCP interface:** see 21.2. Agents use this for anything requiring real-time query or mutation.
- **Token budget awareness:** `avra context --budget=N` produces output that fits in N tokens, progressively summarizing lower-priority sections. Essential for agents with limited context windows.
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0. The format stabilizes post-feedback from real agent workflows.

**Sub-decision 21.2: MCP server integration**

- **Options:**
  - (a) No MCP integration
  - (b) Separate MCP server process
  - (c) LSP and MCP as same process
- **Decision:** **(c) LSP and MCP as the same process, sharing the compiler front-end and analyzer**
- **Rationale:** Two processes (b) means duplicate work (both parse the same project, both maintain symbol tables, both watch files) and opens synchronization issues. No MCP (a) gives up the agent integration story that's core to Avra's thesis. Unified LSP+MCP (c) is the architecturally correct answer: one process, one type-check engine, two protocol adapters.
- **Architecture:**
  ```
  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         avra-server (single process)       │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │  Compiler front-end + analyzer      │  │
  │  │  - lexer/parser                     │  │
  │  │  - type checker                     │  │
  │  │  - diagnostics                      │  │
  │  │  - cross-ref / symbol graph         │  │
  │  │  - autofix suggestions              │  │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │         │                    │             │
  │  ┌──────▼──────┐      ┌──────▼──────┐     │
  │  │ LSP adapter │      │ MCP adapter │     │
  │  └──────┬──────┘      └──────┬──────┘     │
  └─────────┼────────────────────┼─────────────┘
            │                    │
      ┌─────▼─────┐        ┌─────▼─────┐
      │   IDEs    │        │  Agents   │
      └───────────┘        └───────────┘
  ```
- **Shared services both protocols expose:**
  - Project structure (files, modules, symbols)
  - Type information at any span
  - Diagnostics with F-codes, causality, suggestions (20.1–20.5)
  - Autofix application (20.4)
  - Reference graph (who calls what, who imports what)
  - Live type inference results
- **MCP-specific capabilities (beyond LSP):**
  - **Structured error-to-fix loops:** `check → suggest → apply → recheck` as an API flow
  - **Semantic search:** "find all services that handle user auth", "find the function that computes billing"
  - **Project impact analysis:** "if I change this type, what breaks?"
  - **Capability and permission queries:** "what capabilities does this service need?"
  - **Deploy topology introspection:** "what infrastructure does this project require?"
- **LSP-specific capabilities:** standard LSP operations — hover, go-to-definition, completion, rename, code actions, formatting.
- **File watching:** single file-watcher in the server; both adapters see changes instantly.
- **Launching:** `avra-server` starts both protocols on configurable ports/transports. IDE plugins know how to talk LSP. Agent frameworks (Claude Code, Cursor, etc.) know how to talk MCP. The same server answers both.
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0. LSP capabilities mirror well-established LSP conventions; MCP capabilities stabilize during v1.0 based on real agent feedback.

**Sub-decision 21.3: Agent autonomy level**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Agent suggests, human approves
  - (b) Agent makes changes, human reviews
  - (c) Agent fully autonomous in sandbox
  - (d) Configurable per project
- **Decision:** **(d) Configurable per project via capability allow-lists, with safe defaults**
- **Rationale:** One-size-fits-all autonomy (a, b, or c) doesn't match real workflows — a solo developer exploring a prototype wants full agent autonomy in a sandbox, a team landing PRs wants review gates, a production hotfix flow wants strict suggest-only. Configurable per project (d) lets each team pick the boundary that matches their trust model. Critically, agents see and respect these boundaries — there's no way to silently exceed them.
- **Configuration in `forge.toml`:**
  ```toml
  [agent]
  default_autonomy = "review"              # "suggest", "review", or "autonomous"
  
  [agent.permissions]
  apply_autofixes         = true           # high-confidence fixes apply without review
  modify_source           = true           # can edit .av files
  add_dependencies        = "review"       # adding to forge.toml requires PR
  modify_deploy_block     = false          # deploy changes human-only
  modify_secrets          = false          # secret references human-only
  create_new_files        = true
  delete_files            = "review"
  run_tests               = true
  run_deploy              = false          # never auto-deploy
  access_external_apis    = false          # sandbox-only mode
  
  [agent.sandbox]
  scope = "worktree"                       # "worktree", "branch", or "fork"
  max_files_per_session = 50
  max_lines_per_diff = 2000
  ```
- **Three autonomy presets:**
  - **`suggest`** — agent can READ the project via MCP but cannot make changes; every proposal is a suggestion for human review
  - **`review`** (default) — agent makes changes in a branch/worktree; human reviews via PR or equivalent before merge
  - **`autonomous`** — agent makes changes directly in the working tree; reversible via git, but no review gate
- **Granular overrides:** the `[agent.permissions]` table lets projects customize per-action. `true` = allowed automatically; `false` = denied; `"review"` = allowed but requires explicit human approval.
- **Sandbox scopes:**
  - `worktree` — agent works in a separate Git worktree (branch, isolated working directory); review merges back
  - `branch` — agent works in the current working directory but on a dedicated branch
  - `fork` — agent works in a fork of the repo; review via upstream PR
- **Transparency:** agents running via MCP receive the current permission set in the protocol handshake. They explicitly cannot claim "I didn't know I wasn't allowed." Logs show every denied action.
- **Override:** a human operator can grant one-time permissions via MCP: "agent, you may modify the deploy block for this session only." The grant is logged and expires at session end.
- **Versioning:** configuration schema ships in v1.0. Additional permission axes may be added as new capability surfaces emerge.

**Sub-decision 21.4: LLM-friendly error format**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Machine-parseable JSON with fix suggestions
  - (b) Embedded context (types, inferred constraints)
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — formally ratifies that LLMs receive the full structured diagnostic surface from Axis 20**
- **Rationale:** This sub-decision is a conscious ratification of what Axis 20 already established, applied specifically to the LLM/agent surface. The JSON format from 20.2, the causality chains from 20.5, the graded suggestions from 20.3/20.4 — all of it flows to agents via the same MCP interface. LLMs get EVERYTHING humans get, with no second-class treatment.
- **What LLMs receive via MCP:**
  - **Full diagnostic** — F-code, severity, message, primary/secondary spans, source snippets
  - **Type context** — expected type, actual type, inferred constraints at every span
  - **Causality chain** — the multi-level "because" trail showing the root cause (20.5)
  - **Suggestions** — graded confidence fixes (20.3), each with explicit replacement text and span
  - **Application API** — the LLM can apply a suggestion directly via MCP, then re-check
  - **Structured errors** — error values at runtime include `kind` strings and context (12.x), machine-queryable
- **The structured error-to-fix loop:**
  ```
  // The canonical LLM workflow on Avra
  1. Agent queries MCP: check(project)
  2. Server returns diagnostics[] — each with code, message, suggestions, causality
  3. Agent filters: high_confidence = diagnostics.suggestions.filter(.confidence == high)
  4. Agent applies: mcp.apply_suggestion(high_confidence[0])
  5. Server re-checks, returns new diagnostics
  6. Loop until diagnostics.length == 0 OR no more high-confidence fixes available
  7. Agent reports remaining to human with explanations
  ```
- **Beyond errors — positive introspection:**
  - `mcp.describe_type(symbol)` — full type info including derived implementations
  - `mcp.describe_function(symbol)` — signature, contracts, call-site examples, test coverage
  - `mcp.references(symbol)` — all usages
  - `mcp.callers(fn)` / `mcp.callees(fn)` — call graph queries
  - `mcp.capabilities(module)` — what capabilities this module needs
- **LLM metric measurable:** "first-try correctness" — percentage of LLM-generated Avra code that type-checks on first try. This is the north-star metric for the agent experience.
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0 alongside the MCP server.

**Sub-decision 21.5: Spec compactness target**

- **Options:**
  - (a) No specific target
  - (b) Core spec fits in ~5K tokens, providers add ~500-1K each
  - (c) Minimal — everything introspectable via compiler
- **Decision:** **(b) Core spec target ~5K tokens; each standard provider adds ~500-1K tokens**
- **Rationale:** No target (a) leads to a specification that grows unbounded — LLMs pay cost every time they need to load it. Introspection-only (c) skips the benefit of a concise written reference — agents need a baseline document to reason from, and running compiler queries for every question is expensive. A compactness target (b) forces discipline in language design itself: features that can't be explained concisely get cut. Compactness IS a language-design constraint, not just a documentation goal.
- **Budget breakdown (target):**
  - Core spec (syntax, type system, memory model, concurrency, errors): ~5,000 tokens
  - Standard library index (names and 1-line descriptions): ~1,500 tokens
  - Each standard provider reference (`@std/http`, `@std/models`, etc.): ~500–1,000 tokens
- **Why this matters:** a fresh LLM session loading Avra's full core spec costs less than 2% of a standard 256K-context window. The entire stdlib fits in 10% of that. Agents can carry the full reference in context alongside the user's code and still have most of the window for actual work.
- **Discipline this enforces on the language:**
  - Feature must be explainable concisely; novel-but-verbose features lose to simpler alternatives
  - Orthogonality (every feature serves multiple purposes) wins over specialized features
  - Consistency across subsystems (same operator meaning `?` in types, patterns, and expressions) saves tokens
  - Defaults over knobs — every configuration point is budget
- **How compactness is measured:** Avra maintains `spec/core.md`, `spec/stdlib.md`, and per-provider spec files, with a CI check enforcing token budgets. Features that would blow the budget require explicit budget approval during design review.
- **Introspection complements the spec:** the spec gives LLMs the language; `avra context` (21.1) gives LLMs the project; MCP gives LLMs real-time queries. All three together provide complete operability.
- **Versioning:** budget is a design constraint from v1.0. Growth is allowed for new features but each feature must justify its cost.

---

### Axis 22: Models, Services, and Auto-projection

**Sub-decision 22.1: Model declarations**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Core language feature
  - (b) Provider-supplied (`@std/model`)
  - (c) Library-based (like ORMs)
- **Decision:** **(b) Provider-supplied via `@std/models` — registers the `model` keyword and derives**
- **Rationale:** Core-language (a) bakes one opinion about models into the language forever, making experimentation impossible and alienating users who want different semantics (event-sourcing, document, graph). Pure library (c) loses the compile-time projection power (can't derive migrations, OpenAPI, etc.). Provider-supplied (b) is the right answer: `@std/models` IS a first-party, curated, de-facto standard provider — but because it's a provider, alternative model systems are possible. Users can choose `@std/models` for classic relational, `@community/event_model` for event-sourced, `@myorg/custom_models` for domain-specific.
- **Provider registration:** `@std/models` registers:
  - The `model` keyword with a custom parser for field declarations, indexes, relations
  - `@derive Model` which performs all the projections (22.2)
  - Build hooks for schema generation and migration diffs
- **Example:**
  ```
  use @std/models.{model, Id, Relation}
  
  model User {
    id: Id<User>                // auto-generated primary key
    email: string where it.contains("@")   // refinement from 14.1
    name: string
    created_at: Timestamp @auto
    
    posts: Relation<many, Post>    // to-many relation, reverse-inferred on Post
  }
  
  model Post {
    id: Id<Post>
    title: string
    content: string
    author: Relation<one, User>    // to-one relation; FK auto-generated
    published_at: Timestamp?
  }
  ```
- **Why not a language primitive:** the model concept is rich and contested — there's no single "right" answer about identity semantics (UUID vs ULID vs snowflake), migration strategy (auto vs manual), relation representation (FK vs junction table vs embedded). Making models a provider lets the ecosystem evolve multiple approaches without fragmenting the language core.
- **Alternative model providers:** the door is open for `@community/event_model` (event-sourced aggregates), `@community/document_model` (MongoDB-style documents), `@community/graph_model` (nodes and edges) — all using the same provider machinery.
- **Versioning:** `@std/models` ships in v1.0 as part of the MVP stdlib.

**Sub-decision 22.2: Auto-projections from models**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None — user writes each projection
  - (b) Schema, OpenAPI, admin UI, validators auto-generated
  - (c) All projections by default, opt-out per projection
- **Decision:** **(c) All projections generated by default, opt-out per projection via annotations**
- **Rationale:** Manual projection writing (a) is the "write 6 pieces of glue code per model" toil that motivates Avra in the first place. Auto-generation with no opt-out (b) forces projections users don't want (e.g., "don't expose this model via REST"). Default-on with per-projection opt-out (c) gives the magic by default while respecting user intent — exactly the same pattern as observability (19.5) and consistent with the philosophy "magic is visible and escapable."
- **Projections generated by default from a `model` declaration:**
  - **Database schema** — table + columns + indexes + constraints
  - **Migrations** — diff vs the previous schema (see 22.3)
  - **Validators** — input validation functions derived from refinements and types
  - **REST CRUD endpoints** — `GET /users`, `GET /users/:id`, `POST /users`, `PATCH /users/:id`, `DELETE /users/:id`
  - **OpenAPI schema** — for the REST surface, includes all types and endpoints
  - **GraphQL schema** — query and mutation types, resolvers
  - **Admin UI** — auto-generated CRUD UI at `/admin/users`, honors UI hints (see 23.x)
  - **Serializers** — JSON, MessagePack, protobuf (where applicable)
  - **Test fixtures** — factory functions for use in tests
  - **LLM tool descriptions** — MCP-formatted tool descriptions so agents can query/mutate via MCP
- **Opt-out per projection:**
  ```
  model User {
    id: Id<User>
    email: string
    name: string
    password_hash: string
    api_key: Secret<string>
    
    @projections {
      rest: { expose: [id, email, name] }    // exclude password_hash, api_key
      graphql: { mutations: off }            // read-only via GraphQL
      admin_ui: off                          // no admin UI for this model
      serializer.json: { exclude: [password_hash, api_key] }
    }
  }
  ```
- **Security-sensitive fields auto-excluded:** any field of type `Secret<T>` is automatically excluded from all public projections (REST, GraphQL, serializers, admin UI). Password hashes and API keys must be explicitly opt-in to cross into public surfaces — same safety pattern as 19.4.
- **Customization:** each projection supports per-projection customization via annotations. Full control is available; defaults cover the 80% case.
- **Inspection:** `avra explain model User` prints every generated projection and what it contains. Nothing hidden.
- **Versioning:** the set of projections ships in v1.0. Additional projections (e.g., new frontend frameworks, new protocols) may be added by community providers.

**Sub-decision 22.3: Migrations**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Manual SQL
  - (b) Auto-generated from model diffs
  - (c) Diff-generated with user review
- **Decision:** **(c) Auto-diffed against prior schema, emitted as reviewable migration files for human commit**
- **Rationale:** Manual SQL (a) is error-prone toil — developers rewrite what the compiler already knows. Fully automatic (b) is dangerous — schema changes can cause data loss, downtime, or lock storms, and applying them without review is irresponsible. Diff-generated with review (c) is the right answer: the compiler does the diff work, the human commits and reviews the migration file, the migration tool applies reviewed migrations in order.
- **Workflow:**
  ```
  # Developer changes a model (adds a field, renames, adds index)
  # Then runs:
  avra models migrate
  
  # Output:
  # Detected changes to 2 models since last migration
  # Generated: migrations/20260418123045_add_user_phone.sql
  # 
  # Changes:
  #   + User.phone: string?  (nullable, safe to add)
  #   + Post.published_idx: index on (published_at)  (safe to add)
  #   
  # Review the generated migration before committing.
  # To apply: avra models migrate apply
  ```
- **Generated migration file:**
  ```sql
  -- migrations/20260418123045_add_user_phone.sql
  -- Generated by avra models migrate
  -- Models changed: User, Post
  
  -- Add User.phone (nullable)
  ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN phone VARCHAR NULL;
  
  -- Add index on Post.published_at
  CREATE INDEX posts_published_idx ON posts (published_at);
  
  -- Reverse migration (for rollback)
  -- DROP INDEX posts_published_idx;
  -- ALTER TABLE users DROP COLUMN phone;
  ```
- **Safety classifications:** each change is classified:
  - **Safe** — additive, reversible (add nullable column, add index, add table)
  - **Caution** — potentially data-losing (drop column, change type)
  - **Dangerous** — requires downtime or complex steps (rename column, split/merge tables, non-null required field)
- **Dangerous changes require explicit strategy:**
  ```
  model User {
    @rename(from: "full_name") name: string
    @backfill(with: "unknown") location: string    // new non-null field, how to fill existing rows
  }
  ```
  The migration tool respects these annotations and generates appropriate migration steps (rename column or create-then-drop, backfill data, etc.). Without the annotation, dangerous changes are refused with a clear error.
- **Apply strategy:**
  - `avra models migrate apply` — applies pending migrations in order, transactional where possible
  - `avra models migrate status` — shows which migrations are applied, pending, or reverted
  - `avra models migrate rollback` — runs reverse migrations
  - Deploy integration: `avra deploy` runs pending migrations before starting new service instances (blue-green-safe)
- **Versioning:** ships with `@std/models` in v1.0.

**Sub-decision 22.4: Service declarations**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not in language
  - (b) `service` keyword with CRUD auto-generation
  - (c) Provider-defined
- **Decision:** **(c) Provider-defined — `@std/services` registers the `service` keyword**
- **Rationale:** Same reasoning as models (22.1): keeping the core language lean while letting providers own rich concepts. No service support (a) leaves this critical primitive to library glue code — where drift and inconsistency thrive. Baking `service` into the language (b) freezes one opinion about services (what about gRPC? message queues? WebSockets?). Provider-supplied (c) is consistent with the overall architecture.
- **`@std/services` provides:**
  - The `service` keyword
  - `@derive Service` for an existing type or model
  - Default middleware stack (auth, rate limiting, logging, tracing, request ID propagation)
  - Integration with deploy topology extraction (19.3) — services show up automatically in deploy plans
- **Example:**
  ```
  use @std/services.{service, middleware, authenticated}
  use @std/models.{Id}
  
  service UserService {
    base_path: "/users"
    middleware: [authenticated, rate_limit(100.per_minute)]
    
    // Auto-generated CRUD from the User model
    model: User
    
    // Custom endpoints beyond CRUD
    endpoint search(query: string) -> List<User> {
      // implementation
    }
    
    endpoint reset_password(user_id: Id<User>) -> Result<(), Error> {
      // implementation
    }
  }
  ```
- **What gets auto-generated from a service declaration:**
  - Routing table (maps HTTP path + method to handler)
  - OpenAPI schema for the service
  - Client SDK (optional, opt-in per service)
  - Observability spans for each endpoint (19.5)
  - Deploy manifest entry (shows up in `deploy` block topology)
  - Agent-readable tool description (MCP format)
- **Middleware composition:** `middleware: [a, b, c]` applies in order — `a` first. Standard middleware in `@std/services` covers auth, rate limiting, CORS, request ID, tracing, logging. Custom middleware is just a function matching the middleware signature.
- **Service-level concerns vs model-level concerns:**
  - Model: data shape, persistence, validation, migration
  - Service: routing, middleware, auth, rate limiting, API versioning, operational metadata
  - The two are orthogonal; a model can back multiple services with different middleware stacks
- **Alternative service providers:** `@community/grpc_services` for gRPC, `@community/websocket_services` for real-time, `@std/cron` for scheduled jobs — all using the same provider machinery and topology extraction.
- **Versioning:** `@std/services` ships in v1.0 alongside `@std/models`.

---

### Axis 23: UI and Mobile

**Status: DEFERRED to a dedicated design session.**

UI is a substantial surface area that warrants its own concentrated design pass rather than a rushed batch of decisions. It intersects with reactivity, state management, mobile native codegen, web WASM compilation, accessibility, and the model-projection system (22.x) in ways that deserve careful individual treatment.

**Placeholder principles (from user memories, pending formal ratification):**
- Three-tier model-driven approach (just show it / show it like this / I'll draw it myself)
- `mut` inside `view` blocks becomes reactive
- Channels for live data
- Mobile: phased — API + auto-generated Swift/Kotlin bindings first, native codegen later
- Reserved keywords in v1.0: `view`, `render`, `show`, `state` (at view-block scope)

**When this axis is revisited, it should cover:**
- 23.1 UI system architecture (three-tier details, composition model, component authoring)
- 23.2 Reactivity mechanism (`mut` in `view` semantics, dependency tracking, rerender granularity)
- 23.3 Mobile support phasing (bindings vs native codegen, platform-specific concerns)
- 23.4 Web target (Avra → WASM, shared types across boundary, bundle size, SSR vs SPA)
- Additional: styling system, accessibility, animation, forms, routing, state management beyond view-local

**Reserved in v1.0 so as not to foreclose future design:**
- Keywords: `view`, `render`, `show`, `state`
- Annotation prefixes: `@ui`, `@view`
- The `with` keyword in UI-hint position (distinct from the structural-update `with` expression in 9.x)

---

### Axis 24: Testing and Verification

**Sub-decision 24.1: Test framework**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Separate test framework library
  - (b) Built-in `test` and `describe` keywords
  - (c) Provider (`@std/test`)
- **Decision:** **(c) Provider-supplied via `@std/test` — registers `test` and `describe` keywords**
- **Rationale:** Separate library (a) reduces language surface but loses the first-class integration (discoverability, `avra test` CLI, type-level generators). Core keywords (b) freeze one testing philosophy into the language forever. Provider (c) is consistent with the model/service pattern (22.x) — `@std/test` ships curated and de-facto standard, but alternative testing providers (property-heavy, BDD, snapshot-first) can coexist.
- **`@std/test` provides:**
  - `test` keyword for individual test cases
  - `describe` keyword for grouping
  - `setup` and `teardown` for fixtures
  - Common assertions (`assert`, `assert_eq`, `assert_matches`, `assert_throws`)
  - Integration with `avra test` CLI
  - Parallel and sequential test runners
  - Snapshot testing (24.3)
  - Property-based testing (24.2)
  - Test-time dependency injection (24.4)
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  use @std/test.{test, describe, assert, assert_eq}
  
  describe "User.validate" {
    test "accepts valid email" {
      let result = User.validate("alice@example.com", "secret123")
      assert result.is_ok()
    }
    
    test "rejects empty password" {
      let result = User.validate("alice@example.com", "")
      assert_eq(result, Err(ValidationError.EmptyPassword))
    }
    
    setup each {
      db.truncate_all()
    }
  }
  ```
- **Test discovery:** files ending in `_test.av` or under `tests/` are test sources. Tests don't compile into the production binary — `avra build` excludes them; `avra test` compiles them separately.
- **Test-only code:** `@test_only` annotation on functions/types restricts them to test code. Attempting to import a `@test_only` symbol from production code is a compile error.
- **CLI:**
  ```
  avra test                          # run all tests
  avra test auth                     # run tests in module matching "auth"
  avra test --filter="email"         # run tests with "email" in name
  avra test --parallel=8             # concurrency level
  avra test --watch                  # re-run on file changes
  avra test --coverage               # emit coverage report
  ```
- **Alternative providers:** `@community/bdd_test` for behavior-driven, `@community/hedgehog` for property-focused, etc. — all registering their own test keywords.
- **Versioning:** `@std/test` ships in v1.0.

**Sub-decision 24.2: Property-based testing**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not supported
  - (b) Via library
  - (c) Built-in with type-driven generators
- **Decision:** **(c) Built-in with type-driven generators — `Arbitrary<T>` auto-derived for most types**
- **Rationale:** Not supporting property testing (a) misses one of the highest-value testing techniques, especially for LLM-authored code (LLMs can write property assertions faster than example tests). Library-based (b) works but requires manual generator authoring per type. Built-in with auto-derivation (c) is the differentiator: given any type, the compiler automatically derives a generator, and writing a property test is a one-line change from writing a regular test.
- **Auto-derivation for:**
  - Primitives: `int`, `float`, `bool`, `string`, `byte`, `char`
  - Collections: `List<T>`, `Map<K,V>`, `Set<T>` (recursively arbitrary)
  - Enums and unions: uniform over variants, recursively arbitrary on payloads
  - Records/types: field-wise arbitrary
  - Refinement types: rejection sampling against the predicate (falls back to user-provided generator if rejection rate too high)
  - User types: automatic unless user provides a custom `Arbitrary<T>` impl
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  use @std/test.{test, property, assert}
  
  property "reverse is involutive" {
    for_all (xs: List<int>) {
      assert xs.reverse().reverse() == xs
    }
  }
  
  property "sorted list is non-decreasing" {
    for_all (xs: List<int>) {
      let sorted = xs.sort()
      for i in 0..(sorted.length - 1) {
        assert sorted[i] <= sorted[i + 1]
      }
    }
  }
  ```
- **Shrinking:** when a property fails, the framework automatically shrinks the counterexample — finds the minimal input that still fails. Shrinking is derived alongside Arbitrary: smaller lists, smaller numbers, fewer variants.
- **Custom generators:**
  ```
  // Override auto-derivation for domain constraints
  impl Arbitrary for Email {
    fn arbitrary() -> Email {
      Email.new("{username()}@{domain()}")   // always valid emails
    }
  }
  ```
- **Iteration count:** configurable per property; default is 100 iterations. Increase with `property "..." with (iterations: 10000)`. Deterministic seeding for reproducibility.
- **Integration with refinement types (14.1):** refined types (`type Positive = int where it > 0`) generate only valid values. The framework knows the predicate and either samples from a valid range (when the predicate is SMT-analyzable) or uses rejection sampling.
- **LLM value:** property tests are incredibly concise and convey strong specifications. An LLM writing `property "sort is idempotent" { for_all (xs) { assert xs.sort() == xs.sort().sort() } }` tests a deep invariant with one line.
- **Versioning:** ships with `@std/test` in v1.0.

**Sub-decision 24.3: Snapshot testing**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not supported
  - (b) Via library
  - (c) First-class with auto-approval flow
- **Decision:** **(c) First-class snapshot testing with structured diff review and explicit-approval workflow**
- **Rationale:** Snapshot tests are invaluable for rendered output (HTML, JSON, generated code, serialized data) and for regression-proofing data structures. Library-only (b) works but the common pain point — "how do I review and approve snapshot changes safely?" — needs tooling support to be reliable. Built-in first-class (c) solves the workflow problem: snapshots live in a standard location, diffs are reviewed systematically, approvals are explicit.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  use @std/test.{test, assert_snapshot}
  
  test "user serialization" {
    let user = User {
      id: UUID.fixed("abc..."),
      email: "alice@example.com",
      name: "Alice"
    }
    assert_snapshot(user.to_json(), "user_json")
  }
  ```
- **Storage:** snapshots stored in `tests/snapshots/<test_file>/<snapshot_name>.snap`. Always committed to version control — they ARE the test expectations.
- **First run:** snapshot doesn't exist → test records the value, writes the snapshot file, passes. This makes initial test authoring frictionless.
- **Subsequent runs:** compare produced value to recorded snapshot. Match → pass. Mismatch → fail with a structured diff.
- **Review workflow:**
  ```
  avra test                          # runs, reports 3 snapshot mismatches
  avra test --review-snapshots       # opens interactive review
                                     # shows each diff, asks accept/reject/skip
  avra test --accept-snapshots       # accepts all new/changed snapshots (use in PRs)
  avra test --accept-snapshot=user_json   # accept one specific snapshot
  ```
- **CI mode:** in CI, `avra test` fails on ANY snapshot mismatch (no auto-accept). PRs that change snapshots must explicitly include the updated snapshot files. Reviewers see the snapshot diff alongside code changes — no surprise behavioral changes.
- **Snapshot sanitization:** absolute paths, timestamps, random UUIDs, and other volatile values are a common pain. `assert_snapshot` accepts a `sanitize:` option:
  ```
  assert_snapshot(report, "user_report", sanitize: [
    pattern("\\d{4}-\\d{2}-\\d{2}T.*Z") -> "<TIMESTAMP>",
    pattern("[0-9a-f]{36}") -> "<UUID>",
  ])
  ```
- **Inline snapshots (alternative):**
  ```
  test "short output" {
    assert_inline_snapshot(compute_summary(data), r#"
      users: 42
      active: 38
      churned: 4
    "#)
  }
  ```
  For small snapshots where the expected output reads well in the test file itself. Updated in place by `avra test --accept-snapshots`.
- **Versioning:** ships with `@std/test` in v1.0.

**Sub-decision 24.4: Mocking / dependency injection**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Manual via interfaces
  - (b) Auto-generated mocks from traits
  - (c) Test-mode flag for non-deterministic functions
- **Decision:** **(b + c) Auto-generated mocks from traits AND test-mode injection for non-deterministic functions**
- **Rationale:** Manual interfaces (a) are fine but require the programmer to manually write mock implementations — toil. Auto-mock from traits (b) lets tests stub dependencies with minimal ceremony. Test-mode injection for non-deterministic functions (c) addresses the specific case from 13.5 (clock, random, etc.) cleanly. Both together cover the full space: general dependency injection via traits, targeted determinism for specific stdlib functions.
- **Auto-generated mocks from traits:**
  ```
  use @std/test.{test, mock, expect}
  
  trait UserRepository {
    fn find(id: UUID) -> User?
    fn save(user: User) -> Result<(), DbError>
  }
  
  test "create user calls save once" {
    let repo: UserRepository = mock(UserRepository) {
      find(id) -> null
      save(user) -> Ok(())
    }
    
    let service = UserService { repo }
    service.create_user(email, password)?
    
    expect(repo).called_once("save")
    expect(repo).called_with("save", {|u| u.email == email})
  }
  ```
  The `mock(Trait) { ... }` form auto-generates an impl of the trait with the stub bodies. Recorded calls are queryable via `expect()` for assertions.
- **Test-mode injection for non-deterministic stdlib (from 13.5):**
  ```
  use @std/test.{test, TestClock, TestRandom, with_context}
  
  test "token expires after 1 hour" {
    with_context {
      Clock = TestClock.at(Timestamp.from_iso("2025-01-01T00:00:00Z"))
      Random = TestRandom.seeded(42)
    } {
      let token = issue_token(user_id)
      
      TestClock.advance(59.minutes)
      assert token.is_valid()
      
      TestClock.advance(2.minutes)
      assert !token.is_valid()
    }
  }
  ```
  Inside the `with_context` block, calls to `Time.now()` use `TestClock`, calls to `Random.int()` use `TestRandom`, etc. Outside the block, the real implementations are used. This makes time-dependent and randomness-dependent code fully testable without modifying production code to accept Clock parameters.
- **How the test-mode injection works:** stdlib functions that would otherwise call the real clock/RNG check thread-local context for overrides. Zero overhead in production (the check is a branch that the compiler elides when no overrides are registered). Inside `with_context`, the thread-local is set to the test instance.
- **Escape hatch — real mode:** `with_context { Clock = RealClock } { ... }` opts back into real time/random even inside test code. Useful for integration tests that genuinely need real timing.
- **Auto-mock limitations:**
  - Cannot mock concrete functions (only traits)
  - Cannot mock generic functions that use types the mock doesn't know about
  - Mocks are test-only — using `mock()` in production code is a compile error
- **Versioning:** ships with `@std/test` in v1.0.

---

## Part 6: Compiler and Bootstrap

The compiler itself — how it's built, how it evolves.

---

### Axis 25: Bootstrap Strategy

**Sub-decision 25.1: Stage 0 language**

- **Options:**
  - (a) C
  - (b) Rust
  - (c) Self-hosted from day 1
- **Decision:** **(c) Fully self-hosted — Avra compiles Avra, no external bootstrap language**
- **Rationale:** External bootstrap languages (a, b) create a permanent secondary maintenance burden: every language change must be implemented twice (in the bootstrap and in the self-hosted compiler), they drift, and the bootstrap becomes a covert specification that may diverge from the self-hosted reality. Fully self-hosted (c) is architecturally cleaner and reflects the project's current reality: the self-hosted Avra compiler exists and is the source of truth. The language defines itself in itself.
- **Current state:** the self-hosted Avra compiler exists with working `check` and `build` commands. This compiler is the artifact. Further work is improving this code, not writing a separate bootstrap in another language.
- **Compiler source:** written entirely in Avra. Uses the language's own features (traits, enums, shapes, models, providers) to express the compiler's data structures and passes.
- **Bootstrapping from nothing:** a pre-built Avra compiler binary is the seed for new platforms. Building from source on a new platform uses an existing Avra binary (cross-compiled or downloaded) to compile the source of the newest compiler. This is the same pattern used by any self-hosted language (Go, Rust, OCaml — all now compile themselves from themselves).
- **Reproducibility:** builds are reproducible because the compiler is deterministic — same input source + same compiler version → byte-identical output. A new platform bootstrap uses a trusted Avra binary (signed, content-addressed) to compile new binaries.
- **No dual maintenance:** language features are implemented once, in Avra, in the actual compiler users run. There is no second implementation to keep in sync.
- **TRD output:** this design questionnaire feeds into a technical review document (TRD) intended to guide improvements to the existing self-hosted compiler code. The questionnaire defines WHAT the language should be; the TRD will address HOW to evolve the current compiler to match.
- **Versioning:** the language and its compiler co-evolve. Compiler version is the language version.

**Sub-decision 25.2: Bootstrap phases**

- **Options:**
  - (a) One-shot — Stage 0 compiles final compiler
  - (b) Two-phase — Stage 0 → simple Avra compiler → optimized Avra compiler
  - (c) Three-phase — copy-everything → refcounted → optimized
- **Decision:** **None — no phased bootstrap; the self-hosted compiler IS the compiler**
- **Rationale:** Phased bootstrapping is a strategy for getting from "no compiler exists" to "self-hosted compiler exists." That problem is already solved for Avra — a working self-hosted compiler exists. There are no phases to traverse; there is one compiler, and it improves over time through normal software evolution.
- **What evolution looks like WITHOUT phases:**
  - Compiler improvements ship as normal commits to the compiler source (which is itself Avra code)
  - Each new compiler version is built by the previous compiler version
  - Memory model, codegen, and feature implementations evolve in place
  - No "throwaway" stages, no "scaffolding" to retire
- **Starting point for improvements:** the existing self-hosted compiler code. The TRD produced from this questionnaire will identify specific improvements — architectural refactors, feature additions, performance work, codegen improvements — and those land as code changes in the existing codebase.
- **Memory model migration within the existing compiler:** if the compiler itself needs its own memory representation improved (e.g., from a simpler GC-style approach to the full refcount model), that's a gradual refactoring of the compiler codebase, not a new bootstrap phase. The compiler compiles itself throughout the transition.
- **Fixed-point check (still applies):** when a compiler version compiles its own source code, the resulting binary should be byte-identical across iterations. Compiler(vN) compiles Compiler(vN) → binary B; B recompiles Compiler(vN) → binary B'; B must equal B'. This is the standard self-hosting sanity check, independent of any bootstrap story.
- **Platform porting:** porting to a new platform uses an existing Avra binary (cross-compiled for the target platform, or run in an emulator) to compile the compiler source on the target. No bootstrap language is involved.
