- **Versioning:** version numbering reflects language+compiler versions, not bootstrap phases. A single version string identifies both the language spec implemented and the compiler binary producing it.

**Sub-decision 25.3: Phase 1 memory model**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Copy-everything (slow, correct)
  - (b) Naive RC
  - (c) Full target model (risky)
- **Decision:** **None — no bootstrap phases; the compiler uses whatever memory model the language currently implements**
- **Rationale:** With fully self-hosted compilation and no phased bootstrap, there is no separate "Phase 1 memory model" to decide. The compiler source is Avra code; the compiler uses Avra's memory model as it exists in the current version. Evolving the memory model (9.x) is a normal language-and-compiler evolution task, not a phase transition.
- **Relationship to memory-model decisions (9.x):**
  - Axis 9 defines the target memory model: scoped per abstraction level, refcount at app-level, ownership at systems-level, etc.
  - The current compiler code uses whatever subset of that model is currently implemented
  - As the compiler gains support for more sophisticated memory features, those features become available to the compiler's own source — it self-improves
- **Memory model changes in the compiler codebase:** these are refactors, tracked as normal code changes. Example: migrating a data structure from copy-on-write to refcounted + NLL is a PR with tests, not a phase migration.
- **TRD relevance:** the TRD produced from this questionnaire will identify specific memory-model-related improvements to the existing compiler code — e.g., "switch compiler's AST representation to use the structural sharing pattern from 9.23," "apply Perceus-style refcount elision in the type-check pass." These are concrete code tasks, not scheduled phases.
- **Versioning:** the compiler's internal memory usage evolves as language features are added. No phase gating.

**Sub-decision 25.4: LLVM integration**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Inkwell (Rust wrapper)
  - (b) LLVM C API (`libLLVM`)
  - (c) Custom IR + codegen
- **Decision:** **(b) LLVM C API via `@std/llvm` — Avra bindings to `libLLVM` through Avra's FFI**
- **Rationale:** Inkwell (a) is a Rust-specific wrapper — irrelevant to Avra since there is no Rust bootstrap. Custom IR (c) is an enormous multi-year investment competing with Avra's actual mission (none of the optimizer, multi-target codegen, debugger integration, sanitizers, etc. would be free). LLVM C API (b) is the natural choice: LLVM's actual interface, directly usable from any language with C FFI, and `@std/llvm` is Avra's binding to it.
- **`@std/llvm`:** Avra bindings to `libLLVM`, built on the FFI system (15.x). This is one of the most important validations of the FFI design — if FFI can wrap LLVM cleanly, the FFI system is vindicated.
- **Why LLVM over custom IR:** LLVM gives us for free:
  - Optimizer (hundreds of person-years of passes)
  - Multi-target codegen (x86, ARM, RISC-V, WASM, GPU)
  - Debugger integration (DWARF)
  - Profiler integration
  - Link-time optimization
  - Sanitizers (AddressSanitizer, ThreadSanitizer, etc.)
  Building any of this from scratch is a wrong decision at this stage. Avra benefits more from being a good LLVM citizen than from building a custom backend.
- **LLVM version:** currently 19+. Pinned in compiler source / `forge.toml` to avoid silent divergence as LLVM evolves.
- **Linking approach:**
  - Compiler links `libLLVM` as a dynamic library via `@std/llvm`
  - Object emission uses LLVM's built-in target machine codegen (no shell-out to external `llc`)
  - Linking the final executable can use LLVM's `lld` or the system linker, configurable
- **Register allocator pressure (from user memories):** large enum matches can create register pressure on LLVM's allocator. Mitigations in Avra codegen: careful IR structure, potentially splitting large match arms into separate functions at the IR level. This is an optimization applied inside the self-hosted compiler's codegen pass.
- **Long-term flexibility:** LLVM integration is an implementation detail. If a better backend emerges, Avra's language surface doesn't change; only the codegen backend does. `@std/llvm` could coexist with `@std/cranelift` or other backends; the compiler could select based on build configuration.
- **Versioning:** `@std/llvm` bindings ship with the compiler. Version compatibility with LLVM is documented per compiler release.

---

### Axis 26: Compiler Architecture

**Sub-decision 26.1: Compiler pipeline**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Traditional phases (lex → parse → check → codegen)
  - (b) Component-based (each feature registers checker and codegen)
  - (c) Query-based (demand-driven like Rust's rustc)
- **Decision:** **(b + c) Component-based registration, query-based execution — the self-hosting architecture philosophy**
- **Rationale:** Traditional phases (a) are simple but rigid — adding a feature requires threading changes through every phase. Pure component-based (b) without a unifying execution model fragments the type checker. Pure query-based (c) is powerful but can lead to spaghetti if every feature hooks arbitrarily. Combining them is the right answer: features are registered as components (each with its own lexer extension, parser, checker, codegen — the self-hosting philosophy from user memories), and the compiler executes via a query framework that demands what it needs. Features compose cleanly; the query framework handles ordering, caching, and incrementalism.
- **Component structure:** each language feature is a self-describing module:
  ```
  component enum_feature {
    keywords: ["enum"]
    parser: parse_enum_declaration
    type_rules: [enum_exhaustiveness, enum_variance]
    codegen: emit_enum_ir
    diagnostics: [F1200_non_exhaustive_match, F1201_unknown_variant]
  }
  ```
  Adding a new feature means dropping in a component file; no scattered edits across phases.
- **Query framework:** the compiler exposes typed queries over the program:
  - `type_of(expr) -> TypeInfo`
  - `resolve_import(module_path, item) -> Option<Symbol>`
  - `trait_impl(trait, type) -> Option<ImplInfo>`
  - `dependencies_of(decl) -> List<Decl>`
  - `ir_for(fn) -> LLVMFunction`
  Every query is a function from inputs to outputs, memoized in the cache. Calling a query transparently materializes the result on first use and returns cached results thereafter.
- **Execution flow:**
  1. User requests an outcome (build, check, lsp-hover)
  2. Outcome request expands to queries (`ir_for(main) → type_of(main) → resolve_import(...) → ...`)
  3. Query framework evaluates demand-driven, top-down
  4. Results memoized in the cache keyed by inputs
  5. Subsequent queries hit cache when inputs are unchanged
- **Why this matters for the Avra thesis:** the component-based architecture is the self-describing language principle from user memories — "each language feature as a self-describing component with its own lexer, parser, errors, and codegen." The query framework makes that architecturally tractable at scale.
- **Versioning:** component + query architecture ships as compiler improvements to the existing self-hosted codebase. Introduced incrementally through refactoring — not a wholesale rewrite.

**Sub-decision 26.2: Incremental compilation**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None — always full rebuild
  - (b) Module-level incremental
  - (c) Fine-grained (per declaration)
- **Decision:** **(c) Fine-grained per-declaration incremental via query cache**
- **Rationale:** Full rebuild (a) is untenable for productive editing — a 50k LOC project taking 10+ seconds per save destroys the LLM + LSP workflow. Module-level (b) is an improvement but a single declaration change invalidates the whole module. Fine-grained per-declaration (c) is the right answer: the query cache keyed on declaration hashes means editing one function only recompiles that function (and things downstream that actually depend on it). This is how rustc and Roslyn achieve sub-second iteration in massive codebases.
- **How it works:**
  - Each declaration (`fn`, `type`, `trait`, etc.) has a stable query key: `(module_path, decl_name, input_hash)`
  - The type of a declaration depends on the types of its dependencies — explicit edges in the query graph
  - On file change, only declarations whose input hash changed are re-evaluated
  - Downstream dependents are invalidated transitively; but independent siblings are untouched
- **Example:**
  ```
  // Change line 5 of user.av (a comment)
  → No query keys change; nothing invalidates
  
  // Change line 10 of user.av (body of User.greet)
  → ir_for(User::greet) invalidates
  → type_of(User::greet) unchanged (signature same)
  → Callers of User::greet NOT recompiled (only IR changes, not type)
  
  // Change line 12 of user.av (signature of User::greet)
  → type_of(User::greet) invalidates
  → All callers transitively re-checked
  → Only the actually-affected callers; the rest of the program untouched
  ```
- **Storage:** cache stored in `.avra/cache/` with content-addressed keys. Shared between CLI and LSP — compile once, serve both. Persisted across process restarts.
- **Cache invalidation rules:** the cache is conservatively invalidated by:
  - Input hash change (source file content hash for inputs this query depends on)
  - Compiler version change (bump wipes cache)
  - Feature flag change (opted-in experiments invalidate affected queries)
- **Versioning:** fine-grained incrementalism is the target. Current compiler may start with coarser granularity; refinement is ongoing work identified in the TRD.

**Sub-decision 26.3: Parallelism in compiler**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Single-threaded
  - (b) Per-module parallelism
  - (c) Per-declaration parallelism
- **Decision:** **(c) Per-declaration parallelism via the query framework**
- **Rationale:** Single-threaded (a) leaves enormous performance on the table — modern development machines have 8-32 cores. Per-module (b) is an improvement but coarse-grained (a 1000-function module serializes). Per-declaration (c) is natural once the query framework exists: independent queries evaluate in parallel; the framework handles dependency ordering. Avra eats its own dogfood here — the compiler uses Avra's green-thread scheduler (18.1) and per-declaration parallelism emerges from the query graph.
- **How it works:**
  - Query framework tracks dependencies between queries
  - Independent queries are submitted to the scheduler and execute concurrently
  - The scheduler (Avra's green-thread scheduler, 18.1) handles fiber assignment to OS threads
  - Dependent queries wait on their dependencies via the Task API (18.2)
- **Example:**
  ```
  // Compiling a file with 100 top-level declarations
  // Declarations A, B, C, D depend only on stdlib (no cross-dependencies)
  // Declaration E depends on A and B
  // Declaration F depends on C
  
  // Query framework schedules:
  //   Parallel: type_of(A), type_of(B), type_of(C), type_of(D)
  //   After A + B complete: type_of(E)
  //   After C completes: type_of(F)
  //   ... and so on
  ```
- **Linear scaling up to core count** for typical projects — most declarations are mutually independent at the query level.
- **LLVM codegen:** can run parallel to remaining type checking of unrelated declarations. Once a function's IR is emitted, it can be optimized and object-emitted on a separate thread while the type checker works on other functions.
- **Memory cost:** parallel query execution means multiple query states in flight simultaneously. Memory usage scales with parallelism; bounded by a configurable `max_parallel_queries` (default: num_cpus).
- **Versioning:** parallel query execution is a target capability for the current compiler. Current implementation may run single-threaded; parallelization is incremental work identified in the TRD, landed as the query framework matures.

**Sub-decision 26.4: Caching strategy**

- **Options:**
  - (a) None
  - (b) LLVM bitcode caching
  - (c) Full result caching (types, IR, metadata)
- **Decision:** **(c) Full result caching — content-addressed cache of types, IR, diagnostics, and metadata**
- **Rationale:** No caching (a) wastes work that's already been done, violating fast-iteration requirements for LSP and LLM workflows. LLVM bitcode only (b) skips the biggest wins — type checking and diagnostic computation are often more expensive than codegen, especially in projects with heavy generic instantiation and trait resolution. Full result caching (c) caches every query output at every level of the compiler, content-addressed for safety. This is how the query framework (26.1) naturally stores its results.
- **What's cached:**
  - Parsed ASTs (keyed by file content hash)
  - Resolved imports (keyed by module graph hash)
  - Type-checked declarations (keyed by declaration source + dependency hashes)
  - Trait selection results
  - Monomorphized instances (keyed by generic function + type arguments)
  - LLVM IR (keyed by typed AST + optimization config)
  - Object files and link inputs
  - Diagnostics (so error reports don't rerun analysis)
  - Autofix suggestions
- **Content-addressed storage:** every cache entry is keyed by the hash of its inputs. Changing a dependency invalidates the entry automatically. Stored in `.avra/cache/` per project and `.avra/global/` for shared immutable artifacts.
- **Registry integration:** the content-addressed registry (16.8) stores LLVM bitcode for published package versions. Downloading a dependency fetches pre-compiled bitcode for the current target — no compilation of dependencies needed on most builds. Dramatically shrinks initial build time.
- **CI sharing:** `.avra/cache/` can be exported and imported as a tarball, keyed by project hash + compiler version. Build systems that share cache across CI runs get near-instant rebuilds for unchanged code.
- **Cache invalidation:**
  - Input hash change → entry invalidates naturally (new key, new entry)
  - Compiler version change → entire cache invalidates
  - Feature flag change → affected subset invalidates
  - Manual: `avra cache clean` wipes everything; `avra cache clean --module=auth` scoped
- **Cache size management:** LRU eviction with a configurable max size (default: 5 GB per project cache, 20 GB global). Eviction is lazy; never blocks builds.
- **Versioning:** full result caching is the architectural target. The current compiler may implement a subset; refinement is ongoing work identified in the TRD.

---

### Axis 27: Tooling

**Sub-decision 27.1: Formatter**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not included
  - (b) `forge fmt` with canonical style
  - (c) Configurable formatter
- **Decision:** **(b) `avra fmt` with one canonical style, zero configuration**
- **Rationale:** Formatting wars are a pure waste of human attention. Every team that debates tabs vs spaces is one whose founders forgot the company's mission. Configurable formatters (c) bring these debates into every Avra project. No formatter (a) means formatting drift across every file and review. A canonical style (b) — the Go / Rust / Prettier approach — eliminates the debate entirely: the formatter is authoritative, there is no configuration, arguing is a waste of breath.
- **`avra fmt` behavior:**
  - Runs on source files, produces byte-identical canonical formatting
  - Idempotent: `avra fmt` twice is the same as once
  - Applied by default on `avra build` (warns if formatting differs from canonical)
  - Hook available for pre-commit / CI: `avra fmt --check` exits non-zero on diff
  - LSP integration: format-on-save applies `avra fmt` transparently
- **No configuration:** there is no `.avra-fmt.toml`. Line width, indentation, operator spacing — all fixed by the canonical style. The formatter changes only when the language changes (new syntax requires formatting rules).
- **Canonical style (summary):**
  - 2-space indentation
  - 100-character soft line limit; hard wraps only where the grammar requires
  - Trailing commas in multi-line collections
  - Alphabetized imports within groups (stdlib / external / local)
  - No semicolons (Avra is newline-sensitive)
  - `->` arrows with one space on each side
  - Braces on the same line as the declaration that opens them (K&R-style for fns, blocks)
- **Versioning:** ships with the compiler from the start. The canonical style is stable; changes are rare and intentional, versioned in release notes.
- **Why this is a feature, not a limitation:** teams spend zero time on formatting debates. PR diffs don't mix logic and formatting changes. New contributors get formatting right automatically. LLMs produce canonical output from the start because that's what training data (future Avra code) looks like. This is a social benefit, not a technical one.

**Sub-decision 27.2: Linter**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not included
  - (b) Built into compiler (warnings)
  - (c) Separate linter with extensible rules
- **Decision:** **(b + c) Core lints in the compiler as warnings; extensible rules via a lint-provider API**
- **Rationale:** No linter (a) misses a major quality multiplier. Compiler-only (b) limits expressiveness — some lints are project-specific (our team never uses feature X) or provider-specific (using `@std/http`, always set a timeout). External-only (c) duplicates the analyzer infrastructure and loses integration with the compiler's diagnostic surface. Both (b + c) is the right answer: language-level lints ship with the compiler; ecosystem and project-specific lints are opt-in via a provider API that uses the compiler's analysis machinery.
- **Compiler-built-in lints (warnings with F9xxx codes):**
  - `F9000` unused import
  - `F9001` unused variable
  - `F9002` unused function parameter
  - `F9003` shadow of outer variable
  - `F9004` redundant null check (after flow-typing)
  - `F9005` dead code after unconditional return
  - `F9006` match arm unreachable
  - `F9007` missing `@derive` for a model
  - `F9008` capability declared but not used
  - ... and the usual set, adopted progressively
- **Severity control (`forge.toml`):**
  ```toml
  [lints]
  unused_variable = "error"         # promote warning to error
  shadow = "allow"                  # suppress warning
  missing_derive = "warn"           # default
  ```
- **Extensible lint rules via providers:**
  ```
  // Lint provider: @myorg/avra-lints
  @lint_rule
  export fn no_http_without_timeout(ctx: LintContext) -> List<Finding> {
    ctx.find_calls_to("@std/http.get", "@std/http.post")
      .filter((call) -> !call.has_kwarg("timeout"))
      .map((call) -> Finding {
        code: "HTTP001",
        message: "HTTP call without timeout",
        span: call.span,
        suggestion: "add `timeout: 30.seconds`",
      })
  }
  ```
- **Lint providers registered in `forge.toml`:**
  ```toml
  [lint_providers]
  @std/lints = "*"                    # stdlib style and safety lints
  @myorg/avra-lints = "1.0.0"         # team-specific lints
  ```
- **Same diagnostic surface:** custom lints produce findings using the same diagnostic format (20.x) — F-code-like identifiers, suggestions with confidence levels, LSP integration, autofix where applicable. Written once, consumed by every tool.
- **Versioning:** core lints ship with the compiler; extension API ships in v1.x once the query framework is stable enough to expose lint hooks.

**Sub-decision 27.3: Documentation generation**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not included
  - (b) `forge docs` generates from doc comments
  - (c) Auto-generated from source with hyperlinked types
- **Decision:** **(c) Auto-generated from source with hyperlinked types, enriched by doc comments**
- **Rationale:** No docs (a) is unacceptable for a language aimed at ecosystem growth. Doc-comments only (b) miss the compiler's type information — much of what's valuable is already known by the type checker. Auto-generated with hyperlinks (c) extracts every public signature, every referenced type, every trait impl; doc comments enrich but aren't required. Critically, every mentioned type is hyperlinked to its definition, enabling exploration.
- **`avra docs` output:**
  - Per-package documentation with public surface fully documented
  - Every type reference is a hyperlink to that type's docs
  - Every function signature is extractable and shareable
  - Traits show all implementing types; types show all implemented traits
  - Code examples from tests are automatically cross-referenced
  - Stability markers from version metadata (stable / experimental / deprecated)
- **Doc comments:** prefix with `///` for item documentation, `//!` for module-level
  ```
  /// Sends an HTTP GET request to the given URL.
  ///
  /// # Examples
  /// 
  /// ```
  /// let response = http.get("https://api.example.com")?
  /// ```
  ///
  /// # Errors
  /// 
  /// Returns `HttpError.Timeout` if the request exceeds the timeout.
  /// Returns `HttpError.NetworkError` on connection failures.
  export fn get(url: string, timeout: Duration = 30.seconds) -> Result<Response, HttpError> {
    ...
  }
  ```
- **Auto-enriched content (without any doc comment):**
  - Function signature with all parameter types
  - Return type including all possible error variants
  - Capabilities required (from provider manifest)
  - Inferred complexity hints where derivable
  - "Used by" reverse index showing callers in the ecosystem (from registry crawl)
- **Rendering targets:**
  - HTML (default, for `docs.avra.dev`)
  - Markdown (for README-style docs in repos)
  - JSON (for tooling — IDEs, AI agents, search indexing)
  - Terminal (`avra docs @std/http` in the CLI)
- **Published to registry:** when a package is published (16.8), its docs are automatically rendered and hosted at `docs.avra.dev/@namespace/package@version`. No separate doc-hosting setup.
- **LLM-friendly:** the JSON format mirrors the MCP docs schema — agents can query documentation via MCP exactly as they do for user projects. Documentation IS introspection, and introspection IS documentation.
- **Versioning:** ships with the compiler from v1.0.

**Sub-decision 27.4: REPL**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not included
  - (b) Interpreted REPL
  - (c) JIT-compiled REPL (LLVM ORC)
- **Decision:** **(c) JIT-compiled REPL via LLVM ORC**
- **Rationale:** No REPL (a) hurts exploratory programming and interactive data work. An interpreted REPL (b) is a trap — it has different semantics than compiled code (different performance, different memory model, different type inference in corner cases), becomes a bug farm where "works in REPL, broken in compile" appears, and diverges from the main compiler as language features land. JIT-compiled REPL (c) uses LLVM's ORC JIT — the REPL executes the SAME compiled code the full compiler produces, just compiled on-demand and linked incrementally. Same semantics, same performance, same types.
- **How it works:**
  - `avra repl` launches an interactive prompt
  - Each input is parsed as a top-level form, type-checked against the accumulated REPL environment, codegen'd to LLVM IR, JIT-compiled by LLVM ORC, and executed
  - Local bindings persist across inputs (the REPL has a growing environment)
  - Top-level declarations (fns, types) are added to the environment and callable from subsequent inputs
  - Incremental compilation (26.2) makes the per-input compilation fast
- **Example:**
  ```
  > let users = [User{name: "Alice"}, User{name: "Bob"}]
  users: List<User> = [...]
  
  > users.map((u) -> u.name)
  : List<string> = ["Alice", "Bob"]
  
  > fn greet(u: User) -> string { "Hello, " + u.name }
  defined greet
  
  > users.map(greet)
  : List<string> = ["Hello, Alice", "Hello, Bob"]
  ```
- **Imports in the REPL:** `use @std/http.{Http}` works as in any file. Packages are resolved against the current project's `forge.toml` if invoked inside a project, or against a temporary REPL sandbox if invoked standalone.
- **Spawning in the REPL:** `spawn { ... }` works — the REPL's main thread is a fiber in the runtime. You can hold `Task<T>` handles and await them in later inputs.
- **Inspection:** `:type <expr>` shows the type, `:impl <trait>` lists impls, `:help` lists commands. Built-in introspection matching the MCP server's capabilities.
- **State reset:** `:reset` clears the REPL environment.
- **Integration with notebooks:** the REPL is embeddable — a notebook frontend (Jupyter kernel or similar) can drive the REPL backend for block-based interactive computing. Covers the "notebooks fail at scale" concern from user memories by ensuring the REPL IS the same compiler, not a parallel implementation.
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.x once LLVM ORC integration is stable. v1.0 may omit the REPL until ORC is wired through.

**Sub-decision 27.5: Hot reload**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not included
  - (b) Module-level reload in dev mode
  - (c) Function-level replacement
- **Decision:** **(c) Function-level replacement — Erlang/Elixir-style live code update**
- **Rationale:** No hot reload (a) breaks the LLM-driven iteration loop (edit → wait for restart → test). Module-level reload (b) is coarse — editing one function invalidates the entire module's state, including long-running fibers. Function-level replacement (c) is the Erlang/Elixir model: edit a function, new calls use the new version, in-flight calls finish on the old version. This is the granularity developers actually want.
- **How it works:**
  - `avra dev` runs the program with hot-reload enabled
  - File save triggers incremental recompilation (26.2) — only changed declarations reprocess
  - New versions of changed functions are compiled as new code objects via ORC JIT
  - The runtime's function dispatch table is updated atomically — subsequent calls use the new version
  - In-flight calls on the old function finish normally; the old version is eventually GC'd once no stack refers to it
- **Type-compatible changes (hot-reloadable):**
  - Function body changes preserving signature
  - New functions added
  - Private helper function changes
  - Most declaration changes where the public interface is unchanged
- **Type-incompatible changes (require restart):**
  - Public function signature changes (parameter types, return type)
  - Type declaration changes (fields added/removed/retyped)
  - Changes to trait definitions
  - Deploy topology changes (services added/removed)
  The runtime detects incompatible changes and prompts: "Incompatible change to `User.email` (string → int). Restart required. Continue? [y/N]"
- **State preservation:** hot-reload preserves all runtime state — spawned fibers keep running, channels retain buffered values, open connections stay open, in-memory state persists. The only thing that changes is the code each function executes.
- **Limits:**
  - Changes to inline-able functions may not propagate to already-inlined call sites until those callers are also reloaded
  - Tight CPU loops holding a pointer to the old code may continue to use the old version until the loop exits
  - Neither is a correctness issue, but may confuse debugging
- **Connection to observability (19.5):** hot-reload events are automatically traced so timeline views show "at time T, function X was updated to version N." Useful when investigating "did the bug just appear?"
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.x. v1.0 may support module-level reload as a simpler starting point; function-level is the target once JIT ORC integration is mature.

**Sub-decision 27.6: Debugger**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Standard LLDB / GDB via DWARF
  - (b) Custom debugger with Avra-aware introspection
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — DWARF emits for standard debuggers, `avra debug` wraps them with Avra-aware display**
- **Rationale:** Standard debugger only (a) gives raw, LLVM-level views — enum discriminants as integers, refcount headers exposed, closures as structs-with-mystery-fields. Users see Avra code but debug assembly-level representation. Custom debugger only (b) reinvents thirty years of debugger infrastructure (stepping, breakpoints, expression evaluation, remote debugging, platform integration). Both (c) is the pragmatic answer: LLVM emits DWARF so `lldb`/`gdb`/IDE debuggers just work, AND `avra debug` wraps them with Avra-aware pretty-printing and introspection.
- **Standard path (LLDB/GDB):**
  - Compiler emits DWARF debug info alongside object files (via LLVM)
  - `lldb target/debug/myapp` or `gdb --args ...` works out of the box
  - Source-level stepping, breakpoints, variable inspection
  - IDE integrations (VS Code, CLion) work via their standard debug adapters
  - Remote debugging for deployed services via `lldb-server` / `gdbserver`
- **`avra debug` wrapper:**
  - Launches the program under a standard debugger with Avra-specific summary formatters installed
  - Enums display by variant name, not discriminant integer: `User.LoggedIn { ... }` not `tag: 2, data: 0x...`
  - Refcount headers are hidden; values display as their logical content
  - Closures show captured variables by name: `{captures: {threshold: 10, base: "..."}, body: <auth.av:42>}`
  - Structural-sharing collections display deduped references
  - `T?` displays as the value or `null`, not as enum discriminant
  - `Result<T, E>` displays as `Ok(...)` or `Err(...)`
- **Avra-aware introspection commands (in the debugger):**
  - `av fibers` — list all green threads with their state, stack, and spawn location
  - `av channels` — list all channels with buffer contents and waiters
  - `av types <expr>` — show static type of an expression at this point
  - `av trace <fn>` — show the distributed trace context at this function call
  - `av refcount <value>` — show refcount and owners of a value
- **LSP debug integration:** the LSP server exposes debug protocol (DAP) endpoints so IDEs can drive the debugger through a unified interface. Breakpoints set in the editor land in the running program.
- **Versioning:** DWARF emission ships with the compiler from v1.0 (it's free from LLVM). `avra debug` wrapper with pretty-printers ships in v1.x as the Avra-aware introspection surface matures.

**Sub-decision 27.7: Profiler**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Standard tools (perf, Instruments)
  - (b) Built-in profiler with compiler-aware output
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — standard tools work, `avra profile` adds Avra-aware views**
- **Rationale:** Same pattern as the debugger (27.6). Standard tools (`perf` on Linux, Instruments on macOS) work on any LLVM-compiled binary — zero additional effort. But they show raw function names with Avra mangling, miss fiber-specific context, don't attribute refcount operations to source locations, and don't understand channel/spawn topology. `avra profile` adds that Avra-aware layer. Both together cover the full spectrum from "drop into perf like any binary" to "Avra-specific fiber flame graphs."
- **Standard path:**
  - Compiler emits symbol tables usable by `perf` / DTrace / Instruments
  - `perf record ./myapp && perf report` works out of the box
  - AddressSanitizer, ThreadSanitizer, MemorySanitizer available via LLVM sanitizer runtimes (`avra build --sanitize=address`)
  - `valgrind` works for memory analysis
- **`avra profile` additional views:**
  - **Fiber flame graphs** — which fibers spent time in which functions, grouped by spawn ancestry
  - **Channel analysis** — which channels are full/empty most often, which receivers are the bottleneck
  - **Refcount operation attribution** — where the program spends time on refcount inc/dec, tied to source locations; surfaces missed elision opportunities
  - **Arena attribution** — which arenas hold which allocations, drop patterns, lifetime distributions
  - **Trait dispatch hit rates** — static vs dynamic dispatch counts, useful for performance tuning
  - **Allocation attribution** — allocations by source location, aggregated over time
- **Output formats:**
  - Interactive HTML flamegraph
  - `pprof` format for integration with existing tooling (Google, Uber have infra)
  - JSON for programmatic analysis
  - Terminal summary for CI runs
- **Integration with observability (19.5):** profile data can flow through the same OpenTelemetry-compatible pipeline as traces. Long-term performance trends become queryable in the observability backend, not just per-run snapshots.
- **Production profiling:** `avra profile --sampling` enables low-overhead sampling profiling suitable for production deployment. Capture only the hot paths, minimal perturbation to behavior.
- **Versioning:** standard tool compatibility ships with the compiler from v1.0. `avra profile` with Avra-aware views ships progressively through v1.x as the instrumentation is built out.

---

## Part 7: Cross-cutting Concerns

Decisions that don't fit cleanly into one axis but affect many.

---

### Axis 28: Syntax and Readability

**Sub-decision 28.1: Statement terminators**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Semicolons required
  - (b) Newline-sensitive (no semicolons)
  - (c) Optional semicolons
- **Decision:** **(b) Newline-sensitive — no semicolons in the language**
- **Rationale:** Required semicolons (a) are pure ceremony — every line ends with a semicolon except when it doesn't, and the exceptions are arbitrary. Optional semicolons (c) are the JavaScript/TypeScript footgun — ASI (automatic semicolon insertion) has multiple known corner cases where inserting or omitting changes meaning. Newline-sensitive (b) is the cleanest answer: the grammar knows when statements end. Modern languages (Python, Go, Kotlin, Swift) all adopt this model.
- **How newlines terminate statements:**
  - A newline ends a statement unless the grammar demands continuation
  - Continuation is explicit: trailing operator, open parenthesis/bracket/brace, explicit `\` line continuation
  - Inside balanced brackets (`(`, `[`, `{`), newlines are ignored — no surprise statement splits mid-expression
- **Examples:**
  ```
  let x = 10             // statement terminated by newline
  
  let y = some_function(
    a,                    // newlines inside () are fine — arg list continues
    b,
    c,
  )
  
  let z = a +             // trailing `+` — expression continues
          b +
          c
  
  let big = {
    let tmp = compute()   // newline terminates
    tmp * 2               // final expression is the block's value
  }
  ```
- **No ASI ambiguity:** because Avra is newline-sensitive by design (not by insertion), there's no "should I insert a semicolon here?" heuristic. The grammar is deterministic from newlines and brackets alone.
- **Versioning:** locked from v1.0.

**Sub-decision 28.2: Braces vs indentation**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Braces (`{ }`)
  - (b) Indentation (Python)
  - (c) Optional — either works
- **Decision:** **(a) Braces — `{ }` delimit blocks; indentation is for humans only**
- **Rationale:** Indentation-based blocks (b) look cleaner but are more error-prone, especially with LLMs generating code: an off-by-one indent silently changes meaning. Mixed tabs and spaces are a perennial headache. Paste operations across editors lose indentation. Optional (c) is the worst of both worlds — code uses whichever, readers must track. Braces (a) are unambiguous, copy-paste safe, grep-friendly, parser-friendly. The formatter (27.1) enforces canonical indentation so code still LOOKS clean, without the semantic fragility.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  fn greet(user: User) -> string {
    let prefix = if user.is_admin { "Admin" } else { "User" }
    "Hello, " + prefix + " " + user.name
  }
  
  match response {
    Ok(body) -> {
      process(body)
      log("success")
    }
    Err(e) -> {
      log_error(e)
      retry()
    }
  }
  ```
- **Rules:**
  - `{ }` delimits blocks — function bodies, control flow bodies, match arms (when multi-statement), struct/map literals
  - Indentation is enforced by the formatter (27.1) but has no syntactic meaning
  - Single-statement blocks can omit braces: `if x { do_thing() }` — but the formatter always adds them for consistency
- **Why not rely on formatter-enforced indentation:** indentation-sensitive languages conflate formatting with semantics. In Avra, formatting is cosmetic; semantics come from the grammar. This means you can paste code from anywhere and it parses the same, you can use any text editor without configuration, and LLMs can't accidentally change behavior via whitespace.
- **Versioning:** locked from v1.0.

**Sub-decision 28.3: String literals**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Double quotes + backticks (templates)
  - (b) Single + double quotes
  - (c) Triple-quote for multiline
- **Decision:** **Comprehensive — `"..."` with interpolation, `"""..."""` for multiline, `r"..."` for raw, backticks reserved**
- **Rationale:** Single vs double (b) is arbitrary and invites bikeshedding. Most real code needs interpolation, multiline, and raw literals — all three should be first-class. The chosen surface covers all common cases with minimal conceptual overhead.
- **Forms:**
  ```
  let name = "Alice"
  let greeting = "Hello, {name}!"                 // interpolation with {}
  let escaped = "Hello, \{literal braces\}"       // escape braces with backslash
  
  let multiline = """
    This is a
    multiline string.
    Leading whitespace of the closing """ is stripped uniformly from all lines.
  """
  
  let regex = r"\d{3}-\d{4}"                      // raw string, no escapes
  let path = r"C:\Users\alice\file.txt"
  
  let raw_multi = r"""
    Nothing in here is an escape sequence.
    \n stays as \n.
  """
  ```
- **Interpolation details:**
  - `{expr}` embeds any expression; compiler type-checks and calls `.to_string()` or `.format()`
  - `{expr:format_spec}` uses a format specification: `{price:.2}` for 2 decimal places, `{name:>10}` right-aligned to 10 chars
  - Escaping: `\{` for literal `{`, `\}` for literal `}`
  - Compile-time checked: referenced variables must exist and be in scope
- **Multiline `"""..."""`:**
  - Triple-quoted, leading indentation of the closing `"""` is stripped from every line uniformly (like Swift/Scala)
  - Supports interpolation same as single-line `"..."`
  - Newlines literal, no continuation syntax needed
- **Raw strings `r"..."` and `r"""..."""`:**
  - No escape sequences processed
  - Useful for regex, paths, JSON, embedded DSLs
  - Raw strings do NOT support interpolation (they're raw — `{name}` is literal `{name}`)
- **Backticks:** reserved for future use (potentially for tagged template literals like `sql`\`SELECT * FROM users WHERE id = {id}\``, but not committed to v1.0).
- **Character literals:** `'a'` for a single Unicode character (type `char`, 4-byte). Distinct from single-element strings.
- **Byte strings:** `b"..."` for byte literals (type `List<u8>`), no escape processing beyond the usual.
- **Versioning:** `"..."`, `"""..."""`, `r"..."`, and character literals ship in v1.0. Backtick templates reserved.

**Sub-decision 28.4: Comments**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `//` line, `/* */` block, `///` doc
  - (b) `#` line (Python-style)
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(a) `//` line, `/* */` block, `///` item doc, `//!` module doc**
- **Rationale:** Python-style `#` (b) breaks with shell-script expectations and conflicts with Avra's annotation-like use of `#` in some patterns (none currently but preserve the option). Both (c) creates inconsistency. Rust/C-style comments (a) are the industry consensus and widely familiar. Avra adds `//!` for module-level docs to match established doc conventions.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  // Line comment
  
  /*
    Block comment
    can span multiple lines
  */
  
  /*
    /* Block comments nest */
  */
  
  /// Documents the next item (function, type, trait, etc.)
  /// Multiple consecutive `///` lines compose into one doc block.
  /// Supports markdown formatting; code blocks are type-checked where possible.
  export fn add(a: int, b: int) -> int { a + b }
  
  //! Documents the enclosing module.
  //! Placed at the top of a file to describe the module's purpose.
  ```
- **Doc comment processing:**
  - `///` attaches to the next declaration; rendered in generated docs (27.3)
  - `//!` attaches to the enclosing module; rendered at the module's docs page
  - Markdown is supported for formatting
  - Code blocks in doc comments are type-checked by the compiler as examples; broken examples are compile errors
- **Block comment nesting:** `/* outer /* inner */ outer */` — nesting is supported, unlike C. Matters for commenting out code that already contains block comments.
- **No shebang special-case:** the first line of a file being `#!/usr/bin/env avra` is currently not recognized as a comment. If the use case becomes common (executable scripts), Avra will special-case shebang on line 1 in a point release.
- **Versioning:** locked from v1.0.

**Sub-decision 28.5: Naming conventions**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Enforced by formatter (PascalCase types, snake_case fns)
  - (b) Advisory only
  - (c) Not enforced
- **Decision:** **(a) Enforced by formatter + compiler warnings, canonical style is part of the language**
- **Rationale:** Advisory-only conventions (b) get ignored in practice. Not enforced (c) leads to inconsistent codebases. Enforced (a) is another manifestation of "no bikeshedding" — teams use canonical naming because the tools require it. This also makes LLM-authored code consistent and easy to read.
- **Canonical naming:**
  - **Types, traits, enums, shapes** — `PascalCase`: `User`, `HttpResponse`, `Iterator<T>`, `Eq`
  - **Functions, methods, variables, fields, parameters** — `snake_case`: `get_user`, `email`, `is_valid`, `user_id`
  - **Constants** — `SCREAMING_SNAKE_CASE`: `MAX_RETRIES`, `DEFAULT_TIMEOUT`, `PI`
  - **Enum variants** — `PascalCase`: `LoggedIn`, `Pending`, `NotFound`
  - **Modules, packages** — `snake_case`: `auth.login`, `@myorg/user_service`
  - **Lifetimes (if surfaced)** — lowercase single letters: `'a`, `'static` (deferred to systems-level)
  - **Type parameters** — single PascalCase letters or descriptive: `T`, `K`, `V`, `Item`
- **Enforcement:**
  - Compiler emits warnings (`F9010 non-canonical name`) on declarations violating convention
  - Formatter (27.1) auto-fixes simple cases (common transliterations between cases)
  - Cannot be disabled project-wide; individual exceptions via `@allow(non_canonical_name)` on specific declarations for FFI boundaries and other pragmatic cases
- **FFI exception:** bindings to C libraries retain C naming: `c_printf`, `c_strlen`. Marked with `@ffi_binding` — exempt from warnings.
- **Rationale for strict enforcement:**
  - LLMs produce canonical code without training-dependent drift
  - Ecosystem is visually consistent — anyone reading any library looks the same
  - Zero team debate
  - Search/grep is predictable (can search for `user_id` knowing it's the canonical form)
- **Versioning:** ships from v1.0. The convention set is stable; additions for new syntactic categories happen as the language grows.

**Sub-decision 28.6: File extension**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `.av`
  - (b) `.avra`
  - (c) `.fg` (legacy from Forge)
- **Decision:** **(a) `.av` — short, distinctive, CLI is `avra`**
- **Rationale:** `.avra` (b) is three more characters on every file reference. `.fg` (c) is Forge legacy, not Avra. `.av` (a) is short and distinctive. Confirmed from user memories: "File extension / CLI: `.av` / `av` (avoids conflict with niche AVR assembler tool)."
- **Ecosystem collisions:**
  - `.av` is used by a niche AVR assembler tool — minimal overlap with Avra's target users
  - No major conflict with common file-type registrations
  - MIME type: `text/x-avra`
- **CLI command:** `avra` (the tool), not `av`. The extension and command are distinct. `avra build src/main.av`.
- **Test files:** `_test.av` suffix or placed under `tests/` directory (see 24.1).
- **Migration note:** during the transition from "Forge" to "Avra," files may use `.fg` in existing codebases. The compiler accepts both `.av` and `.fg` during a deprecation window; `.fg` emits a warning prompting migration. Eventually `.fg` support is removed.
- **Associated extensions:**
  - `.av` — source file (primary)
  - `.av.lock` — lockfile for dependency resolution
  - `.avc` — compiled artifact (internal)
  - `forge.toml` — manifest (name retained despite Avra rename, since `forge` is the build-configuration verb/file name, not the language name)
- **`forge.toml` name:** kept as `forge.toml` for historical continuity and to distinguish "build configuration" from "language source." Users already know `forge.toml`; renaming to `avra.toml` or similar creates migration churn for no benefit.
- **Versioning:** `.av` extension from v1.0. `.fg` deprecation warning for a few versions, then removed.

**Sub-decision 28.7: Pipe operator**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `|>` (F#, Elixir)
  - (b) Method chaining only
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(c) Both `|>` and method chaining — they compose different things**
- **Rationale:** Pipe-only (a) doesn't work for the many cases where the next operation is a method on the value, not a free function. Method-chaining-only (b) doesn't work for composing free functions or for task-backed pipelines (18.2). Both (c) lets each shine: method chaining for object-like operations, `|>` for function pipelines and task stages. Already referenced extensively in 18.2 and 28.10 — this sub-decision formally ratifies the operator.
- **Method chaining:**
  ```
  let result = "  hello world  "
    .trim()
    .to_upper()
    .split(" ")
    .first()?
  ```
  Method calls flow left-to-right, each call on the result of the previous. The normal `.method()` syntax.
- **Pipe operator `|>`:**
  ```
  let result = data
    |> parse
    |> validate
    |> normalize
    |> save
  ```
  - `x |> f` desugars to `f(x)`
  - `x |> f(y, z)` desugars to `f(x, y, z)` — the piped value becomes the first argument
  - Explicit placement with `_` for non-first-argument piping: `x |> format("value: {}", _)` desugars to `format("value: {}", x)`
- **Piping into lambdas:** `x |> (v) -> v * 2` works — pipes into an inline closure.
- **Piping with spawn (from 18.2):** `source |> spawn parse |> spawn validate |> collect` — each `spawn` stage creates a task connected by a channel; `collect` is a terminal stage that aggregates results. Covered in detail in 18.2.
- **Composition with `it` (28.8):**
  ```
  users
    |> filter(it.age >= 18)
    |> map(it.name)
    |> join(", ")
  ```
  Compact, readable pipelines combining `|>`, `it`, and higher-order functions.
- **Precedence:** `|>` has lower precedence than most operators — the entire expression on the left is piped into the function on the right. Binds tighter than assignment, looser than arithmetic and comparison.
- **Rationale for both patterns:** some operations are naturally methods (`string.trim()`, `list.first()`) because they're conceptually attached to the value. Others are naturally free functions (`parse`, `validate`, `normalize`) because they're generic operations. Forcing everything into one pattern creates awkward code either way. Languages that have both (Elixir, F# with method-like syntax) end up using `|>` for pipelines and dot-method for "on this value" — natural split.
- **Versioning:** both ship in v1.0.

**Sub-decision 28.8: `it` pronoun**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not supported
  - (b) Implicit single-parameter closures (`list.filter(it > 5)`)
  - (c) Kotlin-style with block scope
- **Decision:** **(b) Implicit single-parameter closures via `it`, scoped to the nearest enclosing single-parameter closure**
- **Rationale:** Not supported (a) forces every one-parameter lambda to be `(x) -> x > 5` style — not terrible but accumulates noise in data pipelines. Kotlin's block-scope `it` (c) has the same behavior we're proposing; "Kotlin-style" is basically what (b) means in practice. The `it` pronoun is a concise way to write single-parameter closures for common cases. Appears widely in 14.x refinement types (`where it > 0`), filtering, mapping, and pipe operations — reads naturally.
- **Semantics:**
  - In any single-parameter closure or lambda context, `it` refers to that parameter without naming
  - Only usable where there is exactly one unnamed parameter — not for 0-parameter or multi-parameter closures
  - Multi-parameter contexts require explicit parameter names: `(x, y) -> x + y`
- **Examples:**
  ```
  // Simple filter
  users.filter(it.age >= 18)
  
  // Map
  users.map(it.name)
  
  // With pipe operator
  users
    |> filter(it.age >= 18)
    |> map(it.name)
    |> sort()
  
  // Refinement type predicate
  type Percentage = float where 0.0 <= it && it <= 100.0
  
  // Multi-line method call — still single-parameter
  users.find(u -> {
    u.age >= 18 && u.is_admin       // here, explicit `u` is better
  })
  
  // Short form with `it`
  users.find(it.age >= 18 && it.is_admin)    // fine for short predicates
  ```
- **Scope rules:**
  - `it` binds to the innermost enclosing single-parameter closure
  - Nested `it` shadows outer `it`:
    ```
    lists.map(
      it.map(it * 2)     // inner `it` is list element; outer `it` is the list — but we don't need it
    )
    ```
    However, for readability, nesting `it` inside `it` is discouraged; use explicit names when two levels deep. The formatter emits a style warning.
  - `it` is not available outside a closure context — top-level code using `it` is an error
- **When explicit names are better style:**
  - Multi-line closure bodies — explicit name makes the body clear
  - Nested closures — explicit names avoid `it` shadowing confusion
  - Complex logic where the value represents something specific domain-wise (e.g., "invoice" vs "it")
  The formatter and lint rules nudge toward explicit names when closures exceed ~2 lines.
- **Why this pays off:** data-heavy code in Avra (model pipelines, test fixtures, filter-map-reduce chains) is far more readable with `it`. Compare:
  ```
  // Without it
  users.filter((u) -> u.age >= 18).map((u) -> u.name).sort()
  
  // With it
  users.filter(it.age >= 18).map(it.name).sort()
  ```
  The second reads "users where age >= 18, their names, sorted." That's what the programmer meant. `it` lets the code match the intent.
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0.

**Sub-decision 28.9: `with` expression**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not supported
  - (b) Immutable copy-and-modify: `point with { x: 10 }`
  - (c) Only on structs, not enums
- **Decision:** **(b) `with` expression on any record/shape — immutable copy with updates; already ratified in 9.23**
- **Rationale:** Not supported (a) forces verbose record updates: `{...point, x: 10}` spread-style or field-by-field reassignment — toil. Only on structs not enums (c) arbitrarily restricts a genuinely useful operation. `with` on any record-like value (b) is cleanest and matches what 9.23 already established for structural sharing.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  let point = Point { x: 1, y: 2, z: 3 }
  let moved = point with { x: 10 }
  // moved is Point { x: 10, y: 2, z: 3 }
  // `point` is unchanged (immutability from 11.x)
  
  // Multiple updates
  let transformed = point with { x: 10, y: 20 }
  
  // Works with shape types (not just nominal)
  let user = { name: "Alice", age: 30 }
  let older = user with { age: 31 }
  
  // Expression updates — the right side is any expression
  let incremented = counter with { value: counter.value + 1 }
  
  // Chained with other operations
  let updated = users.map(it with { last_seen: now() })
  ```
- **Semantics:**
  - `expr with { field: value, ... }` produces a new value with the given fields updated
  - All other fields are structurally shared with the original (from 9.23 — no deep copy)
  - Type of the result is the same as the type of `expr`
  - Fields listed in `with` must exist on the source type; extra fields are a compile error
  - Values must have types compatible with the field's declared type
- **Persistent collections (future, v1.x+):** `with` extends to persistent collection operations:
  ```
  let list2 = list with { 3: "updated" }        // list[3] = "updated" (new list, structural share)
  let map2 = map with { "key": new_value }     // map.insert (new map, structural share)
  ```
  Ships alongside persistent collections from 9.23.
- **Enum interaction:** `with` on an enum variant updates fields of that variant:
  ```
  let msg = Message.UserRegistered { user_id: 42, timestamp: t1 }
  let updated = msg with { timestamp: t2 }
  // updated is Message.UserRegistered { user_id: 42, timestamp: t2 }
  
  // with on the wrong variant is a runtime error; the compiler can often
  // prove the variant statically (flow typing) and then it's a compile error
  ```
- **Nested updates:** deep updates use chained `with`:
  ```
  let user = User {
    name: "Alice",
    address: Address { street: "...", city: "SF" }
  }
  let moved = user with {
    address: user.address with { city: "NYC" }
  }
  ```
  A dedicated lens/path syntax could shorten this in v1.x+, but v1.0 uses nested `with`.
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0 for basic records. Persistent collection support and nested-path sugar in v1.x+.

**Sub-decision 28.10: Table literals**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not supported
  - (b) `table { col1 | col2 \n val1 | val2 }` syntax
  - (c) Just lists of structs
- **Decision:** **(b) First-class `table { ... }` literal — pipe-separated columnar syntax that desugars to `List<shape>`**
- **Rationale:** Just lists of structs (c) works but has a readability problem for the specific case of "multiple similar records" — every field name repeats on every row, the shape of the data gets lost in the punctuation. Table literals are one of those rare cases where a syntactic special case pays off: the data is genuinely tabular, reading it tabularly matches how humans think about it, and the syntax is visually unmistakable. Not supported (a) leaves this rich ergonomic win on the table. Data-heavy code (test fixtures, config, seed data, small embedded databases, pricing tables, lookup tables) becomes dramatically more readable with `table { }`.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  let users = table {
    name    | age | active
    "alice" | 30  | true
    "bob"   | 25  | false
    "carol" | 42  | true
  }
  
  // `users` has type List<{name: string, age: int, active: bool}>
  // That's a List of anonymous shape — fields inferred from header row, types inferred from values
  ```
- **Semantics:**
  - First row is the header — each cell is a field name (identifier)
  - Subsequent rows are data — each cell is an expression
  - Column types are inferred from the values in that column; all values in a column must unify to a single type (with the usual numeric widening and optional widening rules)
  - The result type is `List<shape>` where `shape` is the anonymous structural type defined by the header row
  - Desugars to a list-of-structs literal — nothing in the runtime is special about `table`, it's purely a parser-level convenience
- **Expressions in cells:**
  ```
  fn discount(price: float) -> float { price * 0.8 }
  
  let base = 100.0
  let products = table {
    name     | price       | discounted
    "Widget" | base        | discount(base)
    "Gadget" | base * 2.0  | discount(base * 2.0)
    "Gizmo"  | base * 0.5  | discount(base * 0.5)
  }
  ```
  Any expression is valid in a cell, not just literals. The `|` column separator is the only special character within a row.
- **Composition with pipe operator and `it`:**
  ```
  let passing = table {
    name    | score
    "alice" | 92
    "bob"   | 67
    "carol" | 85
    "dave"  | 43
  }
    |> filter(it.score >= 70)
    |> map(it.name)
    |> join(", ")
  // "alice, carol"
  ```
  Composes naturally with `|>` (28.7) and `it` (28.8) — tables are just lists, all list operations apply.
- **Nominal shape assignment:**
  ```
  type User = { name: string, age: int, active: bool }
  
  let users: List<User> = table {
    name    | age | active
    "alice" | 30  | true
    "bob"   | 25  | false
  }
  // Anonymous shape structurally matches `User`, so this assignment works.
  // See 3.x for nominal/structural rules.
  ```
- **Empty tables:**
  ```
  let empty: List<User> = table { name | age | active }
  // Header-only is legal; zero rows. Type annotation required since values don't provide types.
  ```
- **Parser considerations:**
  - `|` is the column separator within table rows, distinct from the pipe operator `|>` used in expression context
  - Whitespace around `|` in table context is required (at least one space) to distinguish from other `|` uses; the formatter aligns columns for readability
  - Trailing `|` at end of rows is allowed and normalized by the formatter
  - Row separators are newlines — a row must fit on one logical line (continuations via `\` or bracket-balanced continuation apply as usual)
- **Interaction with the formatter (27.1):** `avra fmt` aligns columns by padding the widest cell in each column, producing visually clean tables. This is one of the few places the formatter does more than whitespace normalization.
- **LLM value:** LLMs producing test fixtures, seed data, config tables, and example datasets produce much more readable output with `table { }`. The visual structure makes errors obvious at a glance (missing a column in one row is immediately visible).
- **Inspirations:** literate programming traditions, Lua's slight table bias, Elixir's doctest tables, SQL's row constructor — Avra's `table` is the cleanest version I've seen.
- **Versioning:** locked in v1.0. One of the concrete ergonomic wins that sets Avra apart from Rust/Go/TypeScript for data-heavy code.

**Sub-decision 28.11: Closure and lambda syntax**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Rust-style `|params| body`
  - (b) JavaScript-style `(params) => body`
  - (c) Arrow syntax `(params) -> body`
- **Decision:** **(c) `(params) -> body` — single arrow `->`, matching function signatures**
- **Rationale:** Formally ratifying what's been used throughout the questionnaire. Rust's `||` / `|x|` (a) overloads the `|` character which is already used for bitwise OR, boolean OR, pipe operator (28.7), and table column separator (28.10). Keeping `|` out of lambda syntax prevents ambiguity and parse complexity. JavaScript's `=>` (b) is widely familiar from TS/JS training data but uses a distinct glyph from the function return type arrow. Single arrow `->` (c) is the cleanest choice: one arrow glyph used consistently for "produces" everywhere in the language — function return types AND closure bodies AND match arms all use `->`.
- **The unified `->` story:**
  - **Function return type:** `fn add(a: int, b: int) -> int { a + b }`
  - **Closure:** `(a, b) -> a + b`
  - **Match arm:** `match x { 0 -> "zero", _ -> "other" }`
  - **Provider fn registrations and similar:** same arrow
- **Syntax forms:**
  ```
  // Zero-parameter
  let now = () -> Time.now()
  
  // One parameter (explicit)
  let double = (x) -> x * 2
  
  // One parameter with `it` (28.8)
  numbers.map(it * 2)
  
  // Multiple parameters
  let add = (a, b) -> a + b
  
  // With type annotations
  let typed = (x: int, y: int) -> int { x + y }
  
  // Multi-line body
  let complex = (user) -> {
    let prefix = if user.is_admin { "Admin" } else { "User" }
    prefix + ": " + user.name
  }
  
  // Move closure (from 9.22)
  let owned_closure = move (x) -> x * factor   // takes ownership of factor
  ```
- **Parentheses rules:**
  - Parentheses required around the parameter list — even for zero or one parameter
  - Exception: using `it` (28.8) requires no parameter list at all
  - Empty parens `()` for zero parameters
- **Return type inference:** closure return types are always inferred from the body. Explicit return types in closures are supported for documentation but rarely needed:
  ```
  let f: (int) -> int = (x) -> int { x * 2 }      // legal but verbose
  let f = (x: int) -> x * 2                        // idiomatic
  ```
- **Body forms:**
  - Expression body: `(x) -> x + 1`
  - Block body: `(x) -> { let y = f(x); y * 2 }`
  - Block expressions evaluate to their last expression (no explicit `return`)
- **Previously rejected alternatives (for documentation):**
  - `|x| ...` (Rust) — rejected due to `|` overload with pipe, table, OR operators
  - `=>` (JS/TS) — rejected because it visually distinguishes from `->` used elsewhere without adding semantic value
  - `\x -> ...` (Haskell) — rejected as unfamiliar to mainstream programmers
  - `fn(x) { ... }` (Swift closures) — rejected as too verbose for common cases
- **Historical note:** earlier drafts of the Avra questionnaire briefly considered `=>` during discussion of JavaScript-familiar syntax; the user explicitly corrected this back to `->` for consistency with function signatures and match arms. All example code in the questionnaire uses `->`.
- **Versioning:** locked in v1.0. The arrow choice is grammatically load-bearing; changing it post-v1.0 would break every file.

---

### Axis 29: Events and Reactivity

**Sub-decision 29.1: Event system**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not in language
  - (b) `emit` / `on` keywords, in-process default
  - (c) Provider-based (Redis pub/sub, Kafka)
  - (d) All three composable
- **Decision:** **(d) All three composable — channels (18.x) as the primitive, `@std/events` provider for ergonomic in-process events, third-party providers for distributed delivery**
- **Rationale:** No events (a) misses a major pub/sub pattern that's universal in production systems. Core-keyword in-process only (b) can't scale to distributed event delivery (Kafka, NATS, Redis streams). Provider-only (c) misses the ergonomic win of a built-in pub/sub surface. All three composable (d) is Avra's coherent answer: channels are the language primitive, in-process events are a `@std/events` provider that wraps channels with pub/sub semantics, and distributed event providers implement the same surface — so switching from in-process to Kafka is changing the provider, not rewriting the code.
- **Three layers:**
  - **Layer 1 — Channels (language primitive, 18.x):** raw fiber-to-fiber communication
  - **Layer 2 — `@std/events` (in-process pub/sub):** `emit` and `on` against a typed event bus; multiple subscribers per event type; no network involved
  - **Layer 3 — Distributed event providers:** `@community/kafka`, `@community/nats`, `@community/redis_streams` — same `emit`/`on` surface, backed by the distributed system
- **Syntax (`@std/events`):**
  ```
  use @std/events.{emit, on, EventBus}
  
  // Declare event types (any type can be an event)
  type UserRegistered = { user_id: UUID, email: string, at: Timestamp }
  type OrderPlaced = { order_id: UUID, total: float, user_id: UUID }
  
  // Emit (from anywhere)
  emit(UserRegistered { user_id, email, at: Time.now() })
  
  // Subscribe (usually in service initialization)
  on<UserRegistered>((event) -> {
    send_welcome_email(event.email)
    log("user registered: {event.user_id}")
  })
  
  on<OrderPlaced>((event) -> {
    update_inventory(event.order_id)
    notify_fulfillment(event.order_id)
  })
  ```
- **Switching to distributed:**
  ```
  use @std/events.{emit, on}
  use @community/kafka.{KafkaEventBus}
  
  // Initialize the distributed event bus
  let bus = KafkaEventBus.connect("kafka.internal:9092")
  EventBus.set_default(bus)
  
  // Same emit/on code works — events now flow through Kafka
  emit(UserRegistered { ... })     // published to Kafka topic
  on<UserRegistered>(...)           // consumes from Kafka
  ```
- **Event typing:** events are nominal types (or shapes). Subscribers match by type — `on<UserRegistered>` only receives `UserRegistered` events. No string-keyed channel names; the type IS the channel.
- **Multiple subscribers:** all subscribers of an event type receive each published event (fan-out). For exclusive consumption, use a single subscriber or a channel pattern instead.
- **Connection to topology extraction (19.3):** `emit` and `on` calls appear in the extracted topology — the deploy system knows which services publish and consume which events, useful for visualization and dependency tracking.
- **Structured concurrency (18.7):** subscriber fibers are scoped to their spawn context. Closing a scope cancels its subscribers cleanly.
- **Versioning:** `@std/events` ships in v1.0 with in-process delivery. Distributed providers ship as the community grows; interface stabilizes early so providers don't churn.

**Sub-decision 29.2: Event delivery guarantees**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Fire-and-forget
  - (b) At-least-once via backend
  - (c) Configurable per event
- **Decision:** **(c) Configurable per event type, with defaults that match the backend's natural guarantees**
- **Rationale:** Delivery guarantees are not one-size-fits-all. Audit events demand at-least-once-durable (losing one event = compliance failure). UI notification events prefer at-most-once-coalesced (delivering a stale notification twice is annoying; missing one is fine because the UI re-renders anyway). Forcing either extreme (a) or (b) makes one class of users unhappy. Configurable per event (c) matches reality — each event type declares its requirements, backends enforce what they can, events whose demands exceed the backend's capabilities fail at deploy time with clear errors.
- **Delivery-guarantee vocabulary:**
  - **`at_most_once`** — events may be dropped under load; never duplicated. Good for UI updates, soft telemetry.
  - **`at_least_once`** — events are retried until acknowledged; may duplicate. Good for state-mutation events, audit logs.
  - **`exactly_once`** — events are delivered exactly once, requires idempotent consumers or transactional delivery. Good for financial events.
  - **`ordered`** — events delivered in publish order (within a partition key). Composable with above; `at_least_once_ordered` is common for state replication.
  - **`durable`** — events survive restart of the bus. Often implied by at-least-once on a persistent backend.
- **Declaration on event types:**
  ```
  @event(delivery: at_least_once, durable: true)
  type OrderPlaced = { order_id: UUID, total: float, user_id: UUID }
  
  @event(delivery: at_most_once)
  type UIBadgeUpdate = { user_id: UUID, new_count: int }
  
  @event(delivery: exactly_once, ordered_by: account_id)
  type AccountBalanceChange = { account_id: UUID, delta: Money, timestamp: Timestamp }
  ```
- **Deploy-time verification:** the deploy system checks that the selected event provider supports the guarantees declared on every event type. If a user selects `@community/redis_streams` (offers at-least-once) but declares an event requiring `exactly_once`, the deploy fails with a clear error: "Event `AccountBalanceChange` requires `exactly_once` delivery, but provider `redis_streams` supports only `at_least_once`. Options: use a different provider, or downgrade the guarantee."
- **In-process default:** `@std/events` (in-process) provides at-most-once synchronous delivery by default — subscribers run inline on emit, exceptions propagate to emitters. Overridable per-subscription for async/buffered/retried delivery.
- **Retry policy:** at-least-once delivery uses exponential backoff with jitter, configurable globally via deploy config. Max retries configurable per event type.
- **Dead-letter handling:** events that exceed retry budget land in a dead-letter queue (backend-dependent). Observable via `@std/events.dead_letters<EventType>()`. Monitored as part of observability (19.5).
- **Versioning:** in-process delivery with at-most-once default ships in v1.0. Configurable guarantees ship with `@std/events`' v1.0 surface but distributed backends evolve.

**Sub-decision 29.3: Reactive computations outside UI**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not supported
  - (b) `computed` / `watch` as language constructs
  - (c) Only inside view blocks
- **Decision:** **(b) `computed` / `watch` / `signal` as library primitives in `@std/reactive` — not language keywords, usable anywhere**
- **Rationale:** Not supported (a) excludes a widely-useful pattern (caches that invalidate, derived dashboards, config propagation, monitored state). UI-only (c) artificially restricts reactivity to one context — the UI deferred to Axis 23 — even though many backends benefit (live metrics, computed aggregates, cache invalidation). Library primitives (b) are the right fit: `Signal<T>`, `Computed<T>`, and `Watch` as normal library types usable anywhere, with `@std/reactive` as the curated provider. UI when it lands can build on this same substrate rather than a separate mechanism.
- **Primitives:**
  ```
  use @std/reactive.{Signal, Computed, watch}
  
  // Signal: a reactive wrapper around a value
  let count: Signal<int> = Signal.new(0)
  count.set(5)
  println(count.get())                  // 5
  
  // Computed: a derived value that re-evaluates when its dependencies change
  let doubled: Computed<int> = Computed.new(() -> count.get() * 2)
  println(doubled.get())                // 10
  count.set(7)
  println(doubled.get())                // 14 (automatically recomputed)
  
  // Watch: side effects that run when dependencies change
  watch(() -> {
    println("count changed to {count.get()}")
  })
  count.set(10)                         // prints "count changed to 10"
  ```
- **Dependency tracking:** the runtime tracks which signals are read during `Computed.new` and `watch` callbacks. When any dependency changes, the computed or watch is marked dirty and re-evaluated (for computed, lazily on next `.get()`; for watch, eagerly after the current transaction).
- **Transactions:** multiple signal updates can be batched:
  ```
  Reactive.transaction(() -> {
    count.set(10)
    name.set("Alice")
    // Dependents see both changes together; watchers fire once, not twice
  })
  ```
- **Non-UI use cases:**
  - **Live metrics dashboards** — `Computed<AggregatedStats>` that recomputes when underlying counters change
  - **Cache invalidation** — `watch(() -> invalidate_cache())` on dependency signals
  - **Config hot-reload** — signals wrap config; services subscribe via `watch`; config file change triggers updates
  - **LLM agent state** — agents observe reactive state for coordination
  - **Live dependency graphs** — signals for derived values in distributed systems
- **UI integration (when Axis 23 lands):** `view` blocks automatically treat `mut` variables as signals (user memory: "`mut` inside `view` blocks becomes reactive"). The view's render function is a `Computed<ViewTree>`. This is how the three-tier UI will be implemented — no separate UI-only reactivity mechanism; it's the same `@std/reactive` library with view-block sugar.
- **Performance:** dependency tracking has small fixed overhead (a thread-local stack during reactive evaluation). For non-reactive code, cost is zero — you only pay for signals you actually use.
- **Why library rather than language primitive:** reactive systems have design tradeoffs (push vs pull, eager vs lazy, batching semantics) that are hard to unify into one language-level mechanism. A library can evolve as patterns are proven; `@std/reactive` is curated to provide the default, but alternate reactive providers (e.g., `@community/rxjs`-style streams) can coexist.
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0 as `@std/reactive`. UI integration lands with Axis 23's dedicated design.

---

### Axis 30: Annotations and Metadata

**Sub-decision 30.1: Annotation syntax**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `@name(args)` (Java-style)
  - (b) `#[name(args)]` (Rust-style)
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(a) `@name(args)` — Java/Python/Swift-style annotations**
- **Rationale:** Rust's `#[...]` (b) is visually noisy and the `#` character is culturally associated with comments in many languages (Python, shell) — using it for annotations creates a small but persistent reading dissonance. `@`-prefixed annotations (a) are already the dominant style in Java, Python (decorators), Swift, TypeScript, Kotlin, and many DSLs. Widely familiar, visually compact, and what the questionnaire has been using throughout. Both (c) is unnecessary duplication.
- **Syntax:**
  ```
  @deprecated("use `new_api` instead")
  fn old_api() { ... }
  
  @inline
  @pure
  fn abs(x: int) -> int { if x < 0 { -x } else { x } }
  
  @derive(Show, Eq, Hash)
  type User = { name: string, age: int }
  
  @requires(divisor != 0)
  @ensures(it * divisor == dividend)
  fn divide(dividend: int, divisor: int) -> int { dividend / divisor }
  
  @takes_ownership(s)
  extern fn consume_string(s: CString)
  
  @test_only
  fn mock_user() -> User { ... }
  ```
- **Forms:**
  - `@name` — no arguments
  - `@name(arg)` — single positional argument
  - `@name(arg1, arg2, key: value)` — multiple args, can be positional or keyword
  - Multiple annotations stack on one declaration; applied in declaration order (outer-first)
- **Placement:**
  - On declarations: `fn`, `type`, `shape`, `trait`, `enum`, `const`, `model`, `service`, etc.
  - On struct fields and enum variants: inline annotations
  - On expressions (rarely): for `@inline` call sites, `@unchecked` casts — limited, documented cases
  - Module-level: at the top of a file before declarations
- **`@` conflicts:** `@` is also used as the prefix for package namespaces (`@std/...`, `@myorg/...`). These are syntactically distinguishable because annotations are followed by an identifier directly, package names are followed by `/`. Parser handles the disambiguation cleanly.
- **Versioning:** locked from v1.0. `@` is the canonical annotation prefix.

**Sub-decision 30.2: User-defined annotations**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not supported — compiler-only
  - (b) Via providers
  - (c) In language directly
- **Decision:** **(b) User-defined annotations via the provider system (17.x)**
- **Rationale:** Compiler-only (a) is too restrictive — annotations are one of the most useful extension points for tooling, framework authors, and custom ergonomics. Users will want to write their own (`@serialize`, `@cacheable`, `@audited`, `@route`, etc.). Language-level custom annotations (c) would require exposing compiler internals in a fragile way. Provider-based (b) is the right architecture: annotations ARE provider registrations. The provider system (17.x) already supports registering compile-time behavior; annotations are one of those surfaces.
- **How users define annotations:**
  ```
  // In a provider package: @myorg/avra-annotations
  use @compiler.{Annotation, DeclarationInfo, AvraCode}
  
  @provider_register(Annotation)
  export fn register_audited() -> Annotation {
    Annotation {
      name: "audited",
      targets: [fn, method],                   // where it can be applied
      args: { actor: string, resource: string },
      effect: Effect.transform_body,            // modifies compiled output
      transform: transform_audited_body,
    }
  }
  
  fn transform_audited_body(decl: DeclarationInfo, args: Args) -> AvraCode {
    // Generate wrapping code that logs the audit event before calling the original body
    ...
  }
  ```
- **Usage (once the provider is imported):**
  ```
  use @myorg/avra-annotations       // registers `@audited` with the compiler
  
  @audited(actor: "admin", resource: "user")
  fn delete_user(user_id: UUID) -> Result<(), Error> {
    Users.delete(user_id)
  }
  ```
- **Capabilities annotations can have:**
  - **Metadata-only** — visible to tooling, no code effect (e.g., `@deprecated`, `@stability(experimental)`, `@author("Alice")`)
  - **Codegen transformation** — wraps/modifies the declaration (e.g., `@audited`, `@cached`, `@traced`)
  - **Validation** — compile-time checks (e.g., `@requires`, `@ensures` from 14.2)
  - **Derive** — generate additional code alongside (e.g., `@derive(Show, Eq)`, from cross-cutting decisions)
- **Collision handling:** annotation names are scoped by the provider that registered them. `@myorg/audited` and `@otherorg/audited` can coexist; the compiler resolves based on which provider is imported. The short form `@audited` works when unambiguous.
- **Reserved namespaces:**
  - Built-in compiler annotations (`@inline`, `@pure`, `@deprecated`, etc.) are reserved
  - `@std/*` annotations curated by Avra maintainers
  - User-defined annotations use their provider namespace
- **Versioning:** provider system + built-in annotations ship in v1.0. User-defined annotation mechanism ships in v1.x as the provider extension API stabilizes.

**Sub-decision 30.3: Annotation effects on codegen**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Pure metadata — no compilation effect
  - (b) Can modify codegen (Rust derive)
  - (c) Both — declared per annotation
- **Decision:** **(c) Each annotation declares its effect explicitly when registered**
- **Rationale:** Metadata-only (a) forecloses deriving, wrapping, code transformation — too restrictive. Always codegen-affecting (b) means every annotation is a macro, which is heavy and makes compilation performance unpredictable. Per-annotation declaration (c) is the right answer: each annotation registers its effect level at definition time, so users can see at a glance whether an annotation is pure metadata, modifies codegen, or performs validation. The compiler enforces the declared effect — a metadata-only annotation cannot sneak in codegen changes.
- **Effect categories:**
  - **`Effect.metadata`** — pure metadata, no compilation effect. Readable via introspection/reflection for tools, LSP, docs. Examples: `@deprecated`, `@stability`, `@author`, `@since`.
  - **`Effect.validate`** — runs a compile-time check; emits diagnostics if check fails. Does NOT modify generated code. Examples: `@requires`, `@ensures` (14.2), `@total` (14.4).
  - **`Effect.transform_body`** — wraps or modifies the body of the annotated declaration. Example: `@audited` wraps body with audit logging; `@cached` wraps with memoization; `@traced` wraps with span emission.
  - **`Effect.derive`** — adds additional declarations alongside the annotated one. Example: `@derive(Show, Eq)` generates impl blocks; `@derive(Model)` generates migrations, REST, UI, etc.
  - **`Effect.codegen_attribute`** — passes hints to codegen without transforming source-level code. Example: `@inline`, `@no_mangle`, `@link_section("...")`.
- **Visible effects:**
  - `avra explain annotation @audited` lists what `@audited` does: its effect category, what it validates, what it transforms, what it generates
  - `avra explain annotations <declaration>` lists all annotations on a declaration and what each will do at compile time
  - Agents can query this via MCP for full transparency
- **Conservative defaults:** if an annotation is declared without a stated effect, the compiler assumes `Effect.metadata` and rejects any attempt to transform. Opting into transformation is explicit.
- **Debugging annotation transformations:** `avra build --show-expansion <file>` prints the post-annotation source code — the code that actually compiles after all `@derive` and `@transform_body` annotations have run. Essential for debugging when annotations produce unexpected output.
- **Performance:** annotations with non-trivial transforms are executed by the provider's compile-time code. The query framework (26.1) caches transformation results, so repeated builds don't re-run transforms unnecessarily.
- **Versioning:** effect-declaring annotation system ships in v1.0 with built-in annotations. User-defined annotations with effects ship in v1.x.

---

### Axis 31: Numerical Types

**Sub-decision 31.1: Integer sizes**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `int` (64-bit) only
  - (b) Full set: `i8`, `i16`, `i32`, `i64`, `u8`..`u64`
  - (c) `int` at app level, explicit sizes at systems level
- **Decision:** **(c) `int` (pointer-sized) at app level, explicit sizes at systems level**
- **Rationale:** Single-size only (a) is friendly but insufficient for systems work — you can't express a protocol that specifies u8 or u32 fields without explicit sizes. Full-set always-available (b) forces app-level code to make pointless size decisions ("should this user count be an i32 or i64?").
