The scoped answer (c) matches Avra's overall design philosophy: app-level is ergonomic and size-inferred, systems-level opts into explicit sizes when layout matters.
- **App level:**
  - **`int`** — signed, pointer-sized (i64 on 64-bit platforms, i32 on 32-bit). The default integer type for all app-level code.
  - **`uint`** — unsigned, pointer-sized. For indices, sizes, counts that logically can't be negative.
  - That's it. No `i8`, no `u32`, no size soup.
- **Systems level (inside `systems { }` blocks):**
  - Signed: `i8`, `i16`, `i32`, `i64`, `i128`, `isize` (pointer-sized, alias for `int`)
  - Unsigned: `u8`, `u16`, `u32`, `u64`, `u128`, `usize` (pointer-sized, alias for `uint`)
  - Used for explicit layouts, binary protocols, FFI, bitwise manipulation
- **Examples:**
  ```
  // App level — clean
  fn count_users(users: List<User>) -> int {
    users.filter(is_active).length
  }
  
  let age: int = 42
  let page_number: uint = 5
  
  // Systems level — explicit
  systems {
    type TcpHeader = {
      src_port: u16
      dst_port: u16
      seq_num: u32
      ack_num: u32
      flags: u8
      window: u16
    }
  }
  ```
- **No silent narrowing:** conversions between int sizes are always explicit. `let small: i32 = big_int.to_i32()?` — returns a Result because the conversion can overflow.
- **Default literal type:** integer literals without context default to `int`. Literals in systems-level context infer the surrounding type.
- **Versioning:** `int` and `uint` ship in v1.0. Systems-level explicit sizes reserved in v1.0, ship with systems-level semantics (9.x).

**Sub-decision 31.2: Float sizes**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `float` (64-bit) only
  - (b) `f32`, `f64`
  - (c) Both `float` and sized variants
- **Decision:** **(c) `float` (64-bit IEEE 754) at app level, `f32` and `f64` at systems level**
- **Rationale:** Mirrors 31.1 for consistency. App-level code uses `float` (f64) which gives more precision at trivial cost. Systems-level needs explicit `f32`/`f64` for binary protocols, GPU interop, audio DSP, graphics where bit layout and SIMD packing matter.
- **App level:**
  - **`float`** — 64-bit IEEE 754 double-precision
  - No `double` alias (`float` IS double at app level)
- **Systems level:**
  - **`f32`** — 32-bit single-precision
  - **`f64`** — 64-bit double-precision (same as app-level `float`)
  - **`f16`** (reserved) — half-precision; ships when SIMD/GPU interop demands it
- **Special values:** IEEE 754 semantics — `NaN`, `Infinity`, `-Infinity` are valid values. Available as `Float.nan`, `Float.infinity`, `Float.neg_infinity`. Comparisons with NaN follow IEEE rules (NaN != NaN).
- **No implicit int-float conversion:** `let x: float = 5` is a compile error. Write `let x: float = 5.0` or `let x: float = (5).to_float()`. This prevents silent loss of precision and matches strong static typing.
- **Float literals:** must have a decimal point or exponent: `5.0`, `1e10`, `2.5e-3`. `5` alone is an integer literal.
- **Division semantics:** `int / int = int` (truncating), `float / float = float`. Mixed arithmetic requires explicit conversion.
- **Versioning:** `float` ships in v1.0. `f32`/`f64` reserved in v1.0, ship with systems level.

**Sub-decision 31.3: Arbitrary precision**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Not included
  - (b) `BigInt` / `BigDecimal` in stdlib
  - (c) Built-in arbitrary precision with overflow promotion
- **Decision:** **(b) `BigInt` / `BigDecimal` in `@std/numbers` — explicit types, no silent promotion**
- **Rationale:** Not included (a) excludes a whole class of real applications (cryptography, accurate financial math, scientific computing). Overflow promotion (c) makes performance unpredictable — innocuous-looking int arithmetic silently heap-allocates if the result grows. Explicit library types (b) are the Rust/Java model: `BigInt` and `BigDecimal` are standard, discoverable, and clearly-costed. Programmers opt in when they need the precision.
- **Types:**
  - **`BigInt`** — arbitrary-precision signed integer; heap-allocated, grows as needed
  - **`BigDecimal`** — arbitrary-precision decimal with explicit scale; useful for money, scientific data, anything where `0.1 + 0.2 == 0.3` matters
  - **`Rational`** — exact fractions (numerator/denominator as BigInt); useful for symbolic math
- **Usage:**
  ```
  use @std/numbers.{BigInt, BigDecimal}
  
  // Cryptography
  let modulus = BigInt.from_hex("ffffffffffffffffc90fdaa22168c234...")
  let result = base.pow_mod(exponent, modulus)
  
  // Financial math
  let price = BigDecimal.from_str("19.99")?
  let tax = BigDecimal.from_str("0.0825")?
  let total = price * (BigDecimal.one() + tax)    // 21.63175 exact
  
  // From integer literals
  let n: BigInt = 1000.into()
  let factorial_20: BigInt = (1..=20.into()).product()
  ```
- **Money type:** `@std/money` provides `Money` built on `BigDecimal` with currency tagging — the idiomatic choice for financial apps. BigDecimal alone doesn't know about currencies.
- **No silent promotion:** `let x: int = huge_bigint` is a compile error. Conversion is always explicit and fallible: `let x: int = huge.to_int()?` returns a Result that's `Err(Overflow)` if the value doesn't fit.
- **Performance note:** every `BigInt` / `BigDecimal` operation allocates. Use explicitly when the precision matters; stick with `int`/`float` for hot paths. The cost is visible in the type.
- **Versioning:** `@std/numbers` ships in v1.0 with `BigInt` and `BigDecimal`. `Rational` may land in v1.x if demand exists.

**Sub-decision 31.4: Overflow behavior**

- **Options:**
  - (a) Wrap silently (C)
  - (b) Panic on overflow in debug, wrap in release (Rust)
  - (c) Always panic / return Result
- **Decision:** **(b) Panic on overflow in debug, wrap in release by default; explicit `checked_*`, `saturating_*`, `wrapping_*` methods for programmer control**
- **Rationale:** Silent wrap always (a) is a classic C bug farm — integer overflow is undefined-behavior-adjacent and a major source of security vulnerabilities. Always panic/Result (c) adds cost and ceremony to every `+` operator, which is too intrusive. The debug/release split (b) is Rust's pragmatic answer and matches how most real-world code wants to behave: debug catches bugs during development; release gives full-speed arithmetic. When correctness trumps performance, explicit methods make the intent clear.
- **Default operators (`+`, `-`, `*`, `/`, `<<`, `>>`):**
  - **Debug builds:** panic on overflow with a clear diagnostic ("integer overflow: i32 + i32 overflowed at foo.av:42")
  - **Release builds:** wrap using two's-complement arithmetic (the CPU's native behavior)
  - **Configurable:** `[build] overflow_checks = "always" | "debug_only" | "never"` in `forge.toml` per project
- **Explicit arithmetic methods:**
  ```
  // Checked — returns Result, never panics or wraps
  let result: Result<int, Overflow> = a.checked_add(b)
  let x = a.checked_mul(b)?    // propagates overflow error
  
  // Saturating — clamps to min/max on overflow
  let sum = a.saturating_add(b)         // max_int if overflow
  let diff = a.saturating_sub(b)        // min_int if underflow
  
  // Wrapping — always wraps (explicit opt-in to C semantics)
  let hashed = a.wrapping_mul(0x9E3779B97F4A7C15)    // common in hash functions
  
  // Overflowing — returns (result, did_overflow)
  let (value, overflowed) = a.overflowing_add(b)
  ```
- **Why the default matters:** the common case should be safe. A function that naively does `count + 1` in debug catches overflow bugs; in release, the overflow wraps — matching what C does and what most developers assume. Explicit methods let performance-sensitive code opt into documented behavior.
- **Division-by-zero:** always panics (in both debug and release). Use `checked_div` to get a Result. Integer division by zero is never defined behavior — there's no sensible "wrap" for that.
- **Float overflow:** floats don't overflow in the integer sense — they become `Infinity` or `-Infinity`, which is valid. No panic, no check, follows IEEE 754.
- **Versioning:** ships in v1.0 with the default behavior. Additional methods (`carrying_*` for multi-precision arithmetic, etc.) can land incrementally.

**Sub-decision 31.5: Numeric literal syntax**

- **Options:**
  - (a) `42`, `42.0`, `0x2A`, `0b101010`, `42_000`
  - (b) Type-suffixed literals: `42u8`, `3.14f32`
  - (c) Both
- **Decision:** **(c) Both — full syntax set for inferred-type literals AND type-suffixed literals**
- **Rationale:** Inferred literals (a) are the common case and should be clean. Type suffixes (b) are essential for FFI, generics where the context doesn't disambiguate, and systems-level code where the width matters. Supporting both is uncontroversial — every major language does it.
- **Full syntax:**
  ```
  // Decimal integers
  42
  42_000                    // underscores for readability; anywhere in the number
  1_000_000
  
  // Hex, octal, binary
  0x2A                      // hex
  0o52                      // octal
  0b101010                  // binary
  0xFF_FF_FF_FF             // underscores work in any base
  
  // Floats
  3.14
  2.5e-3
  1e10
  6.022_140_76e23
  
  // Type-suffixed (explicit type)
  42i32                     // i32 regardless of context
  200u8                     // u8 — compile error if > 255
  3.14f32                   // f32 single-precision literal
  
  // Type-suffixed with underscores
  1_000_000i64
  0xFF_FF_FFu32
  ```
- **Suffix rules:**
  - Integer suffixes: `i8`, `i16`, `i32`, `i64`, `i128`, `u8`, `u16`, `u32`, `u64`, `u128`, `uint`, `int`
  - Float suffixes: `f32`, `f64`, `float`
  - Out-of-range literals are compile errors: `256u8` → "literal 256 does not fit in u8 (max 255)"
- **Character and byte literals:**
  - `'a'` — character literal (type `char`, 32-bit Unicode scalar value)
  - `b'a'` — byte literal (type `u8`), value is the ASCII code
  - `'\n'`, `'\t'`, `'\\'`, `'\''`, `'\u{1F600}'` — escapes supported
- **No octal implicit:** unlike C, `077` is NOT octal 63 — it's the decimal literal 77. Octal requires explicit `0o77` prefix. This avoids the classic C footgun where leading zeros silently change base.
- **Versioning:** all literal forms ship in v1.0. Systems-level size suffixes (i8, u16, etc.) reserved in v1.0, active with systems-level.

## Open Questions Carried Forward

**Status: all resolved.** The questions accumulated here during early deep-dive sessions before the 31-axis questionnaire was built. Each has since been answered by a specific sub-decision; the list is retained as an audit trail.

| Original open question | Resolved by |
|---|---|
| dyn Trait design | 6.2 — `dyn` chosen for runtime polymorphism |
| Scoped-levels/memory-notes contradiction on lifetime annotations | 11.3 phased rollout + 9.x memory summary |
| `.collect_results()` implementation for List | TRD-level library detail, not a language decision |
| `@pure` annotation spec | 13.3 — opt-in compiler-verified; detailed spec in TRD |
| Provider capability enforcement (WASM sandbox) | 13.2 + 17.5 — capability declarations + WASM |
| `@non_exhaustive` for provider enums | 8.11 — smart non-exhaustive defaults |
| Recursive enum auto-boxing codegen | 8.10 — auto-box recursive at app level |
| Union error type system spec | 12.3 — `Result<T, E1 \| E2>` with auto-widening |
| FFI error mapping annotations | 12.12 + 15.7 — `@error_if` spec |
| Closure capture rules for `mut` bindings | 9.13 — inferred capture mode (ref for mutation, move on escape) |
| Closures crossing level boundaries semantics | 9.14 — captures convert at boundary |
| Systems-level borrow checker scope rules | 11.3 — phased rollout, 9.x memory summary |
| Auto-derive strategy for shape vs type | 7.5 — `@derive` ladder |
| Inline enum representation (string literals vs tags) | 8.5 — declared enums only, no inline |
| Empty collection literal inference rule | 4.4 — annotation required when context doesn't constrain |
| Copy trait definition | 9.20 — auto-derived for Copy-only fields |

**Remaining ambiguity for TRD review:**
- Fine-grained semantics at the edges of closure capture + `mut` + cross-fiber (9.13 ↔ 11.4 ↔ 9.22 interaction) — the rules are correct but the prose could be tightened during TRD authoring.
- `.collect_results()` and similar stdlib ergonomic helpers — library design, not language design.
- The exact grammar of `@pure` transitivity across providers — spec-level detail for when `@pure` is formally introduced.

## Change Log

Track decisions as they're made.

| Date | Axis | Decision | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| Apr 16 | 1.1 Typing discipline | Fully static | No `any`, no gradual typing. LLM feedback loops depend on compile errors. |
| Apr 16 | 1.2 Runtime types | Erased binary, introspectable compiler | Reflection replaced by compile-time code generation via providers and `@derive`. |
| Apr 16 | 1.3 Compile-time eval | Unified lifted code | No macros, no separate comptime. `@lifted` = compile-time scheduled execution. Providers are lifted modules. Paradox collapsed. |
| Apr 16 | 2.1 Soundness | Mostly sound, scoped to levels | App/systems are sound; bare is unsound by design. Soundness is a boundary property. |
| Apr 16 | 2.2 Escape hatches | Per-level only | No `as`, no `unsafe`, no narrow annotations. `bare { }` is the only escape. Narrowing via `is`, conversion via trait methods, reinterpretation via `@transmute` in bare. |
| Apr 16 | 2.3 Runtime type errors | None in sound levels; panics contained at task boundary | No `as!`. Panics are structured events, kill tasks not programs. Forward-compatible with richer supervision. |
| Apr 16 | 3.1 Nominal vs structural | Both, with explicit split | Data structural, behavior/identity nominal. Paradox collapse. |
| Apr 16 | 3.2 Keywords | `type` nominal, `shape` structural | Two keywords, syntactically visible. `shape` is the natural English word. |
| Apr 16 | 3.3 Record typing | Both via keyword | Default `shape` for data, `type` for identity. LLM guidance in CLAUDE.md. |
| Apr 16 | 3.4 Traits | Nominal only | Explicit impl blocks. Shared namespace with shape/type. Boilerplate solved by `@derive`. |
| Apr 16 | Cross-cutting | `@derive` is a lifted function | Not compiler magic — open, extensible, user-authorable. The mechanism behind P12 one-source-many-projections. |
| Apr 16 | 3.5 Width subtyping | Allowed on shapes | Wider shapes substitute for narrower. Core value prop of structural typing. |
| Apr 16 | 3.6 Depth subtyping | Allowed on immutable fields only | Mutable fields invariant. Auto-inferred from `mut` — no variance annotations needed. |
| Apr 16 | 3.7 Private fields on shape | Disallowed | `shape` = public data, `type` = nominal identity with privacy. Clean split. |
| Apr 16 | 3.8 Newtype | `type UserId = UUID` creates nominal wrapper | Zero ceremony, zero runtime cost, erased distinction. |
| Apr 16 | 4.1 Inference scope | Local + bidirectional | Function signatures explicit, bodies infer, expected types flow down. |
| Apr 16 | 4.2 Fn signature annotations | Required (params + return) | Signatures are boundary contracts. Void returns omit `-> Type`. |
| Apr 16 | 4.3 Closure annotations | Inferred from context | Single and multi-param both infer. `it` pronoun for single-arg sugar. |
| Apr 16 | 4.4 Empty literal inference | Error with actionable message | No silent defaults. Bidirectional handles most cases; error only at genuinely ambiguous literals. |
| Apr 16 | 4.5 Method calls on generics | Require provable resolution | Shape bounds give fields, trait bounds give methods, combine with `&`. |
| Apr 16 | 5.1 Generic mechanism | Both parametric + traits + shape bounds | Three constraint mechanisms; combine with `&`. |
| Apr 16 | 5.2 Mono vs dyn | Both — mono default, `dyn` opt-in | Mono for perf, `dyn` for heterogeneity. Phase 3 provider system requires `dyn`. |
| Apr 16 | 5.3 Constraint expression | Both inline and named bounds | Resolved as part of 5.1. |
| Apr 16 | 5.4 Where clauses | Both inline and `where` | Inline for simple (1-2 bounds), `where` for complex or associated types. |
| Apr 16 | 5.5 Const generics | Integers only, deferred to v1.x | Fixed arrays, matrices, bit fields. Systems/bare level feature. Reserve syntax now. |
| Apr 16 | 6.1 Variance annotations | Inferred, no user syntax | Compiler derives from immutability + position. Scala-style annotations avoided. |
| Apr 16 | 6.2 Container variance | Covariant immutable, invariant mut | Direct consequence of immutability-by-default and 3.6. |
| Apr 16 | 6.3 Function variance | Contra args, co return | Liskov-correct. Inferred. Never written by users. |
| Apr 16 | 7.1 HKT support | Associated types only | Full HKTs fail LLM-first test; incompatible with structural typing. |
| Apr 16 | 7.2 Associated types scope | Any trait | General feature. "Impl picks the type" pattern. |
| Apr 16 | 8.1 Sum types | Both enums and unions | Enums for domain, unions for ad-hoc. |
| Apr 16 | 8.2 Enum variant syntax | Contextually inferred | `.circle(r)` when type known, `Shape.circle(r)` when ambiguous. |
| Apr 16 | 8.3 Variant fields | Both named and positional | Wrappers positional, multi-field named. |
| Apr 16 | 8.4 Union exhaustiveness | Discriminated when members distinguishable | TS-style discriminated unions, sound matching. |
| Apr 16 | 8.5 Structural discrimination | Auto for non-overlapping, explicit required otherwise | `kind` field is the style guidance. |
| Apr 16 | 8.6 Inline enums | Removed | Declared enums only. One mechanism, nominal, refactor-safe. |
| Apr 16 | 8.7 Wildcards | Allowed with lint warning | Warns when hiding 3+ variants, `@allow_wildcard` to silence. |
| Apr 16 | 8.8 Non-exhaustive | Smart defaults by source | Provider exports non-exhaustive, user enums exhaustive. |
| Apr 16 | 8.9 Recursive enum memory | Auto-box at app, explicit at systems | No `Box` ceremony at app level. |
| Apr 16 | 9.1 Primary memory strategy | Scoped per abstraction level | Signature Avra feature. P6 applied to memory. |
| Apr 16 | 9.2 Number of levels | Four (app/systems/bare/hardware) | Each has distinct semantics; collapsing any pair loses compiler help. |
| Apr 16 | 9.3 Level transition mechanics | All three (block, function, module) | Each scope granularity solves a different use case. |
| Apr 16 | 9.4 RC atomicity | Non-atomic v1.0, evolve to inferred in v1.x | Single-threaded green-threading v1.0. Honest staging over research-grade inference from day one. |
| Apr 16 | Axis 9 architecture | 10 architectural commitments for v1.0 | Insurance against painting into a corner: traversal protocols, external headers, strategy-dispatched ops, static-linked runtime, reserved keywords. Preserves systems/bare/hardware evolution path. |
| Apr 16 | Axis 9 versioning | v1.0 = app-level only; systems v1.x; bare/hw v2.0 | Lexer reserves keywords in v1.0; code using them errors with "reserved for future use". |
| Apr 16 | 9.5 Cycle detection | Targeted mark-and-sweep with drop-site-precise scheduling | Zero cost for non-cycle-capable types and drops that reach zero. |
| Apr 16 | 9.6 Arena allocation | Compiler-detected + provider-declarable + explicit annotation | Generalized beyond HTTP; any handler-like callback shape can participate. |
| Apr 16 | 9.7 RC elision | Basic v1.0, aggressive Perceus-style v1.x | Last-use move, non-aliased locals skip, pair elimination. |
| Apr 16 | 9.8 Borrow checker | Inferred lifetimes with phased rollout | v1.x phase 1: immutable borrows only. v1.x phase 2: NLL mutable. v2.0: full Rust-equivalent. |
| Apr 16 | 9.9 Stack vs heap | Escape analysis with primitive baseline | LLVM-native, free win. Stack unless it escapes. |
| Apr 16 | 9.10 Drop order | Reverse declaration (LIFO) | Standard; enables RAII. |
| Apr 16 | 9.11 defer vs Drop | Interleaved by declaration order | Both coexist; unified LIFO at scope exit. |
| Apr 16 | 9.12 Copy trait | Auto-derived for Copy-only fields | Primitives + tuples + structs with Copy fields. `@no_copy` to opt out. |
| Apr 16 | 9.13 Closure captures | Inferred from usage | By ref for read, by mut-ref for mutate, by value if escapes. `move` annotation as escape. |
| Apr 16 | Cross-cutting syntax | Closures use `->`, not `=>` or `\|\|` | One arrow consistent with fn signatures. Replaces Rust-style pipes. |
| Apr 16 | 9.14 Level-crossing closures | v1.x+ feature: captures convert at boundary | Spawn cannot pass borrows. Bare escapes forbidden. v1.0 = app-only, no boundary. |
| Apr 16 | 9.15 String rep | SSO (≤23 bytes inline) + UTF-8 | Interning deferred to v1.x. Byte-indexed by default. |
| Apr 16 | 9.16 `with` expression | Structural sharing v1.0; persistent collections + chain fusion v1.x | Field-count retains, no deep copy. |
| Apr 16 | 10.1 Null rep | `T?` as sugar for `Option<T>` | No null keyword; `.some(v)` and `.none` are the variants. |
| Apr 16 | 10.2 Non-null default | Types non-null, `?` opts in | Eliminates billion-dollar mistake. |
| Apr 16 | 10.3 Null operators | Both operators (`?.`, `??`, `!`) AND pattern matching | Concise for common, pattern for exhaustive. |
| Apr 16 | 10.4 Narrowing | Flow-sensitive | `if x != .none` narrows `T?` to `T` in branch. TS/Kotlin-proven. |
| Apr 16 | 10.5 `?` operator | Works for `T?` propagation | Also for Result (axis 12). Unified propagation. |
| Apr 16 | 11.1 Default mutability | Immutable by default | `mut` opts in. Foundational to LLM-correctness. |
| Apr 16 | 11.2 Compile-time const | Separate `const` keyword | `const` for compile-time, `let` for runtime. |
| Apr 16 | 11.3 Interior mutability | Via explicit wrapper types | `Cell`, `Lazy`, `OnceCell` in v1.0; `Mutex` in v1.x. |
| Apr 16 | 11.4 Aliasing rules | Level-dependent | v1.0 app = shared (single-threaded); v1.x systems = exclusive; v1.x+ app multi-threaded = exclusive via channels/Mutex. |
| Apr 16 | 11.5 Immutability depth | Deep — whole tree immutable | Matches programmer intent; enables safe sharing. |
| Apr 16 | 12.1 Error mechanism | Result types | Values not exceptions. Composes with Option `?`. |
| Apr 16 | 12.2 Propagation | `?` operator unified with Option | Compiler picks behavior from expression type. |
| Apr 16 | 12.3 Error unification | Union error types with automatic widening | GENUINELY NOVEL: no From boilerplate, no erasure. `Result<T, IoError \| ParseError>` first-class. |
| Apr 16 | 12.4 Error identity | Nominal with Error trait | Needed for discriminated error unions to work. |
| Apr 16 | 12.5 Inline handling | `catch { }` block | Scoped handler for common "try with default" pattern. |
| Apr 16 | 12.6 catch binding | Both forms | `catch { }` no bind, `catch (e) { }` explicit. |
| Apr 16 | 12.7 Scope cleanup | All three: Drop + defer + errdefer | LIFO ordering. errdefer runs only on error. |
| Apr 16 | 12.8 Panic | Catch at spawn boundary only | Task panic → parent receives TaskPanic error. No exception-style catch-anywhere. |
| Apr 16 | 12.9 `?` in closures | Returns from closure (local scope) | Matches lexical reasoning. Not enclosing function. |
| Apr 16 | 12.10 Error trait | World-class: message + kind + cause + context + trace + suggestions + format | Three novel features: auto context accumulation via `?`, hierarchical `kind` strings, structured context queryable by LLMs. |
| Apr 16 | 12.11 Retry/timeout | Stdlib `@std/core` functions | Leverage `is_transient()` from Error trait. |
| Apr 16 | 12.12 FFI error mapping | Annotation-based `@error_if` | Reserved in v1.0, ships with FFI in v2.0. Critical for wrapping large C APIs. |
